<meta>This page follows the formal dedication to the Maharaja of Bhinga. Having established the patronage of the work, the author now begins the introduction by discussing the supreme historical and spiritual authority of the Vedas, the foundational scriptures of India, drawing upon the testimony of Western orientalist Max Müller to bolster his claims.</meta>

# The Veda

There is no <gloss>historical</gloss> text in the entire world more ancient or authentic than the Veda; for the welfare of humanity, there is no greater book in any language or religious sect. Regarding this subject, a well-known foreign German scholar of a different faith, *Professor Max Müller* <note>The text uses the honorific "Bhatt," a title usually reserved for learned Brahmins, showing the high esteem in which the author holds Müller.</note>, writes that by examining the thoughts, language, intent, style of composition, and grammatical uniqueness of the Vedic Samhita <note>A 'Samhita' refers to the most ancient layer of the Vedas, consisting of hymns, prayers, and mantras.</note>, it is clearly evident that there is no book equal to the Vedic Samhita in the Sanskrit language—nor in the language of any other race or country in the world. This "Rigveda" Samhita is the most ancient work of extremely old Sanskrit literature. It is the first book for the benefit of the human race, the sole primary evidence of human civilization, the oldest history of mankind, and the first guide to religious faith—therefore, it is worthy of respect by all of humanity. At a time for which no history of the human race can be found elsewhere—when the thoughts, religion, faith, civilization, methods of worship, establishment of deities, social customs, hopes, and heart-felt emotions of that era have vanished into the womb of the eternal flow of time, and when no other means exist to recover the history of those days—the history of that immemorial time is recorded in golden letters in a well-organized form within the Rigveda Samhita. It is for this reason that the Rigveda Samhita is held in such high honor and respect among the circles of scholars throughout the civilized world.

---

> The Veda has a two-fold interest: it belongs to the history of the world, and to the history of India. In the history of the world, the Veda fills a gap which no literary work in any other language could fill. It carries us back to times of which we have no records anywhere, and gives us the very words of a generation of men, of whom otherwise we could form but the vaguest estimate by means of conjectures and inferences. As long as man continues to take an interest in the history of his race, and as long as we collect in libraries and museums the relics of former ages, the first place in that long row of books which contains the records of the <term>Aryan</term> <note>In this 19th-century context, "Aryan" refers to the Indo-European linguistic and cultural ancestors of the Indian and European peoples.</note> branch of mankind, belongs forever to the <term>Rig Veda</term> <note>The oldest of the four Vedas, composed of over 1,000 hymns dedicated to the gods of the Vedic pantheon.</note>, the most ancient of books in the library of mankind, which is more ancient than the <term>Zend-avesta</term> <note>The primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism, written in Avestan.</note> and Homer (919–859 B.C.).

->Professor Max Müller's *History of Ancient Sanskrit Literature*, Page 63<-

<summary>This page introduces the Veda, specifically the Rigveda, as the most ancient and significant historical and religious document in human history. It utilizes the authority of German scholar Max Müller to argue that the Vedas represent the earliest records of human thought and civilization.</summary>
<keywords>Veda, Rigveda, Max Müller, Sanskrit Literature, Aryan, human history, ancient civilization, Samhita</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Vedic texts, moving from a general appreciation of their antiquity to a technical explanation of the "Vedangas" or the auxiliary sciences required to understand them. It specifically focuses on "Shiksha" (phonetics) and introduces "Kalpa" (ritual procedure), setting the stage for the study of the Gobhila Grihya Sutra.</meta>

-># The Gobhila Grihya Sutra—[A Discourse on the Kalpa Sutras]<-

## The Limbs of the Veda

While impartial scholars from Germany and other lands praise our Vedas, today we find ourselves devoid of that extraordinary Vedic knowledge. To understand the profound meaning of the Veda, the six <term>Vedangas</term> <note>Literally "limbs of the Veda"; these are six auxiliary disciplines used to understand and preserve the scriptures.</note>, beginning with **Phonetics**, have been established. In the *Mundakopanishad* of the *Atharvaveda*, these sciences—beginning with Phonetics—are called **Lower Knowledge** <note>original: "Apara Vidya"</note>. As the seekers of the Absolute state: "Knowledge is of two kinds: the Higher and the Lower. Of these, the Rigveda, Yajurveda, Samaveda, Atharvaveda, Phonetics, Ritual, Grammar, Etymology, Prosody, and Astronomy are all **Lower Knowledge**. That by which the Imperishable Absolute is realized is called **Higher Knowledge** <note>original: "Para Vidya"</note>."

The knowledge of <term>Dharma</term> <note>Righteousness, duty, or religious law.</note> is the means to attain the knowledge of the Absolute. Because it serves as this means, the section of the Veda dealing with rituals and the six auxiliary limbs is considered Lower Knowledge. Because the knowledge of the Absolute is the ultimate human goal, the <term>Upanishads</term> <note>The philosophical concluding portions of the Vedas.</note> are called **Higher Knowledge**.

The scripture that describes the methods of pronunciation—including letters, accents, and so on—is **Phonetics** <note>original: "Shiksha"</note>. Those who study the *Taittiriya* branch of the Veda state at the very beginning of their Upanishad: "We shall now explain Phonetics." The "letters" referred to are 'A' and the others that follow. This is clearly stated in the texts on Phonetics. Without the science of Phonetics, we cannot correctly chant the Vedic <term>Mantras</term> <note>Sacred hymns or chants.</note>. We shall now describe the second "limb," known as **Ritual**.

## The Kalpa Sutras

The manuals authored by sages such as **Apastamba**, **Baudhayana**, and **Ashvalayana** are known as the **Kalpa Sutras**. The procedures for sacrifices are "conceptualized" <note>original: "Kalpit"</note> or supported within these *Kalpa* texts; therefore, they are named *Kalpa*. 

A question may arise here: did masters like Ashvalayana compose these Ritual Sutras according to the <term>Samhita</term> <note>The primary collection of hymns.</note> or according to the <term>Brahmana</term> <note>The prose texts explaining the rituals.</note> portion of the Veda? If one says they followed the Samhita, the answer is inconsistent; this is because they begin their explanation with the **New and Full Moon sacrifices** <note>original: "Darshapurnamasa yaga"</note>. Had they followed the sequence of the Samhita, they would have first explained the sacrifice in which the very first mantra of the Rigveda—"I laud Agni" <note>original: "Agni mile"</note>—is required. However, the mantra "I laud Agni" is not used anywhere in the New and Full Moon sacrifices. If you suggest that the Ritual Sutras were composed according to the Brahmana portion, that too is improper. In the <term>Dikshaniya</term> <note>The initiatory offering of a Soma sacrifice.</note> sacrifice, there is an offering of a cake on eleven potsherds <note>original: "Ekadasha kapala"; refers to 11 clay pieces used to bake sacrificial cake.</note> dedicated to the deities Agni and Vishnu...

<summary>This page explains the six auxiliary sciences (Vedangas) needed to interpret the Vedas and introduces the Kalpa Sutras (ritual manuals). It notes the distinction between preparatory religious knowledge and the higher spiritual knowledge of the Upanishads.</summary>
<keywords>Vedangas, Shiksha, Kalpa Sutras, Phonetics, Apara Vidya, Para Vidya, Mundakopanishad, Ritual manuals, Sanskrit pronunciation</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical introduction to the Vedic Kalpa Sutras. It transitions from defining the "Lower Knowledge" (Apara Vidya) to explaining the specific logic behind the sequence of Vedic texts, particularly addressing why the order of mantras in teaching often differs from their order in ritual practice.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>Introduction ||</header>

# Introduction ||

The <term>Brahmana</term> <note>The portion of the Veda that explains the meaning and performance of rituals.</note> sections first describe the **Dikshaniyeishti** <note>A preliminary ritual of consecration for the patron of a sacrifice.</note>, stating that one should perform the offering of the **Purodasha** <note>original: "Yajnapistaka"; a sacrificial cake made of ground grain.</note>. (If the *Ashvalayana Kalpa Sutra* had followed the sequence found in the Brahmana section, it would have been appropriate to write about the Dikshaniyeishti first.) In response to these arguments, it is said that the **Mantra-kanda** <note>The section of the Veda containing the actual hymns and formulas.</note> follows the order of **Brahmayagna** <note>The daily "sacred study" or recitation of the Vedas by a student or teacher.</note> and **Japa** <note>The meditative repetition of a mantra.</note>, rather than the sequence of ritual performance. (It is not the case that the mantras for rituals performed first are recorded first. Rather, the sequence depends on what must be taught to a disciple first—the tradition of study—and the order followed by priests based on the number of repetitions required.)

The rules for Brahmayagna are also observed; for instance, any recitation of a verse from the *Rigveda*, a melody from the *Samaveda*, or a formula from the *Yajurveda* constitutes a **Brahmayagna**. In this sacred study (when reading the *Rigveda Samhita*), the rule is to first read the hymn beginning with **"I praise Agni"** <note>original: "Agnimile"; the opening words of the Rigveda.</note>. In the **Vachastoma** <note>A specific ritual of "speech-praise."</note>, the rule is to recite every verse of the *Rik*, *Yajus*, and *Saman*. [Here, one must recite according to the sequence established by the **Sampradaya**—the traditional lineage of teachers.]

There is a rule stating that if the sun has not yet risen even after reaching the **Ashvin Graha** <note>A specific cup-offering to the twin Ashvin deities during the morning ritual.</note>, one must recite all the **Dashatari** <note>Literally "belonging to the ten"; referring to the ten books (Mandalas) of the Rigveda.</note> mantras. Furthermore, it is prescribed that those who accept gifts and are fasting must recite the Vedas three times as a **Prayashchitta** <note>An act of penance or atonement.</note>. [In these cases as well, the sequence established by tradition is to be respected.]

Regarding the **Viniyoga** <note>The specific application or "assignment" of a mantra to a particular ritual action.</note> of these Mantras—meaning where specific mantras must be recited—the traditional sequence of the teachers [the order of before and after] must be accepted with respect. In assigning a specific mantra to a specific task, sages like **Ashvalayana** applied the mantras according to the proofs of **Shruti** (revelation), **Linga** (indicative mark), **Vakya** (contextual sentence), and **Prakarana** (subject matter), as established in the **Mimamsa** philosophy. (For a detailed explanation of Shruti, Linga, etc., see the *Mimamsa Darshana*.) 

If this is understood, there is no conflict even if the sequence of the Mantra section does not match the ritual order. Mantras such as **"For food, [I cut] thee"** <note>original: "Ishe tva"; the opening words of the Yajurveda.</note> have been codified in the order they are used to support the ritual actions. Ashvalayana, **Gobhila**, and others have constructed their **Kalpa Sutras** according to this rule. Since they have been handed down through the sacred tradition in that order, that same order is to be accepted for chanting and study.

<summary>This page explains that the order of mantras in the Kalpa Sutras follows the tradition of teaching and study (Brahmayagna) rather than the strict chronological order of ritual performance. It justifies this sequence using principles from Mimamsa philosophy and traditional lineage.</summary>
<keywords>Kalpa Sutras, Brahmayagna, Ashvalayana, Gobhila, Mimamsa, Viniyoga, Ritual sequence, Vedic study</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of why the Kalpa Sutras (ritual manuals) are structured differently than the Brahmanas (theological commentaries). It transitions from the logic of Vedic study to the practical necessity of ritual archetypes, specifically why the New and Full Moon sacrifices must be explained before the Consecration rituals.</meta>

<page-num>४</page-num>
<header>Gobhila Grihya Sutra - [Investigation of the Kalpa Sutras]</header>

Although the **Consecration Offering** (<term>Dikshaniya Ishti</term> <note>A ritual performed to initiate or consecrate a patron before a major sacrifice.</note>) is mentioned first in the **Brahmana** portion of the Veda, this offering is actually a modified version (<term>vikriti</term>) of the **New and Full Moon Offerings** (<term>Darshapurnamasa Ishti</term>). For this reason, it depends upon the rules of the New and Full Moon rituals. (Without first explaining the New and Full Moon rituals, the description of the Consecration Offering remains incomplete, because the specific procedural steps of the New and Full Moon rituals are extended by analogy (<term>atidishta</term>) into the Consecration ritual.) Therefore, it was proper for authors like **Ashvalayana** <note>A famous ancient sage and author of a Shrauta Sutra belonging to the Rigveda.</note> to explain the New and Full Moon sacrifices first. This demonstrates that the **Kalpa Sutra** texts perform a great service by teaching the practical application (<term>viniyoga</term>) of Vedic hymns during the performance of a sacrifice.

If someone were to object, saying: "Ashvalayana can certainly assign the application of the **Samidheni** <note>Verses recited while the sacrificial fire is being kindled.</note> verses like **'Pra vo raja...'** <note>original: "प्रवोराज" (Pra vo raja...); Rigveda 10.21.1, a hymn to Agni.</note> because they are part of his own traditional study (<term>amnaya</term>). But how can he assign the application of verses like **'Nama pravakte...'** <note>original: "नम प्रवक्त" (Nama pravakte...); a formulaic greeting to the proclaimer.</note> when he has not studied those specific mantras in his own branch?" Just as it is proper to assign the application of mantras one has studied, how can one assign the application of mantras that are not present in one's own branch? Why did Ashvalayana assign their use even though he did not find them in his own Vedic branch (<term>shakha</term>)? 

The answer to this is that there is no fault here. This is because it is established that even mantras studied in other branches (<term>shakhantara</term>) have their ritual application confirmed in other **Brahmanas**; the same logic should be understood here. Regardless of which branch a specific ritual quality or action is taught in, all those mantras can be gathered together for the successful completion of the ritual. (A ritual prescribed in one place may require a specific quality or verse prescribed elsewhere; therefore, mantras from different branches can be applied together.) Those who are well-versed in the **Mimamsa** <note>The school of Hindu philosophy dealing with the art of Vedic interpretation and ritual logic.</note> state that the same ritual action is being described across all Vedic branches. 

Therefore, just as studying **Shiksha** <note>The science of phonetics and pronunciation, one of the six limbs of the Veda.</note> is essential for gaining knowledge of the Veda, the study of **Kalpa** <note>The science of ritual procedure.</note> is absolutely necessary for investigating the meaning of the Veda. In the Kalpa Sutras, instruction is given on how to perform sacrifices through the application of mantras. Without knowing this science, it is impossible to resolve the various doubts that arise regarding the performance of sacrifices. Similarly, the other six limbs of the Veda, such as **Grammar** (<term>Vyakarana</term>), have their own distinct purposes; without knowledge of these, true understanding of the Veda is impossible.

This **"Kalpa Sutra"** is divided into two parts: the **"Shrauta Sutra"** and the **"Grihya Sutra."** Those rituals like the **Agnishtoma** <note>A major five-day Soma sacrifice.</note>, which are performed using the fires directly prescribed by the Shruti (revelation), are performed in what is called the **Shrautagni** <note>The "Sacred Fire" maintained by a householder for high-level public rituals.</note>. All the ritual duties associated with that fire, and the systematic manuals in which these duties are taught—

<summary>This page explains why ritual manuals (Kalpa Sutras) prioritize the New and Full Moon sacrifices as procedural archetypes. It also justifies the use of mantras across different Vedic branches and introduces the division between Shrauta (public) and Grihya (domestic) rituals.</summary>

<keywords>Darshapurnamasa, Dikshaniya Ishti, Ashvalayana, Kalpa Sutra, Viniyoga, Samidheni, Shakhantara, Mimamsa, Vedanga, Shrauta Sutra, Grihya Sutra</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Kalpa Sutras, moving from the logic of ritual archetypes to the specific classification of "Shrauta" (solemn) versus "Grihya" (domestic) rituals. It introduces the specific lineage of the author Gobhila and the Samaveda tradition.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>Introduction</header>

...these are also called **"Shrauta"** <term>Shrauta</term> <note>Rituals derived directly from the Shruti (revelation), typically involving the three sacred fires and specialized priests.</note>. In the same way, the rituals performed using the sacred fire designated for marriage and other domestic duties—as well as the entire sequence of those acts and the texts that prescribe their methods—are called **"Grihya"** <term>Grihya</term> <note>Literally "belonging to the home"; rituals performed by the householder himself using a single domestic fire.</note>. Although there is more than one **"Shrauta Sutra"** for each Veda, there is no strict rule that a specific Shrauta Sutra must be followed by a specific branch (<term>shakha</term>). Therefore, the great commentator **Sayanacharya** states: "The performance of any ritual is common across all branches." <note>original: "सर्वशाखा प्रत्येकमेकं कर्म" (sarvashākha pratyekamekaṃ karma)</note> *

This means that the performance of the ritual prescribed for all branches is essentially the same. **Jaimini**, the master of the **Mimamsa** school <note>The philosophical tradition focused on the correct interpretation and execution of Vedic ritual.</note>, also established this same system (Mimamsa, Chapter 2, Part 4), and this point is reiterated in works like the *Adhikarana Mala*. However, the **Grihya Sutras** vary according to the different branches of the Veda. Consequently, one must accept as many Grihya Sutras as there are branches of that Veda. For the **Samaveda**, the author of the Shrauta Sutra is **Latyayana**, and the author of the Grihya text for his **Kauthumi** branch is the teacher **Gobhila**. All the life-cycle rituals, such as marriage, for the Brahmins who study this particular branch are performed according to the arrangements found in the Sutras composed by this very Gobhila. Furthermore, based on the authority of this text, the great scholar **Mahamahopadhyaya Bhavadeva Bhatta** popularized the procedures for marriage and other rituals in the region of **Bengal** <note>original: "वङ्गदेश" (Vangadesh)</note>. Other regions follow different systems. This teacher Gobhila is the author of four texts: the *Grihya Sutra*, the *Sandhya Sutra* <note>Daily twilight devotions.</note>, the *Snana Sutra* <note>Ritual bathing.</note>, and the *Shraddha Sutra* <note>Rites for ancestors.</note>. If there is interest from our patrons, we shall publish these texts along with translations as well.

Although a commentary (<term>vritti</term>) by **Narayanopadhyaya** exists for this *Gobhila Grihya Sutra*, it contains so many errors due to the scribe's inattention that correcting it for printing would require as much effort as writing a brand new commentary. For this reason, it was set aside. Another Sanskrit commentary on this *Grihya Sutra* was written by **Pandit Chandrakanta Tarkalankar**. This commentary was composed by harmonizing the views of the Grihya Sutras of various other branches, which has made it extremely voluminous. In our view, the commentary on a specific branch’s Grihya Sutra should stay true to that specific branch, because the duties prescribed in one's own Grihya Sutra are written specifically according to one's own branch of study.

Such a Sanskrit commentary on this *Grihya Sutra* has been written by **Pandit Satyavrata Samashrami**, and we have provided this current translation in the common language following that Sanskrit commentary.

---

* See his introduction to the **Rigveda** commentary <note>original: "तदीय ऋग्भाष्यभूमिका द्रष्टव्य" (tadiya rigbhashyabhumika drashtavya)</note>.

<summary>This page defines the difference between Shrauta (solemn) and Grihya (domestic) rituals, noting that while Shrauta rites are universal, Grihya rites are specific to Vedic branches. It establishes Gobhila as the primary authority for the Samaveda's Kauthumi branch and discusses the regional and scholarly lineage of his works.</summary>
<keywords>Shrauta, Grihya, Samaveda, Gobhila, Latyayana, Kauthumi branch, Bhavadeva Bhatta, Bengal, Sayanacharya, Jaimini, ritual manuals, Vedic branches</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Gobhila Grihya Sutra, specifically detailing its internal organization and the history of Vedic branches (shakhas). It connects the previous discussion on the necessity of specific Grihya Sutras for different Vedic lineages to the specific structure of the text at hand.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>Introduction to the Gobhila Grihya Sutra - [A Discussion on the Branches of the Vedas]</header>

The **Gobhila Grihya Sutra** <term>Gobhila Grihya Sutra</term> <note>A foundational manual for domestic rituals belonging to the Kauthuma branch of the Samaveda.</note> consists of 4 **Prapathakas** <term>Prapathaka</term> <note>Literally "lessons" or chapters.</note>, and each chapter contains 10 **Khandas** <term>Khanda</term> <note>Sections or subdivisions.</note>. Each section contains one or more **Sutras** <term>Sutra</term> <note>Concise aphorisms or rules.</note>. In total, it comprises 4 chapters, 39 sections, and 1,009 aphorisms. The content is divided into seven primary topics:

1. **General Ritual Methods** <note>original: "सर्वकर्मसाधारण विधि" (sarvakarmasādhāraṇa vidhi)</note>
2. **Daily Sacrifices** <note>original: "अहर्यज्ञ" (aharyajña)</note>
3. **New and Full Moon Sacrifices** <note>original: "दर्शपौर्णमास" (darśapaurṇamāsa)</note>
4. **Life-Cycle Rites (Marriage, etc.)** <note>original: "विवाहादि संस्कार" (vivāhādi saṃskāra)</note>
5. **Household Duties** <note>original: "गृहकर्त्तव्य" (gṛhakarttavya)</note>
6. **Rites for Specific Desires** <note>original: "काम्यकर्म" (kāmyakarma)</note>
7. **Rites of Worship and Honor** <note>original: "अर्हणीय प्रकरण" (arhaṇīya prakaraṇa)</note>

## The Branches of the Vedas

In the *Charanavyuha*—an appendix to the Yajurveda composed by the great sage **Shaunaka**—the mention of numerous types of Vedic branches makes it clearly evident that various branches <term>shakhas</term> <note>Literally "branches"; different schools or traditions of reciting and interpreting the same Veda.</note> of the different Vedas existed long before the *Charanavyuha* was even written. The names of all those Vedic "divisions" and "branches" that had already vanished or merged with other branches before the *Charanavyuha* was composed are not found in it. However, the branches that were still in existence at the time of its composition are undoubtedly included in its list.

Regarding the **Rigveda**, only five branches are mentioned by name: **Shakala, Bashkala, Shankhayana, Mandukayana,** and **Ashvalayana**. However, there is no mention of ancient branches like the **Aitareya, Kaushitaki, Paingi, Shaishiriya**, and others. In the *Rigvedic Pratishakhya* <term>Pratishakhya</term> <note>An ancient treatise on the phonetics and grammar specific to a particular Veda.</note>, the teachers who founded the Shakala, Shankhayana, Ashvalayana, Mandukayana, and Bashkala branches are also mentioned. For example:

> "The collection of Rigvedic verses was first recited with great effort by **Shakala**, and thereafter by four others: **Shankhayana** and **Ashvalayana**, as well as **Manduka** and **Bashkala**. All these six are sages of the Rigveda <term>Bahvricha</term> <note>A term for the Rigveda, meaning "many verses."</note>, and all are knowers of the same single Veda."
> — (From the *Shaunaka Pratishakhya*) <note>original: “ऋचां समूह ऋग्वेदस्त सभ्यस्य प्रयत्नतः । पठितः शाकलेनादौ, चतुर्भिस्तदनन्तरम् । शांखपाश्वलायनौ चैव, माण्डुको बाष्कलस्तथा । बह्वृचां ऋषयः सर्वे, षडैते एक वेदिनः”</note>

It does not appear that there was any fundamental difference among the students of the various branches of a single Veda. Rather, one sees minor variations across different branches: a slight deviation or change in a word or mantra in one place, the addition of two or four new mantras in another, the transposition of the order of mantras elsewhere, or differences in the phonetic pronunciation of the mantras. Aside from these minor, almost negligible changes occurring through the lineage of teachers and students, no other distinction is seen; essentially, the **Samhita** <term>Samhita</term> <note>The core collection of hymns or mantras of a Veda.</note> of all branches remains the same.

<summary>This page outlines the structural division of the Gobhila Grihya Sutra and discusses the historical evolution of Vedic branches (shakhas), noting that while many branches once existed, their core texts remained largely consistent.</summary>
<keywords>Gobhila Grihya Sutra, Prapathaka, Khanda, Sutra, Charanavyuha, Shaunaka, Shakha, Rigveda, Samhita, Vedic ritual categories</keywords>

<header>Introduction</header>

# The Branches of the Samaveda

The lineage of the teachers who promoted the branches of the **Samaveda** <term>Samaveda</term> <note>The Veda of Melodies and Chants.</note> is provided in the **Vishnu Purana** <note>One of the eighteen major Puranas, a sacred Hindu text focusing on the deity Vishnu and containing genealogical and cosmological records.</note> (Book 3, Chapter 6) as follows: **Jaimini**, **Sumantu**, and **Sukarma** successively studied and taught the *Samaveda Samhita*. **Sukarma**, the grandson of Jaimini, had two disciples named **Hiranyanabha** and **Paushpinji**. Among them, the fifteen disciples of Hiranyanabha, a resident of the Kosala region, became famous as the "Eastern Chanters of the Sama" <note>original: "प्राच्यसामग" (prācyasāmaga)</note>. Through the twenty-four disciples of the sage **Kriti** (who was among them), many branches of the Samaveda were established. **Paushpinji**, the other disciple of Sukarma, had four principal disciples named **Lokakshi**, **Kuthumi**, **Kusidi**, and **Languli**. According to the opinion of the *Vishnu Purana*, the Samaveda had 1,000 branches <term>shakhas</term> <note>Literally "branches"; these represent different recensions or schools of Vedic tradition.</note>. According to **Durgacharya**, the commentator on the **Nirukta** <note>An ancient treatise on etymology and lexicography by Yaska.</note>, the Samaveda was also divided into a thousand branches.

Thus, according to the records in texts like the **Charanavyuha** <note>A text detailing the various schools and branches of the four Vedas.</note>, there are 1,131 or 1,137 branches of the four Vedas. The *Charanavyuha* mentions these seven principal branches of the Samaveda: **Ranayaniya**, **Shathya**, **Mugdha**, **Kalaya**, **Mahakalaya**, **Shardula**, **Langulyayana**, and **Kauthuma**. The **Asurayana**, **Vatayana**, **Pranjali**, **Dvaitabhrita**, **Prachinyogya**, and **Naigeya** are merely sub-branches included within the Kauthuma branch. The **Kauthuma** branch is prevalent in Gujarat, the **Jaiminiya** branch in Karnataka, and the **Ranayaniya** branch in the Maharashtra region. In the Bengal region, Brahmins of Samavedic branches other than the Kauthuma are not found.

> “The branch of the Samaveda tree was Jaimini, the disciple of Vyasa. Hear from me, O Maitreya, the sequence in which he divided it. (1)
> His son was Sumantu, and Sukarma was his son. These two great sages each studied one collection. (2)
> Then his son Sukarma made a thousand divisions of that collection. His two highly intelligent disciples then received them: (3)
> Hiranyanabha of Kosala and Paushpinji, the best of the twice-born. The fifteen disciples from them are remembered as the Northern Chanters. (4)
> Lokakshi, Kuthumi, and also Kusidi and Languli.”
> <note>original Sanskrit verse: "सामवेदतरो: शाखा व्यासशिष्य: स जैमिनि:..."</note>

---

<note>* According to the statement of the Great Sage Patanjali, there are 101 branches of the Yajurveda, 1,000 of the Samaveda, 21 of the Rigveda, and 9 branches of the Atharvaveda. ** "One hundred and one branches for the practitioners of the Yajurveda; the Samaveda has a thousand paths. Twenty-one for the Rigveda, and the Atharva Veda is nine-fold." — From the Mahabhashya <note>original: "एक शतमध्वर्यु शाखा सहस्र वर्त्मा सामवेद ।... महाभाष्ये"</note></note>

<summary>This page details the traditional lineage and geographical distribution of the Samaveda's branches, citing authoritative texts like the Vishnu Purana and Mahabhashya to account for its 1,000 historical recensions.</summary>

<header>[ Thoughts on the Branches of the Veda ]</header>

### -> From the Gobhila Grihya Sutra <-

> Through the variations of Paushyanji and Glipsa, the collections <term>Samhitas</term> were multiplied. || 5 ||
> And the disciple of Hiranyanabha, the great-minded one named Kriti, taught twenty-four collections to his students. || 6 ||
> Through them also, this Samaveda was expanded into many branches. || 7 ||" <margin>Vishnu Purana 3.6</margin>

# Did the branches of the Vedas originate from Vyasa-ji?

Many people say that Lord Krishna Dvaipayana <note>Another name for the sage Vyasa, who is traditionally credited with arranging the Vedas.</note> (Maharishi Vyasa) was the divider of the Vedas, the seer <term>mantra-drashta</term> of the hymns, and the originator of the branches <term>shakhas</term>. It is also written this way in the Puranas (such as the *Vishnu Purana* and *Bhagavata Purana*), but this is not entirely correct. 

Maharishi Vyasa was a peerless scholar of the Veda. He collected the Vedic mantras that were scattered here and there in an even better manner; he placed the mantras of each Veda in their proper locations and taught them to many disciples according to various collections <term>Samhitas</term>. He also used great effort to bring to light the branches that existed before his time but had become dormant or lost due to the passage of time, discovering them from various places and teaching them to his four disciples. In his era, the Veda reached its ultimate peak of development, and he was the unique promoter of the Veda at that time. Through his intellect, penance, and diligence, he traced and systematized all the branches of the Veda as far as possible. Because of this, it is said that the Veda "originated" from "Veda Vyasa" <note>The name "Veda Vyasa" literally means "the one who divided or arranged the Veda."</note>. 

It is written: "The composition of the branches occurred in the Dvapara age" <note>original: "शाखाप्रणयनं चैव द्वापरे सम्भूदिदम्" (śākhāpraṇayanaṃ caiva dvāpare sambhūdidam)</note>. This means that the branches of the Vedas continued to proliferate until the Dvapara Yuga <note>In Hindu cosmology, the third of the four world ages.</note>. We know this because even before the Dvapara, during the Treta Yuga, these branches existed. For instance, the *Ayodhya Kanda* of Valmiki’s *Ramayana* mentions "the teachers of the Taittiriyas" <note>original: "आचार्यां स्तैत्तिरीयाणाम्" (ācāryāṃ staittirīyāṇām)</note> and again, "these Kathas and Kalapas" <note>original: "एतेकठाः कलापाश्च" (etekaṭhāḥ kalāpāśca)</note>. However, the Puranas state the opposite—that branches like the Katha and Kalapa originated among the disciples of Vyasa. The explanation for this contradiction has already been provided <note>i.e., Vyasa revitalized and organized existing traditions rather than inventing them anew.</note>.

# The Lineage of Teachers of the Samaveda

The list of teachers who promoted the tradition of the Samaveda <margin>*</margin> is recorded as follows: Brahma taught Brihaspati, he taught Narada, from him it went to Vishvaksena, he taught Vyasa the son of Parashara, and Vyasa taught Jaimini...

---

<margin>*</margin> Now, the author enumerates the teachers who promoted the tradition of this *Samavidhana*. This is the method of Prajapati. Prajapati spoke this to Brihaspati; Brihaspati to Narada; Narada to Vishvaksena; Vishvaksena to Vyasa, the son of Parashara; Vyasa to Jaimini; Jaimini to Paushpindya; Paushpindya to Parasharyayana; Parasharyayana to Badarayana; Badarayana to Tarkshya and Shalyani; and Tarkshya and Shalyani to many others.
-> The *Samavidhana Brahmana* of the Samaveda <-

<header>Introduction</header>

Paushpinji taught Parasharyayana, who taught Badarayana, who in turn taught Tandi and Shatyayana; these two then taught many disciples. * The number of Brahmana texts <term>Brahmana: prose commentaries on the Vedas that explain ritual procedures</term> belonging to the Samaveda is well-documented. The famous commentator Pandit Kumarila Bhatta writes in his work titled *Tantra Varttika* as follows: 1. Tandya (also known as Praudha, Maha, or Panchavimsha), 2. Shadvimsha, 3. Upanishad (Chhandogya), 4. Samhitopanishad (Jaiminiya or Talavakara), 5. Samavidhana, 6. Devatadhyaya, 7. Arsheya, and 8. Vamsha Brahmana. Among these, the *Shadvimsha Brahmana*—which is merely a supplement to the *Tandya Brahmana*—contains a sixth part called the *Adbhuta Brahmana*. The remaining eight chapters of the ten-chapter *Chhandogya* are the *Chhandogya Upanishad*. The final chapter of the *Talavakara Brahmana* is famous by the name *Kena* or *Talavakara Upanishad*. Among the eight Brahmanas mentioned above, the last four are nothing more than indices <term>Anukramani: systematic lists providing the poet, deity, and meter of hymns</term> for the Samaveda.

## The Sacrificer and the Priest, or the Officiants

A *Yajamana* <term>Yajamana: the patron who pays for and benefits from a sacrifice</term> is one who performs sacrificial rituals <term>Yajnanushthana</term> in their own home. A *Ritvik* is one who is appointed at a specified time to perform the sacrificial work for their own or another's welfare; *Purohita* <term>Purohita: a family priest or chaplain</term> or *Purodha* are simply other names for this role. Over time, as the complexity of sacrificial ceremonies grew, the influence and number of the *Ritviks* also increased, and they transformed into a distinct, top-tier class within the Eternal Aryan or Vedic society. In earlier times, the Eternal...

---

> The eight Brahmanas which are sung along with the secret teachings <term>Rahasya: referring here to the Upanishads</term>; among all those who study the Chhandogya, there is no one who does not follow the regulated accents. || 2 ||
->(From the *Tantra Varttika* by Kumarila Bhatta, 1.3)<-

* Although there are many various texts belonging to the 1000 branches <term>Shakhas</term> of the Samaveda, only these specific texts have been recovered to this day.

<summary>This page lists the primary Brahmana texts and thirty extant works associated with the Samaveda, while defining the social roles of the Yajamana (sacrificer) and the Ritvik (priest).</summary>
<keywords>Samaveda, Brahmana texts, Kumarila Bhatta, Chhandogya Upanishad, Ritvik, Yajamana, Vedic literature, Tantra Varttika</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of Vedic ritual and the transition from individual practice to a professional priestly class, following the list of Samavedic texts provided on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>Of the Gobhila Gryha Sutra - [A Discussion on the Patron and the Priest]</header>

In the Aryan era <note>The early Vedic period</note>, the patron <term>Yajamana: the person who institutes and pays for a sacrifice</term> himself would produce fire by rubbing pieces of wood together, and then offer holy oblations of clarified butter into that blazing fire for the fulfillment of his desires. Because the appointment of an officiating priest <term>Ritvik: a professional priest hired for specific rituals</term> was a costly undertaking, only the wealthy had their sacrifices performed by a domestic priest <term>Purohita: literally 'one placed in front,' a family priest or advisor</term>. In other words, groups of officiating priests were summoned only by the wealthy. * Over time, the responsibility for performing these rites was handed over to priests who were masters of the mantras. Consequently, their influence and majesty increased significantly. The *Rigveda Samhita* was designated for the **Hotr** <note>original: "Bahvricha," meaning "versed in many verses"</note> priests. The *Samaveda* and *Yajurveda Samhitas* were assigned respectively to the **Udgatri** <note>original: "Chhandoga," the singer of the chants</note> and the **Adhvaryu** priests. The specific duties of these different classes of priests were recorded in the Brahmana texts. For the Rigvedic priests, the *Aitareya* and *Kaushitaki Brahmanas* were assigned; for the Udgatris, the *Tandya Brahmana*; for the Adhvaryu priests, the *Taittiriya* and *Shatapatha Brahmanas*; and for the Atharvavedic priests, the *Gopatha Brahmana* was designated. Following the composition of the Brahmana texts, the **Kalpa** or **Shrauta** and **Gryha Sutras** <note>Manuals for public and domestic rituals, respectively</note> began to be formed according to the different branches <term>Shakha: a specific school or tradition of Veda recitation</term> of their respective Vedas. Large-scale sacrifices were performed according to the Shrauta texts, and domestic rites <note>Smartta Karma</note> were conducted according to the Gryha Sutras. 

When the study and teaching of the Vedas declined and obstacles began to arise in Vedic rituals, kings became ignorant of Vedic knowledge. From that time, the seeds of mutual jealousy and hatred were sown among the priests. They began to write slander against one another. Previously, the **Hotr** (Rigvedic), **Adhvaryu** (Yajurvedic), **Udgatri** (Samavedic), and **Brahma** (the Atharvavedic or a master of all four Vedas) had performed the sacrificial work together with mutual joy, each fulfilling their own duty in a single sacrifice. However, they now began to create independent texts, filling them with self-praise and the condemnation of those belonging to other Vedas. The result was that a terrible sectarian <note>original: "sampradayik," relating to different schools or sects</note> malice spread among the kings, the subjects, and indeed all of humanity, leading to the ruin of India <note>Bharatvarsha</note>. As an example of this, we cite the evidence of the text titled "Atharva-Parishishta" <note>A supplementary work of the Atharvaveda tradition</note>.

## Severe Sectarian Hatred Among the Priests!!!

> In the 192nd chapter of the "Atharva-Parishishta" text, it is written that the Rigvedic 'Bahvricha' priest destroys the kingdom of the patron, and the Yajurvedic 'Adhvaryu' priest destroys the patron’s wife and children; and the patron for whom a Samavedic 'Chhandoga' serves as a priest will have his wealth destroyed. This was born of ignorance or negligence...

---

* The sage Apastamba has written in the text titled 'Yajna Paribhasha' <note>Definition of Sacrifice</note> as follows: — It (the sacrifice) is prescribed by the three Vedas. [1] By the Rigveda, Yajurveda, and Samaveda. [2] The Hotr performs with the Rigveda. [16] The Udgatri with the Samaveda. [17] The Adhvaryu with the Yajurveda. [18] The Brahma with all of them. [19] <note>original Sanskrit: "sa (yajña) tribhirvedaivirdhīyate... rgvedena hotā karoti... sāmavedenodgātā... yajurvedenādhvaryu... sarvairbrahmā"</note>

<summary>This page describes the transition from individual domestic worship to a specialized, professional priestly hierarchy. It also laments a historical decline in Vedic scholarship, which allegedly led to sectarian rivalries and the production of polemical literature between the different Vedic schools.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the specialized roles of Vedic priests, shifting from the general history of the Brahmanas to the intense sectarian competition for the prestigious position of the royal chaplain (Purohita).</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>Introduction</header>

...those who select a **Rigvedic** priest <note>original: "Bahvricha"</note> for the office of royal chaplain <term>Purohitya: the position of a king’s domestic priest and advisor</term> will undoubtedly see their country, kingdom, city, and ministers destroyed. The king who appoints a **Yajurvedic** priest <note>original: "Adhvaryu"</note> as his royal chaplain will soon be stripped of his wealth and transport <note>such as chariots and horses</note> and be killed by the strike of an enemy's weapon. Just as a lame person is unable to walk on the intended path, or as a bird newly hatched from an egg is unable to soar through the sky like a fully grown bird; so too, a king is unable to achieve progress or success through a **Samavedic** <note>original: "Chhandoga"</note> priest. Furthermore, if a priest from the **Jalada** or **Maudga** branches of the *Atharvaveda* serves as a king's chaplain, that king will be removed from his throne within ten or twelve months. Only a **Brahma** priest <note>the high-priest who oversees the entire ritual</note> of the *Atharvaveda* is the greatest among all priests; they are capable of managing terrible events and performing rites of pacification, and they can protect the sacrifice from many obstacles and dangers.

**Angira** <note>a sage associated with the Atharvaveda</note> alone is the sole regulating authority of the sacrifice. Atharvavedic priests, who are masters of the **Veda of Brahman** <note>original: "Brahmaveda"</note>, perform the rituals required to pacify various types of sacrificial disturbances <term>Yajnotpata: ill-omens or errors occurring during a ritual</term>—whether they be divine, atmospheric, or earthly. Neither the Yajurvedic, the Samavedic, nor the Rigvedic priests can pacify these various kinds of disturbances. Driven by deep sectarian malice, these authors—blinded by anger—have used these entirely illegal and harsh words against priests of other Vedas and different branches of their own Veda. In doing so, they have shown a complete lack of discernment <note>original: "kandakanda vihinata"</note> in their attempt to assert their own superiority.

> A Rigvedic priest destroys the kingdom; a Yajurvedic priest destroys the sons.
> A Samavedic priest destroys wealth; therefore, the Atharvavedic priest is the only true Guru.
> Whether through ignorance or negligence, the king who takes a Rigvedic priest as his Guru
> Will undoubtedly face the destruction of his country, kingdom, city, and ministers.
> If a king appoints a Yajurvedic priest as his royal chaplain,
> He is quickly slain by weapons, deprived of both wealth and his mounts.
> Just as a lame man cannot travel the road, or a hatchling fly through the heavens,
> Even so, with a Samavedic Guru, a king never achieves growth.
> If the royal chaplain is from the Jalada branch, or somehow from the Maudga branch...

---

* There are also other branches such as the **Shaunaka** and **Paippalada** branches <note>these are the two surviving traditions of the Atharvaveda</note>.

<summary>This page describes the intense sectarian rivalry between Vedic schools, where Atharvavedic texts claim that only their priests are fit to be royal chaplains, warning that other priests bring ruin to the king. The author critiques these claims as being motivated by professional jealousy rather than religious Law.</summary>
<keywords>Purohita, Atharvaveda, sectarianism, ritual errors, royal chaplain, Brahma priest, Angira, priestly rivalry</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the superiority of the Atharvavedic priest (the Brahma) in royal and ritual service, concluding the citation from the Atharva-parishishta before transitioning into a broader philosophical discussion on the nature and necessity of the Samskaras (Life-Cycle Sacraments).</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>The Gobhila Grhyasutra — [A Discussion on Sacraments]</header>

Within ten months to a year, a king who appoints the wrong priest sees his kingdom fall into ruin.
The priest of the *Atharvaveda* <note>original: "Atharva"</note> can both direct fierce power and pacify terrifying portents.
The Atharvan protects the sacrifice, and the sage **Angira** is the lord of the sacrifice.
The knower of the **Brahmaveda** <note>another name for the Atharvaveda</note> is the one who pacifies the various types of ill-omens—whether they be celestial, atmospheric, or terrestrial; therefore, the descendant of **Bhrigu** <note>the chief priest</note> sits to the south.
The **Brahma** priest <note>the presiding priest</note> alone can pacify disturbances; neither the **Adhvaryu** <note>Yajurvedic priest</note>, nor the **Chhandoga** <note>Samavedic priest</note>, nor the **Bahvricha** <note>Rigvedic priest</note> are capable of this.
The *Brahma* priest protects against demonic forces; therefore, the *Brahma* must be one who is learned in the *Atharvaveda*. — (*Atharva-parishishta*)

->॥ Sacraments ॥<-

The assembly of Aryan sages perceived through their divine vision that an unruly human race, if left without rules, would only progress toward a steady decline. They saw that humanity would never set foot upon the auspicious path of the virtuous; rather, driven by their passions, they would constantly perform wicked deeds. Consequently, in such a state, the social fabric cannot remain intact, nor can a sense of religious duty blossom within the human heart. For this reason, with the aim of securing both the worldly and otherworldly benefit of every individual and society as a whole, the great sages systematically established a framework of special rules. Just as the foundation of a house must be made strong before the house itself can be strengthened, the ancient sages warned us even before a human infant is born:

> “He for whom the ritual from conception to the crematorium is ordained with sacred hymns <note>original: "mantras"</note>, he alone has the right to the scriptures, and never anyone else.” (Manu Smriti: 2:91) <note>This verse emphasizes that spiritual eligibility is earned through the lifelong performance of sacraments.</note>

Therefore, it is the duty of every Aryan to perform all the **Samskaras** <term>Samskara: life-cycle sacraments or rites of passage intended to refine the individual</term>, beginning with **Garbhadhana** <term>Garbhadhana: the sacrament of conception</term>, at their proper times. The word *Samskara* means "purification"—the process of removing flaws and imparting qualities. In other words, through these sacraments, inherent defects in an object are destroyed, and specific virtues are added. Consider a mirror: by its very nature, it is clear and capable of holding a reflection <note>original: "pratibimbagrahita"</note>. However, due to a specific defect, it may become covered in grime. As long as that dirt is not removed, its reflectivity and clarity cannot be revealed. For this reason, it requires a "refinement" <note>original: "Samskara"</note>. Once that external tarnish is removed through the action of polishing, the "mirror-ness" of the mirror shines forth once again.

<summary>This page concludes the defense of the Atharvavedic priest's role in pacifying omens and introduces the philosophical necessity of the Samskaras (sacraments) as essential tools for human refinement and social stability.</summary>
<keywords>Atharva-parishishta, Brahma priest, Samskara, Manu Smriti, purification, Garbhadhana, human refinement, social order</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general necessity of rituals to the specific philosophical and practical purposes of the seventeen Samskaras (sacraments). It defines these rites as a process of purification and refinement, likening the development of a person to the creation of a painting, and begins the detailed explanation of the first rite, Garbhadhana (Conception).</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>Introduction</header>

This is the fruit of the first-mentioned sacrament. In certain cases, the inherent defects of an object cannot be entirely removed; rather, a specific quality or excellence is produced within it. Similarly, the individual soul, or the inner mind <note>original: "antahkarana"</note>, is pure by its very nature. However, through contact with desires and worldly attachments, impurity or ignorance <note>original: "ajnana"</note> arises within it. Once impurity is present, the light of discernment <note>original: "viveka"</note> can no longer shine. Without the manifestation of discernment, the downfall of the soul is inevitable, and it is a settled principle that fallen souls encounter unrest everywhere. The primary purpose of the <term>Samskaras</term> <note>life-cycle sacraments intended to refine and purify the individual</note> is to remove that impurity, which is the root of all misfortunes, and to ignite one's own inner luster. The authors of the scriptures have explained this beautifully:

->Just as a painting is gradually brought to life through many different colors and strokes,<-
->so too is the spiritual luster of a Brahmin brought to perfection through rituals performed according to the sacred laws.<-
<note>original: "Citraṃ karmmānekai raṅgairunmīlyate yathāśanaiḥ | brāhmaṇyamapitadvatsyāt saṃskārairvidhipūrvvakaiḥ ||"</note>

Just as a picture is revealed or completed step by step through the artist’s skill and the arrangement of limbs and features, the spiritual power of a Brahmin <note>original: "brahmanyatejas"</note> attains perfection through the repeated performance of ritual sacraments according to the rules. According to some authorities, there are 16 of these sacraments; according to others, there are 17, while some suggest even fewer or more. They are: 1. <term>Garbhadhana</term> <note>Conception</note>, 2. <term>Pumsavana</term> <note>the rite to ensure a male child or a healthy fetus</note>, 3. <term>Simantonnayana</term> <note>Parting the hair of the expectant mother</note>, 4. <term>Jatakarma</term> <note>Birth rituals</note>, 5. <term>Namakarana</term> <note>Naming ceremony</note>, 6. <term>Nishkramana</term> <note>The child's first outing</note>, 7. <term>Annaprashana</term> <note>The first feeding of solid food</note>, 8. <term>Chudakarana</term> <note>The first haircut</note>, 9. <term>Karnavedha</term> <note>Ear-piercing</note>, 10. <term>Upanayana</term> <note>The sacred thread ceremony/initiation</note>, 11. <term>Vedarambha</term> <note>Beginning of Vedic study/Student life (Brahmacharya)</note>, 12. <term>Samavartana</term> <note>Graduation/Returning home</note>, 13. <term>Vivaha</term> <note>Marriage</note>, 14. <term>Grihashrama</term> <note>The householder stage</note>, 15. <term>Vanaprastha</term> <note>Retirement to the forest</note>, 16. <term>Sannyasa</term> <note>Renunciation</note>, and 17. <term>Antyeshti</term> <note>The final funeral rites</note>.

# 1. Garbhadhana (Conception)

As stated previously, the primary objective—both general and specific—of all sacraments is the refinement of the "vital energy" <note>original: "tejas"</note> of human beings. To keep a house strong, efforts must begin from the very foundation. The Revelation <note>original: "Shruti"</note>, which is like a mother wishing for the welfare of all people, states with the intent of achieving that profound and highest goal that the defects present in the bodies of the father and mother are transmitted to the body of the child. Modern science also declares this with a clear voice. Many such examples are found in all living creatures; moreover, even the mental dispositions of the parents are transmitted to the offspring. **Manu** states that through the <term>Garbhadhana</term> and other sacraments, the "seed-defects" of a twice-born child <note>defects arising from the impure intentions or materials of the father and mother</note> as well as "womb-defects" <note>original: "garbhika"; biological defects related to the mother's uterus</note> are all removed.

<summary>This page explains that Samskaras function as a purifying process to restore the soul's natural clarity and lists the seventeen major Hindu sacraments. It introduces Garbhadhana (conception) as the foundational rite necessary to eliminate hereditary and prenatal defects.</summary>

<keywords>Samskaras, Garbhadhana, purification, discernment, spiritual luster, Manu, heredity, twice-born, sacraments</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the seventeen Samskaras or sacraments. The previous page concluded the explanation of Garbhadhana (Conception), and this page begins by summarizing why these rituals are vital for the child's development before moving into the second sacrament, Pumsavana.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>The Gobhila Grhya Sutra — [Reflections on the Sacraments]</header>

> "Through the pregnancy oblations, the birth ceremony, the first hair-cutting, and the tying of the sacred thread, the physical and hereditary defects of the twice-born are wiped away." <note>original: Manu Smriti 2.27. The 'twice-born' refers to members of the social classes who undergo spiritual initiation.</note>

The meaning here is that a child inherits the psychological impressions <note>original: "samskara"</note> of the father and mother. Consequently, if any kind of improper or base thought arises in the mother, it will certainly take root in the child's heart. It is recorded in the *Mahabharata* <note>the great Indian epic</note> that the hero Arjuna once narrated the details of a military strategy to his wife, Subhadra. When the story was only half-finished, Subhadra fell asleep. At that time, Abhimanyu, who was still in Subhadra's womb, was able to learn only that half of the battle strategy narrated by his father. Because his mother fell asleep, he could not learn the remaining half. According to the scriptures, it is observed that those initiated through such sacraments become truly fit for religious vows and fully qualified to grasp the teachings of spiritual science <note>original: "adhyatma shastra"</note>.

## 2. Pumsavana

Every action has some purpose or another, and so the <term>Pumsavana</term> <note>literally "quickening a male child," a rite performed during the third month of pregnancy</note> must also have an objective. What is it? The protection of the fetus <note>original: "garbharaksha"</note>. In general, the period between the third and fourth month is a critical time when miscarriages commonly occur. The primary purpose of this sacrament is to deliver the pregnant woman from this great danger. The second purpose concerns the procreation of a male child—that is, will the developing embryo be a boy or a girl? According to <term>Ayurveda</term> <note>the traditional Indian system of medicine</note>, this is not fixed until the third month. This is because, before the third month, the physical signs of 'female' or 'male' have not yet appeared in the fetus. Therefore, performing the Pumsavana rite at that time to encourage the birth of a son is a source of great joy; of this, there is no doubt.

After the husband performs all the ritual acts, he addresses his pregnant wife, saying: "Mitra and Varuna are two male deities—the Ashwini Kumaras are also male, and Lord Varuna is male. (By their grace) a male child has appeared in your womb," and so on. At such a time, it is certain that the pregnant woman becomes filled with great joy and a sense of peace. Without such encouragement and joy, the body—already weary from physical weakness, fainting, and loss of appetite—would have no means to sustain the pregnancy to its completion. Therefore, for those who seek the deeper truth of these matters, the Pumsavana sacrament is essential.

---

<margin>During the time of Pumsavana, the pregnant woman is made to inhale the scent of two banyan fruits along with grains of barley and <term>urad</term> <note>a type of black lentil</note>. It is written in the Ayurveda that this is an excellent medicine for removing defects in the woman's reproductive organs.</margin>

->Introduction<-

...is not to be ignored. On the contrary, besides the visible result, there is also an unseen spiritual benefit <note>original: "adrishta phala" — karmic merit or spiritual fruit that is not immediately visible</note>.

# 3. Simantonnayana <note>The hair-parting ritual</note>

Just as the period from the third month to the fourth month is a time of risk for <term>miscarriage</term> <note>original: "garbhachyuti" — the premature falling away of the fetus</note>, so too from the sixth month to the eighth month is another period of risk for <term>premature birth</term> <note>original: "garbhabhransha" — the slipping or displacement of the fetus</note>. The more depressed the pregnant woman’s mind becomes and the more her body is gripped by weakness or lethargy, the greater the possibility of a premature birth. This <term>Simantonnayana</term> <note>literally "parting of the hair upward"</note> sacrament is intended to remove that exhaustion and physical weakness.

*Simanta* means "the parting of the hair" (the line on the head) and *unnayana* means "lifting up"—that is, formally arranging the woman’s hair. The husband himself should gather two <term>Udumbara</term> <note>twigs of the cluster fig tree (Ficus racemosa), considered sacred and cooling</note> twigs on a single branch, along with a swastika and several other auspicious items, and tie the pregnant woman’s bun with a thread. Afterward, while using stalks of *kusha* grass, a bunch of leaves, and a thin piece of wood to lift the woman’s hair, the husband should explain the meaning of the mantras he must recite to his wife with great sweetness and gravity.

# 4. Jatakarma <note>The birth ceremony</note>

This is the fourth sacrament. This act must be performed after the child is born and before the umbilical cord is cut. At that time, while <term>chanting mantras</term>, one must write the syllable "OM" on the tongue of the <term>newborn</term> child using a golden rod dipped in a mixture of clarified butter <note>ghee</note> and honey. This increases the child's strength, vitality, and brilliance. This is the perspective of the religious scriptures; now listen to the perspective of medicine and science:

According to chemical processes <note>original: "yaugika prakriya" — referring here to the science of combining substances</note>, it is known that when two or more substances with different properties meet, they produce an entirely new quality. For example, when white lime and yellow turmeric meet, they create a new red color. There are many other such examples. Therefore, there is no objection or inconsistency in the idea that the mixture of clarified butter, honey, and gold used during this sacrament would similarly produce a new, distinct property. It is not a matter of surprise that this elemental power serves as a special benefit to the newborn child. Rather, according to the study of the properties of matter <note>original: "padarthatattva"</note>, it can be called a supreme elixir <note>original: "rasayana"</note> for the newborn’s body—providing strength and nourishment while removing imbalances of wind <note>vata</note> and bile <note>pitta</note>—

<vocab>Simantonnayana (Hair-parting ritual), Jatakarma (Birth ritual), Samskara (Sacrament), Garbhachyuti (Miscarriage), Garbhabhransha (Premature birth), Udumbara (Cluster fig), Sadyojata (Newborn), Mantroccharana (Chanting of mantras), Rasayana (Elixir/Alchemy), Padarthatattva (Science of the properties of matter)</vocab>

The author provides both a spiritual and a scientific-medicinal justification for these ancient practices.</summary>

...accepts. In our medical scriptures <note>original: "vaidyakashastra" — Ayurveda</note>, the qualities of the aforementioned ghee and other substances are written as follows:

**1. Cow’s Ghee** — Especially beneficial for the eyes, cooling, and a remedy for disturbances in the bodily humors of wind, bile, and phlegm <note>original: "vata, pitta, kapha"</note>. It increases reproductive vitality <note>original: "shukra"</note> and digestive fire <note>original: "agni"</note>, promotes strength and longevity, and nourishes the intellect and memory.
[Note: It is especially beneficial for the eyes, cooling, and destroys wind, bile, and phlegm. It increases reproductive essence, digestion, sweetness, transformation, memory, beauty, glow, vitality, and radiance. It supports the stability of the body, age, and strength; it is a rejuvenative, an appetizer, nourishes intellect and memory, stabilizes the body, relieves fatigue, and possesses many virtues. (These are the qualities of cow's ghee) — from the *Bhava Prakasha* <note>A classic 16th-century Ayurvedic text by Bhava Mishra</note>].

**2. Honey** — Cooling, mild, improves the sense of taste on the tongue, destroys the three defects <note>the "doshas" or bodily humors</note>, and relieves breathing difficulties, coughs, and similar ailments. <margin>It is cooling, soft, sweet, especially effective at healing wounds, drying, beneficial for the eyes, and a remedy for breath and cough. (These are the qualities of honey).</margin>

**Gold** — Sweet, astringent, pleasing to the heart, improves appearance and provides strength, beneficial to the eyes, increases bodily radiance and power, and purifies longevity, intellect, and speech. Even severe diseases like consumption <note>original: "kshaya" — tuberculosis</note> and insanity <note>original: "unmada"</note> are calmed by it. <margin>Gold is bitter, sweet, astringent, scraping, pleasing to the heart, a rejuvenative, and strengthening. It is beneficial for the eyes, bestows radiance, and is pure. For humans, it bestows longevity, intellect, strength, stability, and clarity of speech; it is called the supreme pacifier for those suffering from consumption, insanity, and similar diseases. — From the *Rajavallabha* <note>An Ayurvedic materia medica text</note>.</margin>

**Barley** — Astringent, sweet, strengthening, dry, heavy, cooling, and a remedy for urinary issues, excess fat, and bodily humors. In this manner, there are many purposes for rituals like the First Haircut <note>original: "Chudakarana"</note> and others, which we do not record here for fear of being too lengthy.

# Inquiry into the Proper Time for Marriage

First, in this section, it is written in this very *Gobhila Grihya Sutra*:

> After a student <note>original: "Brahmachari"</note> has studied the Veda and presented the final gift to the teacher, he should take a wife with the teacher's permission, provided she is not of the same clan. (1.4). She must not be a near-relative of the mother. (5). Chapter 3, Section 4.

This means that after a student has studied the Veda from beginning to end and given the ritual fee <note>original: "dakshina"</note> to the preceptor, he should marry a girl with the preceptor’s permission who does not belong to his own clan <note>original: "gotra"</note> and is not a close maternal relative <note>original: "sapinda" — those who share the same funeral cake offerings, usually within seven generations</note>. Furthermore:

> A girl who is no longer a "Nagnika" is best. (6). Chapter 3, Section 4.

In this context, the word "but" <note>original: "tu"</note> also signifies "even." An *Anagnika* <note>literally "one who is not naked"</note> is a girl whose menstrual cycle has already begun. As long as she can walk around without clothes <note>original: "nagna" or "ulanga"</note> without shame, she is called a *Nagnika* (a child); she who is different from that is an *Anagnika*—one who has reached puberty and youth. She alone is "**the best**" or the most praiseworthy. Only when a girl’s menstrual cycle has occurred is she ready for the "enjoyment of the fire" <note>referring to the consummation of the marriage and the domestic fire ritual</note>. Only then is the use of the mantra "Soma gave her to the Gandharva" <note>A Vedic wedding verse explaining that deities protect a girl before her husband does</note> appropriate; not otherwise...

<summary>This page details the Ayurvedic benefits of substances used in birth rituals and begins a legalistic inquiry into marriage, emphasizing that a bride should be from a different clan and physically mature.</summary>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header># Introduction #</header>

The praise of those who have reached their season <note>original: "rtumatyah" — girls who have attained puberty</note> for the performance of domestic rites. Therefore, even Manu says: "The husband receives the wife given by the gods, not by his own will (9.95)" <note>This implies that marriage is a divinely ordained duty rather than a pursuit of personal lust</note>. Thus, the result is that a girl who has attained her youth should be accepted, and one who has only just approached youth should not yet be married.

**Commentary:**—A girl who does not feel shame while playing in a naked state is called *Nagnika* <term>Nagnika: literally 'the naked one,' referring to a prepubescent child</term>. In contrast, a girl whose monthly season has manifested, and has thus attained youth, is called *Anagnika*. Only the *Anagnika* girl is considered best for marriage; if such a girl is not available at the proper time, then one whose youth has just begun <note>showing early signs of puberty</note> is also eligible to be wed.

If someone should raise an objection to this based on the following verses:

> At eight years old, she is *Gauri*; at nine years, she is *Rohini*.
> At ten years, she is called a *Kanya* (maiden), and beyond that, she is *Rajasvala* <note>original: "menstruating"</note>. || 1 ||
>
> The mother, the father, and the eldest brother as well—
> These three go to hell upon seeing the maiden menstruating [unmarried]. || 2 ||

That is to say: an eight-year-old is *Gauri*, a nine-year-old is *Rohini*, a ten-year-old is called a *Kanya*, and after that, she becomes *Rajasvala*. And if they see the maiden menstruating, the mother, father, and elder brother go to hell.

To the contrary, there are more robust and authoritative verses regarding *Gauri*, *Rohini*, etc., which provide the etymology of the word *Anagnika* as follows:

> "One should call a maiden *Nagnika* until she reaches her monthly course.
> One who has reached her season is *Anagnika*; one should give [in marriage] such an *Anagnika*. || 13 ||
>
> One who has not reached her menses is *Gauri*; upon reaching menses, she is *Rohini*.
> One without physical signs [of puberty] is a *Kanya*, and one without breasts is a *Nagnika*. || 18 ||
>
> When the signs of puberty appear, Soma enjoys the maiden;
> When the breasts develop, the Gandharvas; and with the menses, Agni is said to enjoy her. || 19 ||
>
> Therefore, one who lacks the signs of puberty, lacks menses, and lacks breast development—
> A maiden not yet 'enjoyed' by Soma and the others is not praised [for marriage]." || 20 ||

**That is to say:**—As long as a girl does not have her monthly period, she is called *Nagnika*. Therefore, one should only marry a girl who is *Anagnika* <note>meaning she has reached physical maturity</note>. || 17 || A girl who has not yet attained her monthly flow is called *Gauri*, and one in whose body the 'seed' <note>original: "rajas" — menstrual flow</note> has appeared is called *Rohini*. A girl who shows no signs of youth is called a *Kanya*, and one who is 'chest-less' (without breasts) is called *Nagnika*. || 18 ||

<note>Verse 19 refers to the traditional belief that three deities preside over a girl’s development before her human husband: Soma (the Moon) grants her coolness and purity when she matures, the Gandharvas (celestial musicians) grant her beauty and voice when her breasts develop, and Agni (Fire) grants her the power of the womb at menstruation.</note>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>## The Gobhila Grihya Sutra — [Investigation into the Time of Marriage]</header>

Soma enjoys the girl who shows signs of youth; Gandharva enjoys the one with developed breasts; and Agni enjoys the one who has reached menstruation || 18 || <note>This traditional verse suggests that a girl passes through the guardianship of various deities as she matures before being ready for a human husband.</note> Therefore, a girl who lacks the signs of youth, has no menstrual cycle, has undeveloped breasts, and has not yet passed through these stages of divine protection is not considered ideal for marriage. These are the words of the *Grihyasangraha* (2.97-98). Furthermore, the primary medical text, the *Sushruta Samhita*, states:

> "From the essence of food, the blood known as menses arises in women. It begins after the age of twelve and begins to decline at fifty." <note>original Sanskrit: *rasadeva striya raktam raja samjnam pravartate...* — Sushruta Samhita, Chapter 14</note>

This means that after twelve years, a girl’s menstrual cycle begins, and it starts to wane after fifty. Furthermore:

> "A wise father should count his daughter's seasons [menstrual cycles]. He should carefully protect the girl at home during her periods for a fixed number of days." || 1 ||

->*Sanskara Kaustubha*, Page 21, printed in Mumbai (Saka 1874 / 1952 CE)<-

This means that a father should count his daughter’s menstrual cycles from the very beginning. There is a regulation for how many cycles a girl must remain in the home; once she has reached that number of cycles, her marriage should then take place. It should never be done before this; on the contrary, if it happens later, it is even better.

Regarding the question of when a bride and groom should marry according to scripture and logic, the late and renowned scholar of Varanasi, the Great Teacher <note>original: *Mahamahopadhyaya* — a title for an eminent scholar</note> Pandit Ramamishra Shastri, writes the following in the preface of his book, *Investigation into the Time of Marriage*:

### PREFACE

At the present time, in various parts of India among those who profess to be followers of the Vedic religion and practices, the custom of marrying girls in mere infancy is a common one, and people think that if they do not conform to this custom they incur sin. But the truth is that the rule about the infant marriage of girls enjoined in the <term>Dharmashastras</term> <note>Dharmashastras: Ancient Indian law books and legal treatises on duty and social conduct.</note> is not what is called <term>nitya</term> (a fixed and obligatory duty, the non-performance of which is a sin) but <term>kamya</term> (optional and to be performed only through the desire of obtaining some particular benefit). The principal age for marriage is that of twelve and upwards as clearly declared by Manu; only it is necessary that the marriage ceremony should take place before the age of puberty is...

<summary>This page argues against child marriage by citing medical texts like the Sushruta Samhita and legal scholars who classify infant marriage as optional rather than a religious obligation. It establishes that marriage should ideally occur after the age of twelve once a girl has matured physically.</summary>
<keywords>marriage age, puberty, Sushruta Samhita, Dharmashastras, nitya, kamya, Ramamishra Shastri, Manu, menstruation, Vedic customs</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the introductory remarks by Pandit Rama Misra Sastri regarding the appropriate age for marriage, transitioning from an English appeal to a more philosophical discussion in Hindi regarding health and human goals.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
## Of the Gobhila Grihya Sutra — [Analysis of the Time for Marriage]

I entertain the hope that by means of this book people will come to know that marriage at the age of twelve or thirteen is not prohibited by the <term>Sastras</term> <note>Sacred Hindu scriptures or treatises.</note>, so long as it takes place before the period above indicated. Hence the marriage of mere infants is wrong. In any case, as appears from what I have stated above, males should not marry before eighteen, & in no rank of life should the wife join her husband till she is past the age of eleven.

My earnest prayer is that the princes & wealthy nobles of this country will exert themselves in this matter, and freely provide means for the circulation of this work throughout India. Then, if they follow the course therein advocated, will the inhabitants of <term>Bharatavarsha</term> <note>An ancient and traditional name for the Indian subcontinent.</note> become wiser, more powerful, energetic, and courageous, & better qualified to understand and take part in abstruse and difficult political concerns.

->A true friend of the Indian People.<-
**PANDIT RAMA MISRA SASTRI** **Banaras.**
7th December, 1890.

Are there any people in the world who do not desire the growth and prosperity of their lineage and clan? Or who do not wish for natural strength, vitality, bodily radiance, and physical beauty? I am firmly convinced that everyone in the world will agree with one voice that the desire for all these aforementioned rewards is natural to every thinking being. The only distinction is that wise people, as soon as they desire a result, consider the means to achieve it; they find a way to obtain it and reach success. On the other hand, those who are unintelligent, lazy, or cursed by fate spend their lives merely wishing for the rewards until the very end, <unclear>sin</unclear> without ever even considering the nature of the means. Everyone will also agree that it is difficult to repair something that is already broken, particularly the body—which is the primary vehicle for achieving the four aims of human life (<term>Purushartha</term> <note>The four traditional goals of human life: Dharma (duty/ethics), Artha (prosperity), Kama (pleasure), and Moksha (liberation).</note>). By protecting the body, all four goals can be realized, but by neglecting it, all four goals sink into the abyss.

---

<margin>* If people cannot all at once conform, as I think they should conform to the directions of <term>Manu</term> and <term>Susruta</term> <note>Manu refers to the Lawgiver of the Manusmriti; Susruta is the ancient father of Indian surgery and medicine.</note>, and make the age of twelve the earliest.</margin>

<summary>Pandit Rama Misra Sastri argues against infant marriage, advocating for minimum ages of eighteen for men and twelve for women to ensure national vitality and the fulfillment of life's four primary spiritual and material goals.</summary>
