<page-num>21</page-num>
### Introduction

So, it is appropriate to consider here: what object is the body? In response to this, theists, atheists, physicians, traditional healers, doctors, followers of the <term>Madhyi</term> <note>Likely referring to a specific local sect or a misprint for a general group.</note> sect, and people of all faiths will agree with one voice that it is a wondrous mass of flesh. It is formed from the <term>reproductive elements</term> <note>original: "shukra and shonita," literally semen and blood.</note> of the mother and father, nourished by various foods and drinks, and <term>composed of the five elements</term> <note>earth, water, fire, air, and ether; original: "pañcabhautika"</note>, subject to both growth and decay. To provide an example of its complexity, nothing can be found on the surface of the earth, nor even—who could say—in the celestial realms. Within this physical frame is etched the image of the detachment found in the hearts of great renouncers like <term>Shuka and Vamadeva</term> <note>Famous sages in Hindu tradition known for their extreme asceticism and wisdom from birth.</note>. It is the vessel for the devotion of figures like Prahlada and Parashara. Within it sits the extraordinary fundamental essence that governs the conduct and character of heroes like Bhima, the famous Arjuna, various masters of science, the charitable Karna, and selfless people like <term>Dadhichi</term> <note>A sage who sacrificed his own bones to forge a weapon for the gods to defeat evil.</note>. To what extent can this be described? It is the ultimate primal seed of the <term>Great Banyan Tree of Worldly Existence</term> <note>original: "saṃsārasvarūpa mahāvaṭavṛkṣa"</note>. Let this satisfy you, and know also that compared to this body, all the riches of the world are utterly insignificant. The empire of emperors, the sovereignty of kings, the wisdom of the learned, and the prowess of the powerful—all these are but a small play of its power.

Having settled upon this truth, I am today prepared to introduce you to how the body can be made excellent. Dear people of <term>Bharatavarsha</term> <note>The traditional Sanskrit name for India.</note>! Take heed: as long as the seed is not good, the land will never yield excellent fruit. Has a superior fruit ever been produced from a raw, immature seed? You marry off your small children, and while still in an immature state, these children fall into the terrible calamity of sexual relations. Everyone knows that for every object, there is a <term>prior state</term> <note>original: "pūrvāvasthā"</note> and a <term>subsequent state</term> <note>original: "uttarāvasthā"</note>; similarly, there is a <term>stage of arrival</term> <note>original: "āgamāvasthā," referring to the period of growth and accumulation</note> and a <term>stage of loss</term> <note>original: "apāyāvasthā," referring to the period of decline</note>.

Among these, childhood is the "prior state" of a human being, and this very stage is—

The "stage of arrival" for strength, <term>vital energy</term> <note>original: "vīrya," often referring specifically to reproductive fluids as the source of vitality</note>, brilliance, intellect, physical beauty, and nourishment. If one begins to spend these resources during this very stage of accumulation, what a wretched state they will fall into! You can consider this for yourselves. If a pond begins to drain away at the very moment the water starts flowing into it, that pond can never be filled, no matter how forcefully the water arrives.

<summary>The author defines the human body as a sacred vessel of vitality and virtue, arguing that childhood is a critical stage for accumulating strength (vīrya) which is wasted by premature marriage and sexual activity.</summary>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>## Gobhila-Grihya-Sutra: [An Inquiry into the Timing of Marriage]</header>

...Why shouldn't it be so? This single example is sufficient to show what kind of harm you suffer from the evil custom of child marriage.

On this occasion, many enemies of the nation and destroyers of their own lineages will say, "What if marriage happens in childhood? Physical intimacy only occurs at the appropriate time." To this, we say it is a matter of great misfortune to take someone's daughter in vain, make her a daughter-in-law, and—after symbolically cutting off her hands and feet—bind her before the blazing fire of <term>child-widowhood</term> <note>original: "balya-vaidhavya"; the author highlights the permanence of widowhood for young girls in this era.</note> with the extremely harsh and inescapable iron chain of marriage. From this chain, no one—not the mother’s family, the father’s family, nor brothers or friends—can release the girl. Yes, the modern <term>Reformers</term> <note>original: "Samajis," likely referring to the Arya Samaj or Brahmo Samaj.</note>, the traditional <term>Vedantists</term> <note>original: "Brahmavadi," those who follow the path of Brahman or the Vedas.</note>, and others might be able to liberate her through the power of divine strength, though they may only manage to stir up a storm of severe scandal. But when such terrible disaster is inherent in child marriage, it is a great misfortune that even wise men should ever practice it.

Many people will close their eyes to this and say, "Death is inevitable at every stage of life in this world. If a young man marries, is there no fear of widowhood? Happiness and sorrow are under God's control alone. We have seen many children who married in extreme childhood and remained well throughout their lives, while many young men have journeyed to the <term>Abode of Death</term> <note>original: "Yam Mandir," the temple or palace of Yama, the god of death.</note> within a month of marriage, leaving behind their young brides. Therefore, blaming child marriage only suits those who believe in a godless world." 

To this, without engaging in much scriptural debate or deliberation, we say only this: the three great gentlemen—<term>Ranjit Singh</term>, <term>Shivaji</term>, and <term>Haider Ali</term> <note>Respectively: the founder of the Sikh Empire, the founder of the Maratha Empire, and the ruler of Mysore. All were famous for their military and political prowess despite lacking formal literacy.</note>—never even learned to write their names, yet they became very great men. This is a historical fact that everyone must accept. So, following your logic, do not teach anyone in your family to read or write; instead, give them lessons in gambling and <term>dacoity</term> <note>original: "dakaiti," meaning banditry or armed robbery.</note>. Because in all these activities, many people have become very wealthy and famous—especially Haider Ali, who gained his fame and national wealth mostly through the power of dishonesty, and Shivaji through dacoity. 

So now, try to become great by relying on dishonesty and banditry. If by mistake you should say, "Success or failure is in God's hands, but a human should make good efforts," then...

The author argues that while outliers exist, human effort must be directed toward logical and moral social practices.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from page 22, the author continues his polemic against child marriage. He employs a famous historical anecdote about Alexander the Great to distinguish between destiny and moral choice.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>## Preface</header>

You have come around to our way of thinking; my statement is also that it is proper for a human being to make a good effort toward what is right. Now, there is a famous proverb: "Success is a matter of luck" <note>original: "Bane ki baat hai"; a common Hindi saying implying that things happen as they are destined to.</note>. Once, Alexander the Great captured a certain pirate and asked him, "Why do you do such work?" In response, the pirate said, "You loot countries with a vast army, many ships, and grand resources, while I do the same work with meager resources and a few men. But 'it is a matter of luck'—you are a great plunderer, yet people recognize and respect you as a Great Emperor, while they call me a dacoit." From this illustration, one might admit that "success is a matter of luck," but everyone must also admit that theft and dacoity are evil, and good deeds are indeed good.

Therefore, you should also consider that perhaps, in some rare instance, a person's body might remain healthy despite a <term>child marriage</term> <note>original: "balya-vivah"; marriage performed before the age of puberty.</note> due to some other circumstance. However, it must certainly be accepted that child marriage is the cause of disaster, while marriage in <term>adulthood</term> <note>original: "tarunya"; refers to the stage of full physical development or youth.</note> is entirely appropriate and the cause of physical happiness and strength.

Now, you must decide for yourself: in all the countries of the world, deaths among older people are fewer compared to deaths among children. As age increases, the body becomes more durable. The stages of a mango tree—from the time the blossoms appear until the fruit is fully ripened—serve as a perfect illustration of this. The number of blossoms that fall is far greater than the number of small green mangoes that fall; and of the small mangoes that fall, even fewer reach the stage of fully ripened mangoes. Such is the condition of human offspring as well.

So, why do you destroy a young girl by binding her to a young boy (who does not even have the sense of how to wear a <term>dhoti</term> <note>a traditional wrapped garment; the author implies the boy is so young he cannot even dress himself</note>) just out of a futile desire to see the faces of a grandson or a daughter's son? Instead, keep her in a state of <term>Brahmacharya</term> <note>original: "brahmacharya"; here referring to a period of celibate study and disciplined education.</note> and teach her knowledge, so that she may become inseparable from the virtues of both this world and the next, becoming an "ornament of your lineage" and not a burden upon the world.

On this point, some small-minded people, who are stubborn about child marriage, even go so far as to say: "This is not the time for celibacy and study. In this <term>Iron Age</term> <note>original: "Kalyug" or "Kalikala"; in Hindu cosmology, the final, most decadent age of the world cycle.</note>, boys begin searching for wives while they are still children. For this reason, it is proper to marry them off in childhood; otherwise, they will be ruined." 

May stones fall upon such an intellect! Parents are unable to help even one boy maintain his disciplined studenthood, yet after marriage, when they become two, and every year they begin to have three, four, or five...

<summary>The author uses analogies of Alexander the Great and a mango tree to argue that while "luck" exists, child marriage is a statistically and morally inferior choice compared to adult marriage. He refutes the "Kaliyuga" excuse that children are naturally predisposed to vice and argues for education over early marriage.</summary>

<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>### Gobhila Grihya Sutra — [Inquiry into the Three-Night Period]</header>

...then how will they be able to provide for their upbringing and maintenance! It is a great tragedy that those who cannot ensure their own child’s observance of <term>celibacy</term> <note>original: "brahmacharya"; the stage of life dedicated to study and self-restraint.</note> do not even consider how they will support a brood of grandchildren. 

A point to be noted here is that I am not striving to preach <term>celibacy</term> until the ages of thirty or twenty-five; my only assertion is that you should marry your children off only when their youth has become firmly established in the body and their bones have reached maturity, according to the climate of their specific region and their financial capacity for food and clothing. By doing so, the <term>fear of widowhood</term> <note>original: "vaidhavy-bhay"; a major social concern as child widows faced harsh lives.</note> becomes relatively minimal, the children's limbs become strong, and their future offspring will also be born healthy and <term>sturdy-bodied</term> <note>original: "dridhanga".</note>. 

However, in every country, a man should never have relations with a woman before the age of eighteen. This is a rule for all lands and all times; it should never be violated. **"The woman at thirteen, the man at eighteen"** <note>original: "tiriya terah marad atharah"</note>—this proverb has been famous from ancient times down to the common folk; remember it. Furthermore, for women as well, contact with a man before the onset of youth is a cause of harm and is entirely contrary to the <term>sacred law</term> <note>original: "dharmashastra".</note> and <term>medical science</term> <note>original: "vaidyakshastra".</note>. 

We have written this entire account extensively in Sanskrit; a true understanding will be gained only by examining that. Due to the current practice of marrying off very young children, the world's health is declining, and the population is becoming short-lived, devoid of memory, poor, and stricken by calamity—all these matters we have described in Sanskrit. Marrying off children by fixing a price in wealth, or giving away a daughter for money, or marrying in old age, or marrying a girl who is older than the groom—all these are also forbidden in the scriptures; all of this has been established.

Now, at this juncture, many people (whose influence in society is meager) thoughtlessly desire to seek refuge in laws against child marriage, and by tainting the sacred Vedic marriage rites, they display their own <term>weakness of heart</term>. But the Government is not such that it would display its own <term>weakness</term>; it is instead vast, tireless, and compassionate, characterized by a very noble policy of trustworthy justice. For this reason, those who are opposed to our religion, or those who are ignorantly and falsely arrogant toward our sacred and eternal Vedic—

<page-num>२५</page-num>
<header>## Preface</header>

We do not have even an atom of fear regarding those locals or foreigners who wish to strike a blow against our <term>faith</term> <note>original: "dharma"; here referring to the socio-religious duty and order.</note> by taking false <term>legal representation</term> <note>original: "vakalatanama"; a document authorizing a lawyer to act on one's behalf. The author suggests critics are misrepresenting Indian traditions to the British authorities.</note> to England. Nevertheless, it is necessary for us to publicly manage our social affairs, such as marriage and other rites, from our own side. 

In our society, we have social teachers ranging from the most learned scholars of the cities to the simple <term>village priests</term> <note>original: "padha purohit".</note> of the countryside. Generally, they provide sound advice, yet they do not make excessive efforts; they do not travel to England, nor do they crown themselves as "patriots" by engaging in various correspondences. Their only shortcoming is that they do not make a public display of their efforts, nor do they show the arrogance of being social leaders—yet they continue to do the work regardless. Truly, they have no other fault. 

Every day, wise <term>Brahmins</term> <note>the priestly and intellectual class.</note> are removing <term>child marriage</term>, <term>infant marriage</term>, and <term>polygamy</term> <note>original: "bahu-vivah".</note> from their own homes and from the <term>four-fold social order</term> <note>original: "chaturvarnya"; the traditional Vedic system of four social classes.</note> that follows the Vedas. They are also gradually correcting whatever other flaws are found in society. Consummation with an infant girl is simply impossible within the traditional Vedic community, because even in the homes of the poorest and most uneducated, a <term>Flower Festival</term> <note>original: "Pushpotsava" or "Phulchauki".</note> is celebrated when a girl reaches her first menses. This "flower" <note>a metaphor for menstruation.</note> only occurs once a woman has passed through childhood. This matter is entirely well-regulated; after the onset of menses, and following a purification ceremony at an auspicious time, the <term>Conception Rite</term> <note>original: "Garbhadhan Sanskar"; the first of the sixteen traditional sacraments, intended to sanctify the act of procreation.</note> is performed. How, then, could there be any possibility of contact with an infant girl among our people? Such a possibility exists only among those for whom physical union is like that of animals, driven solely by the pursuit of the senses. In our Vedic path, this most sacred union is conducted through extensive rituals and the chanting of Vedic hymns.

As for the current state of child marriage, there are several communities in our country where men only marry once they have reached adulthood. For instance, in the <term>land of Mithila</term> <note>a region in modern-day Bihar and Nepal famous for its scholarship.</note>, no man of the Maithil community marries before the age of twenty-one (in fact, it often happens at thirty, thirty-five, or even forty, which we do not consider ideal given the circumstances). It is a natural fact that when a man of a mature age is married in a region, there can be no union with an infant girl. Having spoken of the great, knowledge-centered land of Mithila, there is also the <term>land of Kanyakubja</term> <note>the region around Kannauj in Uttar Pradesh.</note>. Forget the talk of infant marriage there; even "aged maidens" <note>original: "vriddha kumari"; women who remain unmarried until a much later age.</note> are married there...

<summary>The author defends traditional Vedic marriage practices against foreign critics, arguing that internal reforms are already abolishing child marriage and that puberty rites like the Phulchauki ensure consummation only occurs at maturity.</summary>

<page-num>२६</page-num>

-> ## Gobhila Grhyasutra—[Inquiry into the Timing of Marriage] <-

The people of Kanyakubja <note>The region around modern-day Kannauj in Uttar Pradesh</note> are also taking measures, as are others, and it is hoped that we will soon be able to eliminate this calamity <note>referring to the "anarth" or misfortune of improper marriage ages mentioned on the previous page</note>. It is a law of God that where girls are married at an older age, the grooms generally cannot be younger than them; thus, in the Kanyakubja region, the timing of marriage is generally appropriate.

As for Rajputana <note>Modern-day Rajasthan</note>, fifty years ago, among all social classes, girls of older age were married to older grooms. However, since the era of the British government’s rule began, and the merchants of that region started traveling to countries like Calcutta, Bombay, Madras, China, and Sri Lanka for trade and becoming wealthy, they have been overtaken by <insert>(1) child marriage</insert>. In fact, they have also been oppressed by the deep darkness of <insert>(2) marriage to the elderly</insert> <note>often referring to the practice of marrying young girls to much older men</note> and the marriage of older girls to younger boys. The uneducated Brahmins and others of that region are also not far from this misconduct. Yet, by the mercy of God, this misconduct does not exist among the Rajput caste, and it is hoped they will remain free from it. Because of the proximity to Rajputana, the fire of child marriage also spread to the districts of Delhi and the regions near Braj <note>The cultural region around Mathura and Vrindavan</note>. However, thanks are due to the <term>Arya Samaj</term> <note>A Hindu reform movement founded by Swami Dayananda Saraswati in 1875 that advocated for Vedic values and social reform</note> assembly of India, for it has taken excellent measures to stop this, which are proving successful. In Punjab, too, marriage used to occur only when the bride and groom reached youth, and even now it does not happen in extreme infancy; however, some customs have deteriorated. Now, the assembly of learned Brahmins has attempted to reform this through their heroic preaching, and social corrections will continue daily, for the nobles of that region also have a special focus on this matter.

Regarding this country, we pray to God that it may undergo its own <term>virtuous reform</term> <note>original: "saattvik sanshodhan"; reform led by goodness and purity</note> while maintaining the boundaries of the <term>Dharma Shastras</term> <note>Ancient Sanskrit texts on law, duty, and social conduct</note>. It is hoped that the Great Assembly will also follow this path. The same must be said regarding those districts of Madras and Bombay where the knowledge of Sanskrit is in a state of decay, and in its place, new learning and restless enthusiasm are at their height.

Now, concerning the lands across the Narmada and Gujarat: their condition these days is such that, on one hand, they feel contempt at the very name of the Dharma Shastras. On the other hand, there are those who know that not marrying a girl of seven or eight years causes neither religious loss nor social harm. However, out of stubbornness and for the sake of reputation, they perceive <unclear>aesthetic</unclear> defects <note>original: "nepath-dosh"; likely referring to concerns about outward social appearance or ceremonial protocol</unclear> in certain <unclear>types</unclear> of marriages, though these are merely in name.

<header># Preface ||</header>

...are considered a part of the social body. Although across the Narmada River, the marriage of boys usually occurs at an appropriate time and not within eighteen or twenty years <note>The author likely means they wait until after the age of 18 or 20</note>, yet even there, it is essential to pay attention to the fact that the age of girls is being increased to make things right.

Here, I feel great distress seeing the stubbornness of both factions—those who only want the pomp and show of a public wedding, and those who, being "slaves to old tracks" <note>An idiom for those who follow tradition blindly</note> without understanding the true purpose of the scriptures, insist fruitlessly on the marriage of very young girls. I must say that if these two factions worked together, it would be like "gold and fragrance" <note>A classic idiom (Sona aur Sugandh) meaning a perfect combination of qualities</note>. To this end, if a Panchayat <note>a local community council</note> were established in every town, village, and region to determine the proper time for marriage <insert>(3)</insert> according to the Sacred Law <term>Dharmashastra</term> and the climate <note>literally: "water and air"</note> of the region, then the current lack of strength, vigor, valor, sharp intellect, and the loss of both new scriptural and worldly imaginative power in our country—all caused by child marriage—would vanish entirely, along with the various diseases of unknown names that have arisen.

It is hoped that our fellow countrymen and the assembly of traditional scholars <note>the Pandita-mandali</note> (whom people in India still hold in high regard today) will consider this with dignity. May they abandon envy and darkness, cease their stubbornness, and perform their duties with truth.

### ## Inquiry into the Guru's Mantra ||

Before inquiring into the Guru’s Mantra, one must consider who is called a "Guru." The definition of this word "Guru" has been provided by our ancient lawgivers as follows:

> The Brahmin who performs the rites from conception <note>original: niseka</note> onwards in accordance with the law and supports the child with food is called a Guru. <note>original Sanskrit: "niṣekādīni karmāṇi ya: karoti yathāvidhi || sambhāvayati cānnena sa vipro guru rucyate || 142 ||" from Manusmriti 2.142</note>

Furthermore:
> A father merely gives birth to a man, O best of men! But the Teacher <term>Acharya</term> imparts wisdom; therefore, he is called a Guru. <note>original Sanskrit: "pitā hyenaṃ janayati puruṣaṃ puruṣarṣabha ! || prajñāṃ dadāti ācāryastasmātsa gururucyate ||" Valmiki Ramayana 1.11</note>

---

<note>(1) In addition to this, they possess all the qualities of the merchant class <term>Vaishya</term>, but "where there are roses, there are thorns."</note>
<note>(2) During the days of Holi, where there is a gathering of Marwari merchants and Brahmins, they sing this to the beat of the tambourine <term>Daf</term>: "(Kona ne parvathe jevnon soy hai)" <note>A regional dialect phrase likely referring to festive dining or social gathering</note>.</note>
<note>(3) As the determination of the proper time was settled earlier in this book before the section on the seasons, it only remains to be adjusted according to the specific region.</note>
<note>(4) An Acharya is so called because he gathers <note>ācinoti</note> the meaning of the scriptures and establishes the student in conduct. He practices it himself, hence he is proclaimed an Acharya. (From Sāyaṇa's commentary on the Aitareya Aranyaka, Ch. 2; also cited in Shankha Smriti and Apastamba Dharmasutra). He is the one from whom one learns the Dharma (duty/law); he gives a second birth through knowledge.</note>

<page-num>२८</page-num>

<header># Gobhila Grihya Sutra - [The Gayatri Mantra] ##</header>

Furthermore—there are three persons who are considered Gurus <note>spiritual teachers or elders</note> for a man: the mother, the father, and the Teacher <term>Acharya</term>. || 39 || *Vishnu Smriti* <note>An ancient legal and ethical text of Hinduism</note> ||

Furthermore—Why is he called an *Acharya*? Because he imparts proper conduct <term>Achara</term>, or because he gathers the meaning of the scriptures, or because he develops the intellect.

->Nirukta, Chapter 1, Section 3, Verse 2<-
<note>The Nirukta is an ancient treatise on etymology and philology.</note>

This means—by performing the sacraments like the rite of conception <term>Garbhadhana</term> and protecting the son, the father is also called a Guru. Although the mother and father are indeed the child's first gurus, the *Acharya* is the primary Guru because he teaches the Vedas and other sciences || 152 || The father gives birth to the son, but the *Acharya* gives him the intellect by which the disciple gains the power to discriminate between truth and falsehood—this is why only the *Acharya* is truly called the Guru || 3 || A man has three gurus—mother, father, and teacher || 35 || Now hear the opinion of the author of the *Nirukta*. An *Acharya* is one who, being himself accomplished in all sciences, teaches humans the most excellent conduct and makes them accomplished in all aims of life || Through these and many other proofs, it is established that only the *Acharyas* are the true Gurus. Observing ancient history also confirms that in former times in India <note>Bharatvarsha</note>, Brahmins were the priests and teachers who initiated students into the Vedic Guru-mantra, the Gayatri. This is proved by the following evidence:

> One should immediately teach the Gayatri to a Brahmin, for the scripture says "the Brahmin is of the nature of Agni <note>the fire deity</note>." || 7 ||
> The Trishtubh meter to a member of the royal class <term>Kshatriya</term>. || 8 || The Jagati meter to a merchant or farmer <term>Vaishya</term>. || 9 || Or the Gayatri to everyone. || 10 || 3 ||

> **Harihara’s Commentary**—One should immediately teach or speak the Gayatri to a Brahmin. Why? Because the scripture says "the Brahmin is indeed of the nature of Agni." This is a statement from the Vedas. The Trishtubh is for the royal class, the Jagati for the merchant class, or the Gayatri for all. The "Trishtubh" refers to the Savitri <note>a prayer to the sun</note> composed in the Trishtubh meter. The "Jagati" refers to the Savitri composed in the Jagati meter for the Vaishya. It is implied from the context that one should "teach" these. Or, one may teach the Gayatri to all—Brahmins, Kshatriyas, and Vaishyas alike—meaning the Savitri prayer in the Gayatri meter, which is the famous verse beginning with "Tat Savitur" found in all branches of the Vedas. || *Paraskara Grihya Sutra* <note>A manual of domestic rituals</note>.

This means—one should teach the Gayatri to a Brahmin, the Trishtubh to a Kshatriya, and the Jagati to a Vaishya. Alternatively, one may teach the Gayatri alone to all three classes. This latter option (stated in sutra 10) is in accordance with all branches of the Vedas. Similarly, in the *Gobhila Grihya Sutra*, the Gayatri mantra is prescribed for everyone.

"Now he approaches him and says: 'Sir, please teach me the Savitri prayer...'" || 38 || Of that...

<header># Introduction #</header>

He recites fifteen verses <note>original: "अन्वाह पञ्चोद्धैदेश ऋचः"</note> and the Great Mystical Utterances <term>Mahavyahriti: the sacred sounds Bhur, Bhuvah, Svah</term> ending with the syllable Om. || 39 || 4 ||
->Gobhila Prapathaka 2, Khanda 37<-

**Explanation:** (At the time of the initiation ceremony <term>Upanayana</term>) After this, the boy should stand before the teacher <term>Guru</term> with folded hands and humbly pray: "O Teacher! Please teach me the Vedas and instruct me in the *Gayatri* <note>the most sacred prayer of the Vedas</note>." || 39 || In this manner, as the boy begins his Vedic studies, the Teacher should instruct him in the *Savitri* mantra <note>another name for the Gayatri</note>, one quarter-verse <term>charana</term> at a time. || 39 || 40 ||

It is not written here that different mantras, such as the *Gayatri* or *Trishtup* <note>different poetic meters</note>, should be taught to the different classes <term>Varnas</term> like Brahmins and others; rather, the instruction of the single *Gayatri Savitri* is prescribed for everyone.

### Should a woman also become a disciple of a teacher, just as a man does?

It is written in the *Gobhila Grihya Sutra* <note>a manual of domestic rituals</note> that for women, the husband alone is the teacher <term>Guru</term>, and no other. In fact, all ancient and modern scriptures forbid others, stating "The husband alone is the guru of women" <note>original: "पतिरेको गुरुस्त्रीणां"</note>. This means that whatever worldly or spiritual tasks are to be done, she should perform them all according to the instruction of her husband. A woman is restricted from going on pilgrimages or performing fasts and vows independently; her primary duty is solely to serve her husband.

> Following the mantra, she greets the Teacher by reciting her lineage. || 92 || Gobhila Prapathaka 2, Khanda 38 ||

<gloss>The "addressed one" <note>original: 'अनुमन्त्रिता'</note> refers to the bride. By reciting the "lineage" <note>original: 'गोत्रेण'</note>, she speaks the family name she has acquired from her husband and "greets" <note>original: 'अभिवादयते'</note> the "Teacher" <note>original: 'गुरुं'</note>, who is her husband. || 92 ||</gloss>

The wife should speak the sentence: "I, of such-and-such lineage and such-and-such name, greet you (the husband)," and she should offer her respects by touching his feet. According to the English custom, one says "Good morning" <note>transliterated in the original as 'गुडमौर्निङ्ग'</note> (she should not merely say *Namaste*). There is evidence of this same practice in various other domestic and legal scriptures <term>Grihyasutras and Dharmasutras</term>.

### || What used to happen to the cow during the Honey-Offering? ||

The scriptures state that when a great person, such as a Teacher <term>Acharya</term> or a priest <term>Ritvik</term>, arrives, they should be honored with the Honey-Offering <term>Madhuparka-vidhi: a ritual reception for honored guests</term>. History records that in ancient times, when the Aryan faith was at its true peak, guests were indeed worshipped according to this rite. Today, this practice has become so unfamiliar that various doubts have begun to arise regarding it. The items include: 1. A seat <term>Vishtara</term>, 2. Water for washing the feet <term>Padya</term>, 3. An offering of water <term>Arghya</term>, 4...

\* "A king, a priest, a graduate, a teacher, a father-in-law, and a maternal uncle should be honored with the Honey-Offering <term>Madhuparka</term> when they visit after the passing of a year." - *Manu Smriti*, Chapter 3, Verse 118.

<meta>Continuing from page 28, where the author discussed the initiation of students and the domestic role of women, the text now transitions to the specific rituals of hospitality found in the Grihya Sutras, focusing on the Madhuparka ceremony and the historical debate regarding the treatment of animals during guest reception.</meta>

<page-num>३२</page-num>
<header>The Gobhila Grihya Mantra - [The Honey-Mixture Section]</header>

# The Gobhila Grihya Mantra

The water for rinsing the mouth <term>Achamana</term>, the honey-mixture <term>Madhuparka: a ceremonial offering of honey, curd, and ghee</term>, and a cow—these six items were traditionally offered for the respectful reception of "great souls" <note>venerable guests or sages</note> who arrived at the home. These six items are: (1) a seat <term>Asana</term> for sitting, (2) water for washing the feet <term>Padya</term>, (3) water for washing the hands <term>Arghya</term>, (4) water for purifying the mouth <term>Achamana</term>, (5) the "honey-mixture" <term>Madhuparka</term> made of yogurt, clarified butter, and honey for consumption, and (6) a cow <term>Gau</term> as a gift. When all six of these are present, the worship of the guest is considered complete in all its parts.

Now, a question arises here: what happened to the cow that was given during the <term>Madhuparka</term>? Was this cow simply given as a gift to the arriving Mahatma, or was the cow slaughtered and its meat fed to the arrived guest? 

When we look at the sentences of the Vedas, the religious laws <term>Dharma Shastras</term> of the sages, the customs of the ritual manuals <term>Kalpa Sutras</term>, and the examples cited by famous scholars, it appears that both things used to happen. This is because in the section regarding the "Guest Sacrifice" <term>Atithyeshti</term> in the *Brahmana* scripture <note>(2) referring to the Shatapatha Brahmana</note>, it is written: "When a human king or any other venerable man arrives at one’s home, people would honor him by slaughtering either a large ox <term>Ukshan</term> or a barren cow <term>Vasha</term>." 

Similarly, it is written in the traditional law codes <term>Smriti</term> (3) that one should sacrifice either a large bull or a large goat for a learned scholar <term>Shrotriya</term> who has arrived. The author of the ritual manuals, Ashvalayana, also writes that if the arriving scholar wishes for the cow to be slaughtered, he should chant this mantra: "My sin is slain, slain is my sin" <note>(4) original: "हनो मे पाप्मा पाप्मा मे हतः"</note>—and then give the command for the slaughter by saying, "Yes, do it!" 

Furthermore, it is written in the *Latyayana Shrauta Sutra* of the Sama Veda that when the cow is brought and stood before him, the guest should speak this sentence: "Do it," etc. From these various statements, it appears certain that at the time of <term>Madhuparka</term>, a cow was indeed slaughtered. 

If one were to argue that the terms like "Do it" <note>original: "कुरुत"</note> or "He will cause it to be done" <note>original: "कारयिष्यन्"</note> mentioned in the ritual manuals (5) have some other meaning and do not refer to slaughter, then consider what Ashvalayana writes further: (6) "There is no Madhuparka without meat." Since no other animal's meat is prescribed in that context other than the cow, it must be understood that it refers to the cow. Based on such evidence, it appears that cow slaughter occurred during the <term>Madhuparka</term> ceremony. However, those same—

---

(1) One should offer the seat, the water for feet, the water for hands, the water for rinsing, the honey-mixture, and the cow, announcing each one. <note>original: "विष्टर पाद्यमर्घ्यमाचमनीयं मधुपर्कं गौनितिप्रत्येयं निवेदयेत्" — Ashvalayana Grihya Sutra 1.24.7</note>
(2) Just as when a human king or another venerable person arrives, they slaughter an ox or a barren cow... <note>original: "तद्यथैवायें मनुषराज आगतेऽम्यस्मिन् वअर्हन्युक्षणं वैहन् वशंवाहनं" — Shatapatha Brahmana</note>
(3) One should offer a large ox or a large goat to a learned scholar. <note>original: "महाक्षं वा महानं वा श्रोत्रियायपप्रपयत्" — Yajnavalkya Smriti</note>
(4) While chanting "My sin is slain, slain is my sin," he should say "Do it" to the one who is to perform the act. <note>original: "हनोमे पाप्मा पाप्मा मे हतइति जपिनं कुरुतेति ब्रूयात्। कारयिष्यन्" — Ashvalayana Grihya Sutra 1.24.38</note>
(5) "Do it" is the command for the future act... <note>original: "कुरुतेति भविष्यानाज्ञा मयान्" — Latyayana Shrauta Sutra</note>
(6) There is no Madhuparka without meat. <note>original: "नामास मधुपर्क" — Ashvalayana Grihya Sutra 1.24.26</note>

<summary>This page discusses the ancient Vedic ritual of Madhuparka (honoring a guest) and provides scriptural evidence from the Brahmana and Grihya Sutras suggesting that cow slaughter was historically a part of this hospitality rite.</summary>
<keywords>Madhuparka, cow slaughter, hospitality, Ashvalayana Grihya Sutra, Shatapatha Brahmana, guest reception, Vedic rituals, Shrotriya</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the Madhuparka (honey-mixture) ceremony. While the previous page established that a cow was one of the six traditional offerings for a guest, this page examines the conflicting scriptural evidence regarding whether that cow was slaughtered for a feast or set free. The author navigates the tension between Vedic commands for non-violence and specific ritual instructions for animal sacrifice.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>

->## Introduction<-

Evidence from the Vedas <term>Shruti</term>, the Law Codes <term>Smriti</term>, and the ritual manuals <term>Kalpasutras</term> also proves that during the "honey-mixture" <term>Madhuparka</term> ceremony, the slaughter of a cow <note>original: gobadh</note> did **not** always take place. It is written in the Vedas (1) that "This cow is the mother of the **Rudra** deities; she is the daughter of the **Vasu** deities; she is the sister of the **Aditya** deities and is the very source of the nectar of immortality <note>referring to milk</note>. Therefore, I have repeatedly said to those who seek knowledge: do not kill this innocent cow, who is not to be slain," and so on. 

Furthermore, it is written in the Law Codes (2) that those who kill someone who has sought refuge, or who kill the "Mother of the World" (the cow), are considered great sinners. Additionally, even where the authors of the ritual manuals <term>Kalpasutras</term> describe the option for slaughter (3), they mention that when the mantra "Mother of the Rudras..." is recited, the guest may say: "Aha! Let her go; let her graze on the grass." The sage **Latyayana** has expressed the same view (4). 

By looking at the statements from both sides, the established conclusion is that **both** practices occurred: the slaughter of the cow and its release <term>Utsarjana</term>. Although in various places the cow is referred to as "one not to be killed" <term>Aghanya</term> and "the infinite/indivisible" <term>Aditi</term>—which confirms the Vedic view that the cow is inviolable—yet, in the previously mentioned passages from the *Brahmanas* <note>scholarly commentaries on the Vedas</note> such as "Just as others...", the ritual for cow slaughter is clearly prescribed. In such cases, the fact that the cow has names suggesting it shouldn't be killed is treated as a general sentiment. This is similar to how the Vedas say, "Do not cause violence to any living being" <note>a general rule</note>, yet also state "One should sacrifice an animal to Agni and Soma" <note>a specific ritual rule</note>, thereby prescribing animal violence for a specific sacrifice.

Scholars of logic and interpretation <term>Mimansakas</term> view the former Vedic statement as a "general rule" and the latter as the "specific" or "actual" instruction. By prioritizing this special rule and disregarding the general rule of non-violence, those who advocate for ritual slaughter believe that, based on the strength of the Vedic passages, the slaughtering side is the correct one. Consequently, in their view, the slaughter of a cow during the <term>Madhuparka</term> ceremony was not considered improper. While the option to release the animal <term>Utsarjana</term> is also written in the Vedas, those who were meat-eaters did not prefer this "release option." They considered the serving and eating of meat to be the highest form of hospitality for a guest. Therefore, among meat-eaters, where both the guest and the host were consumers of meat, they considered it good for a cow to be slaughtered during the ceremony.

---

(1) <note>original Sanskrit: Mata rudranam duhita vasunam...</note> "The cow is the mother of the Rudras, the daughter of the Vasus, the sister of the Adityas, the navel of immortality. I say to the people who understand: do not kill the innocent cow, the Aditi."
(2) <note>original Sanskrit: Sharanagatam cha hanyadyo...</note> "He who kills one who has sought refuge, or kills a cow, which is like a mother to humanity [is a sinner]."
(3) <note>original Sanskrit: Mata rudranam duhita vasunam...</note> This refers to the mantra recited when deciding whether to release the cow.
(4) <note>original Sanskrit: Gamanagam munchatu cha...</note> "Let the cow, which is not to be killed, be released."

<summary>This page discusses the historical tension in Vedic tradition between the general prohibition against killing cows and the specific ritual practice of slaughtering them for esteemed guests. It concludes that both releasing the cow and sacrificing it were practiced, often depending on the dietary preferences of the host and guest.</summary>

<keywords>Madhuparka, cow slaughter, non-violence, Aghanya, Mimansakas, ritual sacrifice, Vedic hospitality, Latyayana, Smriti</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the Madhuparka (honey-mixture) ceremony. While the previous page established that a cow was one of the six traditional offerings for a guest, this page examines the conflicting scriptural evidence regarding whether that cow was slaughtered for a feast or set free. The author navigates the tension between Vedic commands for non-violence and specific ritual instructions for animal sacrifice.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>Gobhila-Grihya-Sutra -- [Discussion on the Honey-Mixture Offering]</header>

Those who abstained from meat-eating considered the "release"—meaning the act of not slaughtering the cow during the *Madhuparka* <note>a ceremonial welcoming of a guest with honey and milk</note>—to be the superior path. The great poet **Bhavabhuti** hinted at this very sentiment in his play **Uttararamacharita** (1). Furthermore, if one were to argue that everyone who ate meat slaughtered a cow for the *Madhuparka*, that would also be incorrect. Many people respected the name "Aghanya" <note>original: "one not to be killed," a common Vedic epithet for cows</note>, but to fulfill the rule that "a *Madhuparka* cannot be without meat," they would instead consume goat meat. Therefore, in the **Yajnavalkya Smriti** <term>a foundational legal text</term>, the sage **Yajnavalkya** wrote the term "Great Goat" (*mahaja*) alongside "Great Ox" (*mahoksha*) for those who wished to avoid cow slaughter. 

Moreover, the author of the **Mitakshara** <term>a famous 11th-century commentary on Yajnavalkya's laws</term> interpreted the meaning of this passage differently (2). He writes that upon the arrival of a learned scholar <term>Shrotriya</term>, one should not necessarily present or slaughter a bull or a goat. Since honoring a scholar is a duty for every householder, how could poor householders provide a bull or goat for every scholar, especially since a scholar might visit a householder’s home daily? If one argues this is a matter of religious law, that too is improper; for even if a practice is prescribed as a religious duty, it should not be performed if it prevents one from reaching heaven or leads to public condemnation (3). These are the established sentences of the Law Codes.

Although both of these views have existed since ancient times, deciding which is true and which is false is the task of the **Mimamsa** <term>the school of Hindu philosophy focused on ritual interpretation and logic</term> school. Therefore, we shall consider this matter according to the principles of Mimamsa. If we simply accept that because both practices have "always existed" they are both equally valid or authoritative, we fall into error. This cannot be the case, for both good and bad actions have always occurred in the world. Merely because a practice is "ancient" does not mean that things like theft or adultery can ever be considered good or authoritative. If you argue that cow slaughter is authorized by the authority of the Vedas, then the investigation of Vedic authority belongs to the Mimamsa...

---

(1) This is the allusion: "Greatly respecting the sacred tradition that the *Madhuparka* must contain meat, householders offer a heifer or a great ox to a learned scholar who arrives as a guest..." <note>original Sanskrit: "samanso madhuparka ity amnayam..." from Bhavabhuti's play depicting ancient hospitality</note>.

(2) "One should prepare a great ox or a great goat for the scholar"—this should be interpreted in this way. Here, we have interpreted that this is intended for the guest-offering <term>Arghya</term> and not for the purpose of a gift or for slaughter. As it is said regarding the nature of human error: "The laws were prescribed for the errors of men's intelligence." <note>original Sanskrit fragment: "prajna-paradhe vihitam dharman manujanam..."</note>.

(3) "That which does not lead to heaven and is hated by the world [should be avoided]," from the **Manu Smriti** <term>The Laws of Manu, the most authoritative legal text in Hinduism</term>.

<summary>This page argues against the necessity of cow slaughter in the Madhuparka ceremony, suggesting that goat meat was an acceptable substitute and that social conscience and logic (Mimamsa) should override literal ritual interpretations that cause public outcry.</summary>
<keywords>Madhuparka, cow slaughter, meat-eating, Bhavabhuti, Yajnavalkya Smriti, Mitakshara, Mimamsa, Manu Smriti, guest hospitality, Aghanya</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological and theological debate regarding animal sacrifice in the Vedas. While the previous page discussed the substitution of goats for cows and the practical difficulties of providing large animals for every guest, this page introduces the Mimamsa school of logic to explain why certain "violent" passages should not be interpreted as literal commands.</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>Introduction</header>

# Introduction

...is dependent! Which Vedic sentence can be considered authoritative, and in what manner, cannot be decided without **Mimamsa** <term>the ancient Indian science of ritual interpretation and logic</term>. If every single Vedic sentence were taken as a literal authority, then the sentences **"The trees performed a sacrifice"** and **"The serpents performed a sacrifice"** would also have to be accepted as literal truth. However, they are not accepted as literal facts because inanimate trees cannot perform rituals. To maintain the scriptural authority of such statements, the venerable Sage **Jaimini** states (1) that:

**"The entire purpose of the Vedas is to prompt action. Those Vedic sentences in which a command for action is not found are 'meaningless' statements; however, if they are unified with a 'command sentence' (Vidhi), then they too can become authoritative. Otherwise, they cannot."**

Even though inanimate trees cannot perform a sacrifice, this sentence is meant to praise the sacrificial rite. It is like someone saying, **"Because they are full of fruit, even the trees bow their heads."** The true meaning of this sentence is simply that a person of great wealth or power should always remain humble. Similarly, the meaning here is that if even inanimate trees are described as performing sacrifices, then a conscious human being must surely perform them. Just as the sentence **"The trees performed a sacrifice"** was given a meaningful interpretation through this intent, we should consider the current issue in the same way.

Regarding the sentences starting with **"Just as others..."** etc., which contain accounts of honoring great men through the slaughter of a cow, the intent here is also not to actually slaughter a cow. Rather, the truth of the matter is that this belongs to the section on **Atithyeshti** <term>the ritual of honoring a guest</term>. That entire section concerns the worship of guests. Every sentence found there has the purpose of praising the guest and showing them the highest possible honor. Therefore, the intended meaning of that sentence is that if people are willing to go so far as to sacrifice a cow to honor a guest, then by any other means, one should certainly perform **guest-worship** in every possible way! That is all there is to this Vedic text; it is not a decree for cow-slaughter. If we were to take that sentence as a command for cow-slaughter, it would contradict the scriptural prohibition: **"Do not kill the innocent, sinless cow."** Therefore, the following arrangement should be understood: the sentence suggesting violence is merely an **Arthavada** <term>a rhetorical or laudatory statement not meant to be a literal law</term>. No logic supporting a command for violence is written there; however, the sentence **"Do not kill the cow"** is a **Vidhi** <term>a direct command or law</term>. In that command, the word **"Anagam"**—meaning **"the innocent one"**—refers to the prohibition of violence...

---

(1) <note>original Sanskrit: "Amnayasya kriyarthatvad anarthakyam atad-arthanam; vidhina tveka-vakyatvat stutyarthena vidhinam syuh." (Mimamsa Sutra 1.2.1 & 1.2.7)</note> Translation: Since the purpose of scripture is to enjoin action, those parts which do not refer to action are purposeless; however, because they form a single context with a command, they serve to praise that command.

<summary>This page applies the Mimamsa rules of interpretation to argue that Vedic mentions of cow-slaughter are "Arthavada" (rhetorical praise) rather than "Vidhi" (literal commands), intended to emphasize the importance of guest-hospitality.</summary>
<keywords>Mimamsa, Jaimini, interpretation, Arthavada, guest-worship, non-violence, Vedic logic, cow protection</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the interpretation of Vedic texts using Mimamsa logic. While the previous page established that some Vedic descriptions (like trees performing sacrifice) are figurative, this page applies that logic to the prohibition of cow slaughter, contrasting "descriptive" statements with "direct commands."</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>The Gobhila Grhya Sutra - [The Ritual Immolation of Animals]</header>

The supporting logic has also been written; therefore, the first statement is weak, and the "prohibition of violence" statement is strong. Furthermore, it is a tenet of **Mimamsa** <term>the science of interpreting Vedic rituals and logic</term> that any sentence containing words like "which," "that," "other," "like," and "this" <note>original Sanskrit: "यत्" (yat), "तत्" (tat), "अन्य" (anya), "इव" (iva), "अद:" (adah)</note> becomes subordinate and weak when compared to a direct **Injunction** <term>Vidhi; a primary command or law</term>. Therefore, here too, the direct command found in the **Samhitas** <term>the core hymns of the Vedas</term> which states, **"Do not slay the innocent cow, who is Aditi"** <note>original: "गामनागामदितिं वधिष्ट" (gām anāgām aditiṃ vadhiṣṭa)</note>, holds significantly more authority than those descriptive **Brahmana** <term>prose commentaries on the Vedas</term> passages that seem to suggest cow slaughter using words like "that," "as if," or "others." Consequently, only those people who were greedy for violence and meat, and who did not understand the profound intent of the Mimamsa scriptures, practiced the killing of cows or other living beings during the honoring of guests. According to the scriptures, the primary and authoritative position is the prohibition of violence or the "release" <note>utsarjana; the practice of letting the animal go free instead of killing it</note> of the animal; the side favoring slaughter is not authoritative. *

->**Salutations to Shri Ramachandra.**<-

->Greetings and blessings to the illustrious Pandit Bhimsen Sharma.<-

It is my opinion as well that, compared to **Tamasic** <term>dark, ignorant, or violent</term> worship, **Sattvic** <term>pure, harmonious, and enlightened</term> worship brings greater satisfaction to the deity and leads to the greater welfare of the worshipper. However, one should not reduce the value of the offerings <note>original: "mūlya-dravya"</note>. Furthermore, due to the diversity of human inclinations, one should not attempt to change the practices of those who place their faith in Tamasic tendencies until they have developed a firm faith in the Sattvic way. May all be auspicious. Date: the 7th day of the bright fortnight of Bhadra, Sunday.

->Shri Pandit Shivkumar Sharma.<-

**Translation of the above:**—Sattvic worship, rather than Tamasic worship, is the cause for the satisfaction of the deity and the welfare of the one performing the worship. I concur with this. However, do not decrease the value of the materials for worship. Do not change the tendencies of those who believe in Tamasic practices due to their varied personal dispositions until they have a firm faith in the Sattvic path.

# Animal Immolation or Violence in Sacrifice

## The Opinion of Shri 108 Pandit Bhimsen Sharma Ji (Introduction to the Apastamba Grhya Sutra, pp. 4-5)

---

\* This opinion on the subject of violence in sacrifice belongs to the well-known editor of **"Dharma Divakara" (Volume 4, Ray 4, Samvat 1943, pages 132–137)**. This journal used to be published in Calcutta but has since ceased publication.

\*\* In Central India, there is a small princely state called Suthaliya. The honorable King of Suthaliya sought a scriptural ruling from Shri 108 Pandit Bhimsen Sharma ji to stop the custom of sacrificing goats and other animals during the **Navaratri** <term>a nine-night festival dedicated to the Goddess Durga</term> worship. Regarding this matter, the aforementioned Pandit ji wrote to the famous Shri Pandit Shivkumar Shastri ji of Benares to seek his opinion. The reply to that letter is printed above.

<summary>This page argues that Vedic scriptures prioritize the prohibition of animal sacrifice over its practice, using Mimamsa logic to dismiss passages suggesting slaughter. It also includes a scholarly correspondence supporting "Sattvic" (bloodless) worship over "Tamasic" (violent) traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Mimamsa logic, cow protection, Sattvic worship, Tamasic worship, animal sacrifice, Bhimsen Sharma, Shivkumar Sharma, Suthaliya state, Vedic interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific logical arguments regarding cow slaughter on page 34 to a broader discussion on the contextual nature of Dharma (duty/righteousness). It explains how the validity of a ritual depends on the time, place, and the internal state of the practitioner, justifying why certain ancient Vedic practices are no longer performed today.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>Introduction ||</header>

Our respected readers should take special note of this: when we accept these **Sutra** <term>aphoristic manuals of ritual and law</term> texts as truly authoritative, it is established that a deed—performed in the specific place and time, and according to the specific method prescribed by the **Shastras** <term>sacred scriptures</term> for a specific person—is the proper **Dharma** <term>righteous duty</term> for that person. If performed otherwise, that same act becomes **Adharma** <term>unrighteousness</term>.

For example, for a **householder** <term>Grihastha; the second stage of Hindu life focused on family and society</term>, having relations with one's wedded wife in one's own home, during the proper season and at night, is Dharma. If the householder does not do so, it is Adharma. However, for a **student-celibate** <term>Brahmachari</term> or a **renunciant** <term>Sannyasi</term>, doing so is Adharma. Even for a householder, having relations with his own wife during a pilgrimage, in a forest, or during the day is Adharma.

If we do not follow the commands of the Shastras, the concepts of Dharma and Adharma cannot be established at all. Crying is generally considered bad everywhere, but according to the authority of the **Vedas** <note>original: "अन्यत्र त्वद्रुदत्याः संविशन्तु" (anyatra tvadrudatyāḥ saṃviśantu) — meaning: 'Let those who weep enter elsewhere,' a verse often cited regarding the bride's departure</note>, the crying of a daughter leaving her father's house for her husband's home is considered good. Giving insults is normally a bad act, but during a wedding, both women and men consider insults <note>this refers to traditional 'Gali' songs sung playfully during Indian weddings</note> to be auspicious.

Following this same logic, the **ritual immolation of animals** <term>Pashvalambhana</term> was not considered bad in ancient times. However, according to social custom, killing animals against the scriptures simply to increase one’s own flesh <note>i.e., for gluttony</note> was considered extremely evil.

Now, a time has come where people are more shocked by things written in the scriptures, yet they are not so disturbed by the fact that thousands of cows and other animals are killed daily for meat-eaters—a fact everyone knows. When the ancient **Sages and Teachers** <term>Rishis and Acharyas</term> foresaw such a terrible time approaching, they wrote in advance: **"That which is condemned by the world"** <note>original: "लोकविक्रुष्टं मेवच" (lokavikruṣṭam eva ca)</note>. This means that if a particular Dharma is considered bad by the public at a certain time, it should not be performed. Therefore, the act of "animal immolation" is not a duty in this current age. 

For this reason, one should not feel distress or hesitation upon seeing such thoughts in these ancient books. Look at any tradition of wedding or the **sacred thread ceremony** <term>Yajnopavita</term>; it is written that the groom should touch the bride’s heart while reciting the mantra, **"Into my vow, I take your heart"** <note>original: "ममव्रतेते हृदयं" (mama vrate te hṛdayaṃ)</note>. In the past, people's principles followed the rule that **"the knowledge of Dharma is prescribed for those not attached to wealth and desire"** <note>original: "अर्थ कामेष्व सक्तानां धर्मज्ञानं विधीयते" (artha kāmeṣv asaktānāṃ dharmajñānaṃ vidhīyate)</note>. In their view, greed and lust were destroyed in the face of Dharma, just as darkness is utterly destroyed before the sun; thus, they felt no hesitation in touching the bride’s heart during the wedding. 

But now, everyone feels hesitation in doing so. The reason for this is that the **inner conscience** <term>Antahkarana</term> has been suppressed by lust and greed. Similarly, in the case of ritual animal immolation, because a pure sense of Dharma no longer remains in the conscience, one feels shame, fear, or hesitation. This is why we are no longer eligible or authorized to perform those ancient acts.

<vocab>Bhumika (Introduction), Dharma (Righteous Duty), Adharma (Unrighteousness), Shastra (Scripture), Grihastha (Householder), Pashvalambhana (Animal Sacrifice), Loka Riti (Social Custom), Samskara (Sacrament/Rite of Passage), Sankoch (Hesitation/Shame), Antahkarana (Inner Conscience)</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that Dharma is contextual, depending on time, place, and the purity of the practitioner's heart. It argues that while animal sacrifice was once a scriptural rite, it is now prohibited because it is socially condemned and modern people lack the spiritual purity required for such ancient rituals.</summary>
<keywords>Dharma, ritual animal sacrifice, Pashvalambhana, social custom, householder duties, wedding rituals, Vedic interpretation, inner conscience</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory remarks and arguments regarding animal sacrifice (Pashusanjnapana), transitioning from the philosophical justification of contextual Dharma on page 35 to a pragmatic editorial conclusion and acknowledgments by the translator.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>Of the Gobhila Grihya Sutra - [Animal Sacrifice]</header>

The summary is this: even if our respected readers consider the act of **animal sacrifice** <term>Pashusanjnapana; literally "quieting" the animal</term> to be entirely improper according to their own views, they should understand that no one is insisting that we perform such acts today. On the contrary, the **Dharma Shastras** <term>codes of law and ethics</term> forbid it in this current age. Therefore, it is useless and pointless to find fault simply because it is written in these ancient texts. We should take advantage of whatever matters in these books seem beneficial and helpful to us according to the needs of our time for the fulfillment of our goals. Since all actions can never be suitable for everyone in all places and at all times, how could every single writing in these texts be favorable to us now? 

For example, a **cooling grass mat** <note>original: "khaskhas ki tatti" — screens made of vetiver grass used for cooling rooms</note> is useless in the winter, yet it becomes meaningful again when the heat returns. Similarly, warm clothes might seem like a useless display during the summer or in a hot country, but they become helpful and meaningful again upon reaching a cold country or when winter arrives. Furthermore, just as a poison kept in a grocer's shop can become as beneficial as nectar for one who is authorized to use it as medicine, it is a mistake for someone to feel hatred, disgust, or dislike toward it. In the same way, one should not harbor any hatred or disgust toward the subjects of animal sacrifice found in these texts.

->Pandit Bhimsen Sharma, Editor of **Brahmana-Sarvasva**—Etawah<-

### Conclusion

In closing, I must state that in this translation of the **Gobhila Grihya Sutra** <term>a manual of domestic rituals for the Sama Veda tradition</term>, we received great assistance from the Sanskrit commentary of Pandit Satyavrata Samashrami; therefore, we thank that great soul millions of times from the bottom of our hearts. Furthermore, in the printing of this work, Shri 108 Pandit Bhimsen Sharma has assisted us with proofreading and corrections. For this reason, we are also grateful to this learned Pandit.

The version of this book printed by Pandit Satyavrata Samashrami is available for 5 rupees, but it has one major flaw: while the Sanskrit commentary includes the **opening words** <term>pratikas; the first words of a verse used as a reference</term> of the hymns for the domestic rituals, it does not provide the full hymns themselves. To get the **Mantra Brahmana** <term>the specific portion of the Veda containing the full verses</term> separately, one must pay another 5 rupees. Thus, if someone wishes to acquire a complete Gobhila Grihya Sutra, they would have to spend 10 rupees. We have printed all the hymns from the *Mantra Brahmana* in their proper places (wherever required), and despite including a 40-page introduction and the Hindi translation, we have kept the price at only 2.5 rupees. 

My second request is that we have not provided an extensive critique on several subjects mentioned in the *Grihya Sutras* in this introduction. As of yet, all the different *Grihya Sutras* belonging to various branches of the **Vedas** <term>the oldest scriptures of Hinduism</term> have not been collected, and research is currently underway. It is my hope that in the introductions to the **Ashvalayana** and **Paraskara Grihya Sutras**, we will write a full analysis of every subject mentioned within those manuals.

Yours—
Shastra Prakash Office—Mathurapur }
Post—Biddupur (Muzaffarpur) } Udayanarayana Singh of the Kshatriya Clan

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the actual text of the Gobhila Grihya Sutras, following the introductory materials and Table of Contents. It introduces the first two fundamental rules of domestic Vedic ritual: the intention of the text and the basic requirements of the practitioner.</meta>

->ॐ<-
<note>The sacred syllable "OM," used here as an auspicious invocation to begin the text.</note>

## Belonging to the Sama Veda ||

# Now, the Gobhila Domestic Sutras ||

---

## 1. Now, therefore, we shall explain the domestic rituals. || 1 ||

**Commentary:** The word 'Now' (*atha*) is a particle used to indicate the beginning of a book. 'Therefore' (*atah*) is an expression used according to the rhetorical style of the teachers of that time. 'Domestic rituals' (*grṛhyā-karmāṇi*): that which is beneficial for the householder (*gṛhya*) is traditionally understood to refer to the domestic fire; the text will state shortly thereafter, "This fire alone becomes his domestic fire (Sutra 21)." In that fire, we 'shall explain' (*upadekṣyāmaḥ*)—meaning we shall teach the specific procedures for—the 'rituals' (*karmāṇi*) that must be performed, such as the daily fire offerings (*Agnihotra*) and the preparatory rites like establishing the sacred fire (*Agnyādhāna*). The long vowel in the word 'Grṛhyā' is an archaic Vedic form. || 1 ||

->Now, the author states the rules common to all rituals:<-

**Translation:** After this, we shall provide instruction on the daily fire offerings and other rituals to be performed in the \*domestic fire, as well as useful rites such as the "establishment of the fire." || 1 ||

## 2. The rite is to be performed by one wearing the sacred thread and who has sipped water. || 2 ||

**Commentary:** The 'rite' (*kṛtyam*)—meaning the collection of actions to be described hereafter—must be 'performed' (*kāryam*) by a person who is 'wearing the sacred thread' (*yajñopavītinā*) and, furthermore, is 'one who has sipped water' (*ācāntodakena*), meaning a man who has performed the ceremonial sipping of water <term>ācamana; a ritual purification involving sipping water from the palm of the hand while reciting mantras</term>. || 2 ||

---

\*Fire is generally of three types: 1. <term>Śrautāgni; the "Revealed Fire" used in major public Vedic sacrifices described in the Brahmana texts, such as the Garhapatya fire</term>, 2. <term>Gṛhyāgni; the "Domestic Fire" used for household rites</term>, and 3. <term>Laukikāgni; the "Common Fire" used for everyday purposes</term>. 

In other words, the fire whose use and arrangement is heard of in the "Brahmana" portion of the Vedas is called "Śrautāgni." Aside from this, there is the fire which, although known from the Vedas as being useful and mandatory for a householder, does not have its specific methods of use detailed in the Vedas themselves. Therefore, the fire instructed through the traditional codes (*Smritis*) of sages like Gobhila is called 'Domestic Fire' (*Gṛhyāgni*) or 'Remembered Fire' (*Smārtāgni*). Finally, the fire for which there is neither a scriptural command nor a prohibition in the Vedas, but which is used by people for tasks like cooking, is called "Common Fire" (*Laukikāgni*).

<summary>This page introduces the Gobhila Grihya Sutras, defining "domestic rituals" as those performed in the household fire and establishing the two primary requirements for a practitioner: wearing the sacred thread and ritual purification through water.</summary>
<keywords>Gobhila Grihya Sutra, domestic rituals, Grihyagni, Shrautagni, Laukikagni, Yajnopavita, Achamana, Sama Veda, Vedic fire sacrifice</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening chapter of the Gobhila Grihya Sutra, completing the commentary on the second rule regarding ritual purity and establishing the fundamental rules for the timing and materials of domestic sacrifices.</meta>

In the Commentary on the Gobhila Domestic Sutras—

Commentary—The rituals described hereafter should be performed while wearing the <term>sacred thread<note>Sanskrit: *Yajñopavīta*. A cord worn by the "twice-born" castes to signify spiritual initiation.</note></term> ‡ and after sipping water for ritual purification. || 2 ||

### 3. One should know the time for rituals to be during the northern course of the sun, in the bright fortnight, on an auspicious day, and before the sun turns toward the afternoon. || 3 ||

'During the northern course' (*udagayane*) means the northern path of the sun <note>original: *uttarāyaṇa*—the six months between the winter and summer solstices</note>; 'in the bright fortnight' (*pūrvapakṣe*) refers to the waxing moon <note>original: *śuklapakṣa*</note>; 'on an auspicious day' (*puṇye'hani*) means a day free from flaws such as being obscured by heavy clouds; 'before the sun turns' (*prāg āvarttanāt*) means before the change of the day’s direction, referring to the forenoon. This 'time' (*kālaṃ*) should be 'known' (*vidyāt*) for all the rituals that are about to be performed. || 3 ||

Commentary—Wherever in this text no specific instruction is given (such as "perform this at a specific hour"), the practitioner should understand that the work must be done during the sun's northern course, during the waxing moon, and on a "flawless" (clear) day before noon. || 3 ||

### 4. And also according to the specific instruction. || 4 ||

One should also understand the timing according to specific instructions (*yathādeśam*). Wherever we prescribe a particular time for a specific rite, that time alone should be followed, rather than the general rules regarding the northern course of the sun, etc., mentioned in the previous rule. || 4 ||

Commentary—Whenever a specific time or arrangement is directed for a certain ritual, that specific instruction is authoritative, not the general rule found in Sutra 3. || 4 ||

### 5. All rituals are accompanied by the necessary materials. || 5 ||

'All' (*sarvāṇi*) domestic rituals are 'accompanied by materials' (*āhāryavanti*), meaning they must be performed with the necessary ritual tools and offerings, such as <term>Kusha grass<note>A sacred grass (*Desmostachya bipinnata*) used for purification and as a ritual seat.</note></term> and other implements. || 5 ||

Commentary—In all domestic rituals, various "implements" such as Kusha grass and— <note>The text here cuts off mid-sentence.</note>

---

<margin>
‡ **Sacred Thread**—When the thread hangs from the left shoulder across to the right side, it is called the "Sacred Thread" (*Yajñopavīta*). When it hangs from the right shoulder to the left side, it is called the "Ancestral Thread" (*Prācīnāvītī*). When it is worn around the neck like a garland, it is called "Level-threaded" (*Nivīta*). 

For rituals concerning ancestors, one should be "Ancestral-threaded"; for rituals concerning the gods, one should be "Sacred-threaded." At times when neither divine nor ancestral work is being done—such as during bodily functions, urination, or general movement—one should be "Level-threaded." This serves as a ritual signal. In ancient times, most people spent the majority of their time in divine rituals, and so they were usually "Sacred-threaded." In this current age, for those twice-born (*Dvija*) who have fallen away from their ritual duties, being "Level-threaded" is more appropriate; evidence for this will follow in this text.
</margin>

<summary>This page defines the general timing for Vedic domestic rituals (northern sun, waxing moon, forenoon) and introduces the requirement of ritual materials. It also provides a detailed explanation of how the sacred thread must be positioned depending on the type of ritual being performed.</summary>
<keywords>Gobhila Grihya Sutra, Uttarayana, Shukla Paksha, Yajñopavita, ritual timing, Kusha grass, sacred thread positions, forenoon rituals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory section of the Gobhila Grihya Sutra. It transitions from general rules regarding the timing of rituals to the specific procedures for establishing the sacred domestic fire—a pivotal moment in a young man's life as he moves from studenthood to becoming a householder.</meta>

<margin>Chapter 1, Section 1, Sutras 3-9</margin> ...the materials <note>original: *sāmagrī*</note> are required. || 5 ||

## 6. At the completion of a ritual, a worthy meal should be offered according to one's ability. || 6 ||

'At the completion' (*apavarge*) refers to the end of a religious ceremony. A 'worthy meal' (*abhirūpa-bhojanam*) means food that is appropriate and suitable according to the standards of the scriptures; such a meal should be provided for one, two, or many individuals 'according to one's ability' (*yathā-śakti*), meaning it should be consistent with one's own financial means. || 6 || This concludes the general rules for all rituals.

Translation of the Hindi Commentary: At the conclusion of every ritual, one should feed one, two, or more appropriate persons according to the scriptures and one's own means. || 6 ||

---

->Now he describes the rules for establishing the sacred fires:<-

## 7. A student, having studied the Veda, and being about to offer the final fuel stick... || 7 ||

It was previously mentioned: "domestic rituals" (*gṛhya-karmāṇi*). Here, the text addresses: what kind of fire is the <term>domestic fire<note>Sanskrit: *gṛhya-agni*. The sacred fire kept by a householder for daily offerings and life-cycle rites.</note></term>? This is explained in the following passage, starting with 'a student' (*brahmacārī*) and ending with the definition of the domestic fire.
'A student' (*brahmacārī*), 'having studied the Veda' (*vedam adhītya*)—meaning having stayed at the teacher's home and completed his Vedic studies—is 'about to offer' (*ādhāsyan*) the 'final' (*antyām*) 'fuel stick' (*samidham*) which marks the end of his student life. This relates to the later instruction: "one should establish the fire" (Sutra 14). While a student normally offers fuel sticks into his teacher's fire every day, at this specific time he does not do so in the usual way. Instead, after fetching water and performing the <term>ritual bringing-forward of the fire<note>Sanskrit: *agnipraṇayana*</note></term>, he should place that final fuel stick into his own fire. || 7 ||

Translation of the Hindi Commentary: A student (while living at the teacher's home), at the conclusion of his Vedic studies, being prepared to sacrifice the final 'fuel stick' (*samit*—sacred wood for the ritual fire). || 7 ||

## 8. ...or when wishing to take the hand of a wife. || 8 ||

If the student did not take up the sacred fire at the time of offering the final fuel stick that ended his studenthood, then he should offer that final stick into his teacher’s fire just as on previous days. When else should the fire be established? He says: 'Or' (*vā*) when 'wishing to take' (*jighṛkṣan*) the 'hand' (*pāṇiṃ*) of a 'wife' (*jāyāyāḥ*). One should "establish the fire" (Sutra 14) before the <term>marriage ceremony<note>Sanskrit: *pāṇigrahaṇa*, literally "taking the hand," the central rite of a Hindu wedding.</note></term>. || 8 ||

Translation of the Hindi Commentary: Or, having become prepared for the marriage ceremony (taking a wife's hand). || 8 ||

## 9. Having fetched water secretly, having swept and smeared a place that slopes toward the east or north, or is level, having drawn a line toward the east in the middle, and to the north of that, having drawn three lines toward the east... he should sprinkle it with water. || 9 ||

<vocab>completion, worthy meal, according to ability, student, having studied the Veda, fuel stick, establishing the fire, wife, marriage (taking the hand), bringing forward the fire</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the general rules for ritual offerings, emphasizing the feeding of worthy persons, and begins the instructions for establishing the domestic fire at the end of studenthood or before marriage.</summary>
<keywords>completion (apavarga), worthy meal, student (brahmachari), Vedic study, fuel stick (samit), domestic fire (grihya-agni), marriage (panigrahana), ritual preparation</keywords>
