<meta>This page continues from page 3, which concluded the general rules for completing a ritual. It begins the specific technical instructions for establishing the sacred domestic fire, moving from the philosophical requirements to the physical preparation of the ritual ground.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Grihya Sutra—</header>

Regarding the preparation for bringing the fire (*agni-praṇayana*), what kind of sanctification of the site is desired? It is said—'Well-protected' (*anuguptāḥ*)—meaning kept safe from things such as the disposal of excrement or urine, or being used for oil massages. Having 'fetched' (*āhṛtya*) 'waters' (*apaḥ*), one should 'sweep' (*parisamūhya*) the 'site' (*deśam*) with those waters. This site should be 'level' (*samam*) and 'sloping toward the east or north' (*prāk-udak-pravaṇam*). In the 'middle' (*madhyataḥ*) of that site, one should draw a line pointing 'eastward' (*prācīm*). At its 'back' (*paścāt*)—at the base of that line—one should draw another line pointing 'northward' (*udīcīm*), which is 'attached' (*saṃlagnām*) and joined to the eastward line. In the 'middle' (*madhye*) of the site, one should 'draw' (*ullikhya*) 'three more eastward lines' (*tisraḥ prācīḥ*). Then one 'should sprinkle' (*abhyukṣet*) the site with drops of water from the tips of Kusa grass and other materials. This prepared site is called a '<term>sthaṇḍila<note>A level, rectangular piece of ground prepared and sanctified specifically to hold a sacred fire.</note></term>.' || 9 ||

Translation of the Hindi Commentary:—(From a water source like a pond where the water is not defiled by being thrown into, or by filth, urine, oil-smearing, or moss, but is carefully protected under royal decree or similar authority), one should bring pure water and smear the site with it. It is necessary that this site be level and slope gradually toward the east or north. In the middle of this smeared site, one should mark a line pointing eastward. Below that, one should draw a northward-pointing line and join it to the first. In the middle, draw three more lines. Finally, sprinkle the previously fetched water upon it. This is called a "sthaṇḍila." || 9 ||

### 10. This procedure of marking lines applies everywhere. || 10 ||

'This' (*eṣā*) action, starting from the fetching of water, is called '<term>lakṣaṇāvṛt<note>Literally "the rule of marks"; the standard sequence of drawing lines and sprinkling water to purify the sacrificial ground.</note></term>'; it is to be 'everywhere' (*sarvatra*)—that is, practiced whenever the ritual of bringing the fire is performed. || 10 ||

Translation of the Hindi Commentary:—The name of this ritual action is "lakṣaṇāvṛt." This is suitable for use everywhere in the context of bringing the sacred fire. || 10 ||

### 11. They bring the fire forward while facing it, with the words: Bhūr, Bhuvaḥ, Svaḥ. || 11 ||

With the mantra '*Bhūr Bhuvaḥ Svaḥ*' <note>These are the *Mahāvyāhṛtis*, the three sacred utterances representing Earth, Atmosphere, and Heaven.</note>, and having positioned oneself so as to be 'facing it' (*abhimukhaṃ*), they 'bring forward' (*praṇayanti*) the 'fire' (*agnim*). The word *praṇayanti* is in the 'Vedic subjunctive used in the sense of the optative' <note>original: *liṅ-arthe leṭ* (a technical grammatical reference to Pāṇini 3.4.7)</note>; thus it means "they should bring forward." The plural form is used to establish a general rule: all those performing rituals should carry out this bringing of the fire in this manner. || 11 ||

If the master of the house (the father, etc.) is still alive at the end of the student’s period of celibacy, or if the fire was not taken during the time of the marriage ceremony (*pāṇigrahaṇa*), the text now states that when the master of the house dies—whenever he may pass away—the taking of the fire must then be performed:

---

<margin>*</margin> Instruction is given regarding what kind of site should be prepared for bringing the fire.

<vocab>Gobhila Grihya Sutra, bringing the fire, sacred mound, procedure of marks, sacred utterances, marriage ceremony, rite of passage</vocab>

<keywords>sthaṇḍila, lakṣaṇāvṛt, agni-praṇayana, Bhūr Bhuvaḥ Svaḥ, ritual geometry, domestic fire, Gobhila</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from page 4, which detailed the physical preparation of the ritual ground (sthaṇḍila). It now moves from the physical preparation to the specific timing and conditions under which a student or householder must formally establish their own sacred domestic fire.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>Chapter 1, Section 1, Sutras 8-13 ] . Establishment of the Sacred Fire ॥</header>

Commentary:—After that, in that sprinkled and sanctified place, one should recite the sacred syllables—"Earth, Atmosphere, Heaven" <note>original: "Bhur Bhuvah Svah"</note>—and establish the fire directly in front of oneself. ॥ 11 ॥

### 12. Or, upon the death of the master of the house, the fire is established as the supreme station. ॥ 12 ॥

The word "Or" <note>original: "vā"</note> indicates an alternative; "master of the house" <note>original: "gṛhapatau"</note> refers to the father or elder; when he has "passed away" <note>original: "prete"</note>, only then is the "establishment of the supreme station" <note>original: "parameṣṭhīkaraṇam"</note> performed. This term is a causative grammatical form used here as an adverb; it describes the manner in which the fire is accepted as one's own. One should "perform the establishment of the fire" (as per Sutra 14). The spelling of "Parameṣṭhikaraṇam" with a short 'i' is not found in any manuscripts and is therefore incorrect. ॥ 12 ॥

Commentary:—If the father or the head of the household is still alive at the time one completes their celibate studentship <note>original: <term>Brahmacharya<note>The first stage of life in the Vedic system, dedicated to study and discipline.</note></term></note> or at the time of marriage, when should the student take up the sacred fire? Regarding this, it is said that he should take up the fire only upon the death of the master of the house. ॥ 12 ॥

->Three general times for taking the fire have been mentioned; now the text explains a specific detail—<-

### 13. Likewise, at the conjunction of a lunar date, a star, and a holy day. ॥ 13 ॥

"Likewise" <note>original: "tathā"</note> means that just as a specific time is required for the final offering of the fuel-stick, so it is for the establishment of the fire. At the "conjunction" <note>original: "samavāye"</note>—the auspicious meeting—of these three: the <term>tithi<note>A lunar day.</note></term>, the <term>nakshatra<note>A lunar mansion or constellation.</note></term>, and the <term>parva<note>A holy day, such as the new or full moon.</note></term>. (This connects to what follows). ॥ 13 ॥

->If such a conjunction does not occur quickly, it is said—<-

<margin>* While living in the teacher's house during the study of the Vedas, students daily offer fuel-sticks <note>original: <term>samid-āhuti<note>The ritual offering of specialized wooden sticks into a sacred fire.</note></term></note> into the teacher's domestic fire. However, when studies are completed, the final offering should not be done as before; rather, the offering should be made into a fire prepared by the student himself. If, due to some obstacle, the fire is not taken at that time, then it must be taken before marriage, so that the wedding rituals (such as the offering of fried grain and the taking of the hand) can be performed in his own fire. But if at that time the father or another master of the house is living, then even then, it is unnecessary to take the fire in a separate place; therefore, one should take the fire only upon the death of the master of the house.</margin>

Commentary:—Just as a specific time is expected for the final offering of the fuel-stick, similarly, the meeting of the lunar date, the star, and the holy day is necessary for the establishment of the fire. * ॥ 13 ॥

Generally, three times are prescribed for taking the fire: 1. At the time of the fuel-offering marking the end of studentship, 2. At the time of marriage, and 3. Upon the death of the master of the house. Now, through this sutra and the next, the timing is explained in even greater detail. ॥

<vocab>Agnyadhanum (Establishing the fire), Parameshthikaranam (Supreme establishment), Samid-ahuti (Fuel offering), Brahmacharya (Student life), Grihyagni (Domestic fire), Tithi-Nakshatra-Parva (Auspicious calendar conjunctions)</vocab>

<meta>This page follows page 5, which discussed the timing of establishing the sacred domestic fire, specifically noting that a son establishes his own fire only after the death of his father. This page (page 6) continues this theme by detailing the specific lunar days and the physical sources from which the sacred fire should be procured.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>On the Commentary of the Gobhila Grihya Sutra—</header>

## One should perform the establishment of the fire on either the New Moon or the Full Moon day. ॥ 14 ॥

'**Or**' <note>original: "vā"</note> means alternatively; '**on the New Moon**' <note>original: "darśe"</note> refers to the day of the Amavasya; '**or**' <note>original: "vā"</note> alternatively; '**on the Full Moon**' <note>original: "paurṇamāse"</note> refers to the Purnima. '**The establishment of the fire**' <note>original: "agnisamādhānam"</note> means the proper placing, holding, and nourishing of the sacred fire. '**One should perform**' <note>original: "kurvīta"</note> indicates the requirement. ॥

If one finds that there is a delay in the perfect alignment of the lunar date, the constellation, and the festival day, then one should simply take up the fire on any available New Moon or Full Moon day. ॥ 14 ॥

From where should one bring the fire to establish it? The text says:

## Having brought the fire from the house of a Vaishya or from a metal hearth, one should place it. ॥ 15 ॥

'**From the house of a Vaishya**' <note>original: "vaiśyakulād vā"</note> means from the home of a householder belonging to the merchant or farming class <note>original: <term>Vaishya<note>The third social class in the Vedic system, traditionally responsible for commerce, agriculture, and cattle-rearing.</note></term></note>. Or, '**from a metal hearth**' <note>original: "ambarīpāt"</note> means from a large frying pan or a furnace. '**Having brought**' the '**fire**,' one should '**place it**' <note>original: "abhyādadhyāt"</note>, which means to perform the formal reception and establishment of the fire. ॥ 15 ॥

Bring the fire from the home of a householder of the Vaishya caste, or from a large furnace, and establish it in its place. ॥ 15 ॥

## Or alternatively, from the house of a "great sacrificer," whether he be a Brahmin, a Kshatriya, or a Vaishya. ॥ 16 ॥

'**Or alternatively**' <note>original: "apivā"</note> from the '**house**' <note>original: "āgārāt"</note> of a '**Brahmin**,' a '**Kshatriya**' <note>original: "rājanya"</note>, or a '**Vaishya**' who is a '**great sacrificer**' <note>original: "bahuyājinaḥ"</note>. This phrase connects to the previous instruction regarding bringing the fire. The text mandates taking the fire from a "great sacrificer," who can belong to any of these three classes. The mention of the Vaishya here, even though they were mentioned in the previous sutra, is intentional. If the Vaishya had not been mentioned here, it might seem that the fire should only be taken from a Brahmin or Kshatriya who is a great sacrificer, or from a common Vaishya house. By including the Vaishya in this list, the text clarifies that it is desirable to take fire from the home of any of these three if they perform many sacrifices, but also allows for fire from a common Vaishya household if a "great sacrificer" is not available. ॥ 16 ॥

Or, one may bring and establish the fire from the house of a "great sacrificer" (someone in whose home Vedic sacrifices are frequently performed). It is acceptable whether this sacrificer is a Brahmin, a Kshatriya, or a Vaishya. ॥ 16 ॥

## Or alternatively, one may establish another fire by churning. ॥ 17 ॥

'**Or alternatively**' <note>original: "apivā"</note>, by '**churning**' <note>original: "mathitvā"</note>—referring to the process of generating fire using two wooden sticks—one should '**establish**' '**another**' <note>original: "anyam"</note>, meaning a brand new, fresh fire. ॥ 17 ॥

Or, one may take a new fire by churning an 'Arani' <note>original: <term>Arani<note>A ritual device consisting of two pieces of wood; the fire is produced by the heat of friction when the upper stick is rotated rapidly against the lower block.</note></term></note> to produce a fresh flame. ॥ 17 ॥

<summary>This page outlines the specific timing (New or Full Moon) and sources for establishing a sacred domestic fire, including the homes of various castes, frequent sacrificers, or through the ritual churning of wooden sticks.</summary>
<keywords>Agni-samadhana, New Moon, Full Moon, Vaishya, Bahuyajin, Arani, sacred fire sources, domestic ritual</keywords>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>Chapter 1, Section 2, Sutras 14-22 ] Establishing the Sacred Fire ॥</header>

## But that [fire produced from fire-drills] yields merit alone, and does not bring prosperity. ॥ 18 ॥
## One should do as one desires. ॥ 19 ॥

'**Does not bring prosperity**' <note>original: "anarddhukaḥ"</note> means it is devoid of material wealth; '**it yields merit alone**' <note>original: "puṇyaḥ tu eva"</note> means it generates only spiritual merit. This refers to fire produced by friction <note>original: <term>āraṇeyaḥ<note>Fire produced by the "arani," two wooden blocks (usually pipal wood) rubbed together to create a spark.</note></term></note>. '**Therefore**' <note>original: "iti"</note>, for this reason, '**one should do as one desires**' <note>original: "yathā kāmayeta tathā kuryāt"</note>. If the practitioner desires worldly fruits like wealth and prosperity, he should bring the fire from the house of a merchant <note>original: "vaiśyakulād"</note> or a similar source as mentioned previously. If, however, he has no such inclination and desires only spiritual merit, he should take the fire only after churning the fire-drills. ॥ 18-19 ॥

**Commentary:** From the fire newly produced by these 'fire-drills' <note>original: "araṇi"</note>, merit is indeed gained when performing the rituals to be described hereafter, but* material wealth is not obtained. ॥ 18 ॥

**Commentary:** Therefore, let him act according to his desire [choosing from the options in Sutras 15-17]. ॥ 19 ॥

## That fire in which he places the final fuel stick, or in which he makes offerings while wishing to take the hand of a wife—he should carefully preserve that. ॥ 20 ॥

'**He**' <note>original: "saḥ"</note>, the person; '**in whichever fire**' <note>original: "yat eva"</note> '**he places the final fuel stick**' <note>original: "antyāṃ samidham ādadhāti"</note>, '**or**' <note>original: "vā"</note> '**makes offerings while wishing to take the hand of a wife**' <note>original: "jāyāyāḥ pāṇiṃ jighṛkṣan juhoti"</note>, such as the offerings of puffed rice and the like, '**that**' <note>original: "tam"</note> fire '**he should carefully preserve**' <note>original: "abhisamyaccheta"</note>, meaning he must guard it with effort. ॥ 20 ॥

**Commentary:** Having brought the fire in this manner, that fire in which he offers the final "fuel stick" <note>original: "samit"</note>, or in which he completes the marriage rituals like the puffed rice offering <note>original: <term>lājā homa<note>A core wedding ritual where the bride and groom offer puffed rice into the fire to symbolize fertility and prosperity.</note></term></note>, he should keep that fire with great care. ॥ 20 ॥

## That alone becomes his domestic fire. ॥ 21 ॥

'**That fire alone**' <note>original: "saḥ eva agniḥ"</note> becomes '**his**' <note>original: "asya"</note>, the one who has taken it; '**domestic**' <note>original: "gṛhyaḥ"</note>, meaning beneficial for the home or useful for household rituals. For this reason, it becomes known by the name 'Grihya' <note>The Domestic Fire</note>. ॥ 21 ॥

**Commentary:** This is his 'Grihya' fire; that is, in this fire, he must perform all his household duties for many days. ॥ 21 ॥

## And by that very act, his morning oblation is considered offered. ॥ 22 ॥

'**And**' <note>original: "ca"</note> furthermore, '**by that very act**' <note>original: "tena eva"</note>—either by placing the final fuel stick or by the marriage offerings—this '**morning oblation**' <note>original: "prātarāhutiḥ"</note> is '**offered**' <note>original: "hutā"</note>; it is considered accomplished. The meaning is that on that day, another morning oblation is not required. The word '**iti**' indicates the conclusion of the section on establishing the sacred fire. ॥ 22 ॥

### Now, the time for the daily offerings, etc.—

**Commentary:** And this 'final offering' <note>original: "antyāhuti"</note> or 'puffed rice offering' <note>original: "lājāhuti"</note> itself serves as his morning...

---

* <note>original: "यह अर्थवाद है।"</note> This is a rhetorical praise-statement <note>original: <term>Arthavada<note>A technical term in Sanskrit hermeneutics for a passage that explains or praises a rule rather than stating a literal command; it is meant to encourage a certain choice.</note></term></note>.

<summary>This page concludes the instructions for establishing the domestic fire (Grihyagni), giving the householder the choice between fire from a merchant's home for prosperity or fire from friction-drills for merit. It establishes that the fire used during the wedding or the final establishment ritual becomes the official household fire, and that ritual act counts as the first daily morning offering.</summary>

<keywords>Agnyadhana, Arani, Grihya fire, morning oblation, Laja-homa, marriage ritual, spiritual merit vs prosperity, Arthavada</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on establishing the domestic fire. Having established the fire through specific rituals (or fire-drills), the text now transitions into the daily maintenance of that fire and the specific timing of the first official offerings that mark the beginning of the householder's ritual life.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra —</header>

...the offering will be accomplished; on that day, a second morning offering is not necessary. ॥ 22 ॥

### From this point forward, the offering in the domestic fire is prescribed, beginning specifically with the evening offering. ॥ 23 ॥

The morning offering for that day is already accomplished by the establishment ritual itself, but the evening offering of that same day must still be specifically instructed. Therefore, I say: '**beginning with the evening offering**' <note>original: "sāyamāhutyupakrama eva"</note>. '**From this point forward**' <note>original: "ataūrdhvaṃ"</note> means after the instruction on establishing the fire. '**In the domestic fire**' <note>original: "gṛhye'gnau"</note> means in that specific consecrated fire. '**The offering is prescribed**' <note>original: "homo vidhīyate"</note>—this means the method for both evening and morning offerings is now being taught. ॥ 23 ॥

**Commentary:** Even though the morning offering for that first day is considered complete through the establishment ceremony, the rule for the evening offering must still be taught; therefore, after this, the evening and morning <term>homa<note>A ritual of making offerings into a consecrated fire.</note></term> are described for all days in this domestic fire. ॥ 23 ॥

### Before the time of kindling the fire, one should fetch the water used for the service—clean and well-protected—for both evening and morning. ॥ 24 ॥

### Or perhaps only in the evening. ॥ 25 ॥

### Or one may take the water from a large jar or a water-pot. ॥ 26 ॥

### Having kindled the fire before sunset, one should make the evening offering once the sun has set. ॥ 27 ॥

### Having kindled the fire before sunrise, one should make the morning offering either when the sun has risen or before it has risen. ॥ 28 ॥ 1 ॥

For ease of understanding, the commentary on the twenty-seventh and twenty-eighth sutras is given first, followed by the explanation of the twenty-fourth and subsequent sutras:

'**Before sunset**' <note>original: "astamayāt purā"</note>—as long as the sun has not yet set—one should '**kindle**' <note>original: "prāduṣkṛtya"</note> or brighten the '**fire**' <note>original: "agniṃ"</note>. Then, '**once the sun has set**' <note>original: "astamite"</note>, '**one should make the evening offering**' <note>original: "sāyamāhutiṃ juhuyāt"</note>. This explains only the timing for the evening offering (27). '**Before sunrise**' <note>original: "udayāt purā"</note>—as long as the sun has not yet risen—having '**kindled**' the fire, one should make the '**morning offering**' <note>original: "prātarāhutiṃ"</note> either '**when the sun has risen**' <note>original: "udite"</note> or '**before it has risen**' <note>original: "anudite"</note> at the moment of dawn. This explains only the timing for the morning offering (28). Both for '**evening**' <note>original: "sāyaṃ"</note> and '**morning**' <note>original: "prātaśca"</note>, '**before the time of kindling**' <note>original: "prāduṣkaraṇavelāyāḥ purā"</note>—that is, before the time the fire is brightened—one should '**fetch**' <note>original: "āharet"</note> the '**water**' <note>original: "apaḥ"</note> which is '**well-protected**' <note>original: "anuguptāḥ"</note> (safe and pure) and '**used for the service**' <note>original: "paricaraṇīyāḥ"</note> (suitable for ritual sipping and cleaning) (24). '**Or perhaps**' <note>original: "apivā"</note> one should fetch the water only once a day in the '**evening**' before the time of kindling the fire; by that...

<meta>catchword: by that</meta>

<meta>This page concludes the first section of Chapter 1, which details the logistics of water and fire maintenance, and begins the second section focused on the "sacred thread" (yajñopavīta), an essential ritual garment for the householder.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>Chapter 1, Section 2, Verse 1 ] The Rule for the Sacred Thread ॥</header>

The morning actions such as the ceremonial sipping of water and other rites must be performed, but one should not have to go out and fetch water again in the morning (25). **"Or"** <note>original: "apivā"</note>—alternatively, one may fetch protected water just once, either in the evening or in the morning before the time of kindling the fire, and store it in a large jar or a water pot; then, every day, one should **"take"** <note>original: "gṛhṇīyāt"</note> from that same **"jar"** or **"water pot"** for the evening and morning rites. ॥ 26 ॥

### This concludes the commentary on the first section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra. ॥ 1, 1 ॥

<term>Commentary</term>:- In the evening—before sunset—one should kindle that preserved fire well and make offerings into it once the sun has set. ॥ 27 ॥ In the morning—before sunrise—one should kindle that same preserved fire and make offerings after the sun has risen, or while it is rising. ॥ 28 ॥ In the evening and the morning (at both times), one should fetch protected water suitable for sipping and other rituals before the time the fire is kindled. ॥ 24 ॥ Or, by bringing this water once in the evening, the work for both times can be accomplished. ॥ 25 ॥ Or one should bring this water one day in the evening or morning before kindling the fire and keep it in a large jar or a water pot (a large vessel for holding water); later, one should take water from it as needed every evening and morning (26) *27-28 ॥

The translation of the first section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra is complete. ।1।१।

---

->Now, the Rule for the Sacred Thread.<-

# He makes the sacred thread from thread, a garment, or even a rope of Kusha grass. ॥ 1 ॥

It was previously stated that "one should act while wearing the sacred thread" <note>original: "yajñopavītinā kṛtyam"</note>—now, that sacred thread itself is being taught—
**"Thread,"** **"or"** <note>original: "vā"</note> a **"garment,"** **"or even"** <note>original: "apivā"</note> a **"rope of Kusha grass"** <note>A sacred grass used in Hindu rituals.</note>; whatever is easily available at that time and place, that should be made into the **"sacred thread"** <note>original: "yajñopavītaṃ"</note>. **"He makes"** <note>original: "kurute"</note>—<term>this is a form in the Vedic subjunctive mood</term> <note>original: "leṭorūpamidam"</note>, meaning "he should make." ॥ 1 ॥

---

<margin>*</margin> For ease of understanding, the translation of verses 27 and 28 was provided first, followed by the translation of verses 24, 25, and 26.

<summary>This page concludes the instructions on the daily fire and water rituals and introduces the requirements for the sacred thread (yajñopavīta), noting that it can be made of thread, cloth, or kusha grass depending on availability.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the first section to the second section of Chapter 1 of the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra. While the previous page discussed the preparation of ritual water and fire, this page focuses on the physical requirements and the method of wearing the sacred thread (yajñopavīta), which changes position based on whether the ritual is for the gods or for the ancestors.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra—</header>

Commentary:—A cotton thread, or a cord, or even a rope made of *kuśa* grass <note>a sacred grass used in Hindu rituals</note>—whichever is easily obtained at that time—one should perform the work with that as the sacred thread. ॥ 1 ॥

## Lifting the right arm, placing the thread over the head, and establishing it on the left shoulder so it hangs down under the right armpit—in this way, one is "Yajñopavītī" [invested with the sacred thread]. ॥ 2 ॥

In this verse, **"lifting the right arm"** means raising it up; **"placing over the head"** means wrapping it around; **"on the left shoulder"** means setting it upon the left shoulder; and **"it hangs down under the right armpit"** means it should hang beneath the interior of the right armpit. **"In this way,"** by wearing any of the aforementioned materials such as the thread, one becomes **"Yajñopavītī."** In this context, the characteristics of the *Prācīnāvītī* style are also described—

Commentary:—By placing the thread on the right shoulder, wrapping it over the head, and wearing it so it hangs from the left shoulder down below the right armpit—he who wears the thread in any of these three manners is called a "Yajñopavītī" <note>This refers to the standard orientation for rituals dedicated to the gods</note>. ॥ 2 ॥ *

## Lifting the left arm, placing the thread over the head, and establishing it on the right shoulder so it hangs down under the left armpit—in this way, one is "Prācīnāvītī." ॥ 3 ॥

**"Left"** <note>original: "savyam"</note> means the left side. All other terms should be interpreted as in the previous verse. ॥ 3 ॥

Commentary:—In the same way, by placing the sacred thread over the left shoulder, wrapping it over the head, and wearing it so it hangs from the right shoulder down to below the left armpit—he who wears the thread in any of these three manners is called a "Prācīnāvītī" <note>This orientation is used specifically for rites concerning the deceased and ancestors</note>. ॥ 3 ॥

## But in the ancestral sacrifice, one must indeed be a "Prācīnāvītī." ॥ 4 ॥

**"In the ancestral sacrifice"** <note>original: "pitṛyajñe"</note> refers to rituals such as the *Śrāddha* <note>a ceremony performed in honor of a deceased ancestor</note>; in such cases **"indeed"** one **"must be a Prācīnāvītī."** From this, it is naturally understood that in situations other than rituals for the gods or the ancestors, one should remain in the *nivītī* <note>wearing the thread suspended like a necklace around the neck</note> position. ॥ 4 ॥

Now begins the rule for the ritual sipping of water and the rule for touching water—

Any regulations regarding the length of the thread found in other branches of scripture or texts that contradict this are not to be accepted by the twice-born <term>Dvija (twice-born)</term> who follow the Kauthuma branch of the Sāmaveda. ॥ 2 ॥

<note>Technical Terms Used: 
**Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra**: An ancient manual of domestic rites.
**Yajñopavītī**: The state of wearing the sacred thread over the left shoulder (for gods).
**Prācīnāvītī**: The state of wearing the sacred thread over the right shoulder (for ancestors).
**Pitṛyajña**: Sacrifice or ritual dedicated to the ancestors.
**Kauthuma Branch of the Sāmaveda**: A specific traditional school of Vedic recitation and ritual practice.</note>

Having previously defined the two primary ways of wearing the sacred thread—Yajñopavītī for the gods and Prācīnāvītī for the ancestors—the text now clarifies the third position, Nivītī, used for everyday life. It then moves into the specific sequence of Ācamana, the ritual sipping of water for purification, which is a prerequisite for all Vedic ceremonies.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>Chapter 1, Section 2, Verses 2-9 | The Procedure for Ritual Sipping (Ācamana) ॥</header>

Commentary:—In rituals specifically for the ancestors <note>pitṛ-yajña</note>, one must be "Prācīnāvītī" <note>wearing the sacred thread over the right shoulder</note>. From this, it is established that except during rituals for the gods and rituals for the ancestors, at all other times one should be "Nivītī" <note>wearing the thread suspended from the neck like a necklace</note> ॥ 4 ॥ <insert>+</insert>

## Moving to the north of the fire, having washed the hands and feet and sat down, one should sip water three times and wipe the lips twice. ॥ 5 ॥
## Having sprinkled the feet with water, one should sprinkle the head. ॥ 6 ॥
## One should touch the sense organs with water. ॥ 7 ॥
## Namely: the eyes, the nostrils, and the ears. ॥ 8 ॥
## Whatever else may require attention should also be touched with water. ॥ 9 ॥

It is stated: "The work must be performed only after one has sipped water" <note>ācāntodakenaiva kṛtyam</note>—now, the specific procedure and duties for that are being taught—

From the **"fire"**, moving to the **"north"** <note>udaṅ</note>, having **"moved"** by walking there, having **"washed the hands and feet"** and **"having sat down"**, one should **"sip"** water **"three times"**; one should **"wipe"** the lips **"twice"**, meaning to clean away any water clinging to the upper or lower lips. Then, **"having sprinkled the feet"**, following the sprinkling of the feet, one **"should sprinkle the head"**. Then the **"eyes"**, referring to the two eyeballs; then the **"nostrils"**, meaning the two nasal cavities; and then the **"ears"**, meaning the two external ear canals—these six **"sense organs"** one should **"touch"** **"with water"**. After that, **"whatever"** other part of the body **"may require attention"** <note>original: mīmāṃsyam; referring to parts that might be impure or require mental focus</note>, **"that too"** should be **"touched"** with water. 5—9

---

<insert>+</insert>—In the second verse of this general ritual procedure section, it was established that one should be "Yajñopavītī" <note>thread over the left shoulder</note> when performing rituals for the gods. In this specific context, the arrangement for being "Prācīnāvītī" during ancestor rituals has been made. From this, it follows that during times when no ancestor or divine ritual is being performed—or while attending to bodily functions—there is no requirement to remain in the Yajñopavītī or Prācīnāvītī positions. This also proves that when one is not occupied with divine or ancestral work, it is appropriate to remain "Nivītī." The sage Manu says—"The Nivītī is worn suspended from the neck" <note>original: 'nivītī kaṇṭha sajjane', Manusmṛti 2.63</note>, meaning that one who wears the sacred thread like a garland or necklace around the neck is called "Nivītī" ॥ 5 ॥

<meta>This page continues the detailed instructions for Ācamana (ritual purification by sipping water). The previous page concluded the basic physical actions—sipping three times, wiping the lips twice, and sprinkling the head and sense organs. This page introduces specific prohibitions and behavioral restrictions during the ritual as cited by various authorities (ācāryas).</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra—</header>

Commentary:—Having moved slightly to the north of the ritual fire, and having washed both hands and feet, one should sit in an appropriate place and perform ritual sipping <term>Ācamana|The ritual sipping of water for internal purification before a ceremony</term> three times. After that, one should wipe away the water clinging to the upper and lower lips twice. Following this, water should be sprinkled on both feet and the forehead. Then, one should touch water to the locations of the six sense organs—namely, both eyes, both nostrils, and both ears. Furthermore, one should touch any other limb with water that one desires to purify. ॥ 5, 6, 7, 8, 9 ॥

### Concerning this, they say:— ॥ 10 ॥

"Concerning this" <note>tatra</note> refers to the subject of ritual sipping. "They say" <note>āhuḥ</note> refers to certain teachers who state the following rules—beginning with "this" <note>etat</note> and continuing through to the end of the section which states "otherwise one remains impure" <note>original Sanskrit: 'ucchistho haivāto'nyathā bhavati', Verse 30</note>. ॥ 10 ॥

Commentary:—In the matter of this ritual sipping, certain teachers <term>Āchārya|Ancient scholars or spiritual teachers</term> say:— ॥ 10 ॥

### One should not perform ritual sipping while walking. ॥ 11 ॥

"While walking" <note>vrajan</note> means wandering here and there. One should not perform the purification <note>na upaspṛśet</note> in this state. ॥ 11 ॥

Commentary:—One should not perform ritual sipping while moving about. ॥ 11 ॥

### Nor while standing. ॥ 12 ॥

"While standing" <note>tiṣṭhan</note> means remaining in an upright position; it follows from the previous rule that one should not sip water in this way. ॥ 12 ॥

Commentary:—One should *also* not perform ritual sipping while standing up. ॥ 12 ॥

### Nor while laughing. ॥ 13 ॥

"Nor while laughing" <note>na hasan</note> means while engaged in laughter, one should not perform the ritual. ॥ 13 ॥

Commentary:—One should *also* not perform ritual sipping while laughing. ॥ 13 ॥

### Nor while looking around. ॥ 14 ॥

"Looking around" <note>vilokayan</note> means gazing at something else; one should not perform the ritual while distracted. ॥ 14 ॥

Commentary:—One should not perform ritual sipping while looking here and there (being absent-minded). ॥ 14 ॥

### Nor while unbowed. ॥ 15 ॥

"Unbowed" <note>apraṇataḥ</note> refers to being in a haughty state due to anger, pride, or hypocrisy; in such a state, one should not perform the ritual. ॥ 15 ॥

Commentary:—One should not perform ritual sipping if one is not humble due to anger, vanity, or similar states. ॥ 15 ॥

### Nor with the fingers. ॥ 16 ॥

"With the fingers" <note>aṅgulībhiḥ</note> refers to the practice of taking water only on the tips of the fingers with a misguided intent; one should not perform the ritual in this manner. ॥ 16 ॥

<vocab>Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra, Ācamana, Bhāṣya (Commentary), Upaspṛśet (To touch/sip for purification), Āchārya</vocab>

<summary>This page lists specific behavioral prohibitions for the ritual of Ācamana, forbidding the practitioner from sipping water while walking, standing, laughing, being distracted, or acting with pride.</summary>
<keywords>ritual purity, Ācamana, etiquette, Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra, Vedic purification, mindfulness in ritual</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific prohibitions regarding Ācamana (ritual purification) from the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra. While the previous page addressed physical movements (like walking), this page details the quality of the water, the specific parts of the hand to be used, and the proper attire and posture required for the ritual to be valid.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>The Rules for Ritual Sipping ॥</header>
<meta>Chapter 1, Section 2, Sūtras 17-23</meta>

Commentary:—One should not perform ritual sipping <term>Ācamana|Ritual purification by sipping water</term> by taking water with the tips of the fingers (with an improper or disrespectful mindset). ॥ 16 ॥

### Not by an unprescribed part of the hand. ॥ 17 ॥

"By an unprescribed part" <note>original Sanskrit: atīrthena</note> refers to the parts of the hand not designated as sacred <term>Tīrtha|Specific areas of the hand used for different ritual purposes</term>, such as the "Brahma-tīrtha" (the base of the thumb) and others described by sages like Manu. One should "not" perform the ritual sipping through any other path or method. ॥ 17 ॥

Commentary:—One should not perform ritual sipping through an unprescribed method (such as taking water from a metal vessel directly by the mouth or pouring it straight into the throat). ॥ 17 ॥

### Not noisily. ॥ 18 ॥

"Noisily" <note>original Sanskrit: saśabdaṃ</note> refers to making a sound as if one were playing. One who is doing so should "not" perform the ritual sipping. ॥ 18 ॥

Commentary:—One should not perform ritual sipping with a slurping sound (as if playing in the water). ॥ 18 ॥

### Not without looking. ॥ 19 ॥

"Without looking" <note>original Sanskrit: anavekṣitam</note> means taking water in the hand without first examining it to see if it contains visible insects or other impurities. One should "not" perform the ritual sipping in this manner. ॥ 19 ॥

Commentary:—One should not perform ritual sipping without first carefully inspecting the water. ॥ 19 ॥

### Not with shoulders outside the knees. ॥ 20 ॥

"With shoulders outside" <note>original Sanskrit: bāhyāṃsaḥ</note> refers to a person whose shoulders <note>skandhau</note> are positioned outside the span of their two knees. Being in such a posture (hunched or crooked), one should "not" perform the ritual sipping. ॥ 20 ॥

Commentary:—One should not perform ritual sipping while the shoulders are positioned outside the knees (due to a bent or improper body posture). ॥ 20 ॥

### Not by treating a portion of the lower garment as an upper garment. ॥ 21 ॥

"Of a portion of the lower garment" <note>original Sanskrit: antarīyaka-deśasya</note> refers to taking one end of the cloth one is already wearing as a waist-wrap and "treating it as an upper garment" <note>original Sanskrit: uttarīyatāṃ kalpayitvā</note> by draping it over the shoulder. One should "not" perform ritual sipping in this state. ॥ 21 ॥

Commentary:—One should not perform ritual sipping while wearing only a single cloth and attempting to use a part of it to serve as the required upper garment. ॥ 21 ॥ <note>The Hindi commentary here is numbered 22 in the source, likely a scribal or printing error in the original manuscript.</note>

### Not with hot water. ॥ 22 ॥

"With hot" <note>original Sanskrit: uṣṇābhiḥ</note> refers to water that has been heated by fire or other means. One should "not" perform the ritual sipping with such water. ॥ 22 ॥

Commentary:—One should not perform ritual sipping with hot water. ॥ 22 ॥

### Not with foamy water. ॥ 23 ॥

"With foamy" <note>original Sanskrit: saphenābhiḥ</note> refers to water that is dirty or contains froth. One should "not" perform the ritual sipping with such water. ॥ 23 ॥

<vocab>Ācamana-vidhiḥ, Ācamana, Tīrtha, Brāhmādikaṃ, Saśabdam, Anavekṣitam, Bāhyāṃsaḥ, Antarīyaka-deśa, Uṣṇābhiḥ, Saphenābhiḥ</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines seven specific prohibitions for the ritual of Ācamana, forbidding the use of improper parts of the hand, making noise, failing to inspect the water, improper posture, improper dress, and using hot or foamy water. These rules emphasize that purification must be performed with mindfulness, cleanliness, and physical decorum.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the instructions regarding the restrictions and proper method for Ācamana (ritual purification by sipping water). The previous page concluded with instructions on the quality of the water; this page begins with a prohibition against foamy water and moves into physical requirements regarding clothing, posture, and the depth to which the water must be swallowed.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>On the Commentary of the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra—</header>

Commentary:—One should not perform ritual sipping with foamy water. ॥ 23 ॥

### Nor wearing shoes anywhere. ॥ 24 ॥

The word "and" <note>original Sanskrit: ca</note> implies "also." "Anywhere" <note>original Sanskrit: kvacit</note> refers to specific locations where it is inappropriate. "Wearing shoes" <note>original Sanskrit: sopānatkaḥ</note> means being equipped with footwear; one should "not" perform the purification <note>original Sanskrit: upaspṛśet</note> in this state. ॥ 24 ॥

Commentary:—And in prohibited places, one should not perform ritual sipping while wearing shoes on both feet. ॥ 24 ॥

### With something tied to the head. ॥ 25 ॥

### Tied at the neck. ॥ 26 ॥

### And not while stretching out both feet. ॥ 27 ॥

One who has a binding or attachment on the head is "with something tied to the head" <note>original Sanskrit: kāsaktikaḥ</note>. "At the neck" <note>original Sanskrit: gale</note> refers to being "tied" <note>original Sanskrit: baddhaḥ</note> in the throat region in such a way that it might obstruct the downward flow of water. "And" <note>original Sanskrit: ca</note> also, "stretching out" <note>original Sanskrit: prasārya</note> "both feet," one should "not" perform the purification. ॥ 25—27 ॥

Commentary:—One should not perform ritual sipping while there is a tight binding on the head or neck, or while stretching out both feet. ॥ 25, 26, 27 ॥

### By sipping again at the end, one becomes pure. ॥ 28 ॥

"At the end" <note>original Sanskrit: antataḥ</note> means after finishing an action or before starting an action that has not yet begun, or after waking from sleep and so on. "By sipping again" <note>original Sanskrit: pratyupaspṛśya</note>, following the specific procedure of repeated sipping to be described later, "one becomes pure" <note>original Sanskrit: śucirbhavati</note>. ॥ 28 ॥

Commentary:—Upon waking from sleep and at other such times, one will achieve purity by sipping water again. ॥ 28 ॥

### One should indeed sip water that reaches the heart. ॥ 29 ॥

Regarding the quantity of water for sipping—"reaching the heart" <note>original Sanskrit: hṛdayaspṛśaḥ</note> means as much as, when swallowed, reaches the heart. Only that amount of "water" <note>original Sanskrit: āpaḥ</note> should one "sip" <note>original Sanskrit: ācāmet</note>. ॥ 29 ॥

Commentary:—One must perform ritual sipping with at least as much water as is required to moisten the path down to the heart. ॥ 29 ॥

### Otherwise, one remains impure. ॥ 30 ॥

"Otherwise than this" <note>original Sanskrit: ato'nyathā</note> means sipping in a manner different from what has been prescribed. In that case, one "remains" <note>original Sanskrit: bhavati</note> "impure" <note>term: Ucchiṣṭa|A state of ritual impurity or being 'soiled' by food or saliva</note> "indeed" <note>original Sanskrit: ha</note>, which signifies certainty. This connects back to the phrase "Thus they say" from the first Sūtra. ॥ 30 ॥

Commentary:—If this is not done (if the water does not reach the heart), the person remains in a state of ritual impurity. ॥ 30 ॥

<summary>This page details the physical requirements for the purification ritual (Ācamana), prohibiting shoes, head/neck bindings, and improper posture, while defining the necessary water volume as that which reaches the heart.</summary>
<keywords>Ācamana, ritual purity, footwear prohibitions, posture, heart-touching water, Ucchiṣṭa, Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific rules for Ācamana (ritual sipping), transitioning from the physical posture of the practitioner to the specific circumstances that necessitate a second round of purification, known as Pratyupaspārśana.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>Ch. 1 Sec. 2 Su. 23-32 ] Rules for Ritual Sipping and Re-touching ॥</header>

## Now for the Supplementary Purifications ॥ 31 ॥

"Now" <note>original Sanskrit: atha</note> indicates the transition to the next topic. "Supplementary Purifications" <note>original Sanskrit: pratyupaspārśanāni; literally "re-touchings"</note> refers to the question: under what circumstances does ritual sipping take the specific name of "re-touching"? This is to be explained next. ॥ 31 ॥

Commentary:—What are the specific instances where ritual sipping is called "re-touching"? That is now described. ॥ 31 ॥

# After sleeping, eating, sneezing, bathing, drinking, or changing clothes,
# and after traversing a road or a cremation ground, even if one has already sipped water, one should sip again. ॥ 32 ॥

"After sleeping" <note>original Sanskrit: suptvā</note> refers to the time after waking from slumber (1). "After eating" <note>original Sanskrit: bhuktvā</note> refers to after consuming a meal (2). "After sneezing" <note>original Sanskrit: kṣutvā</note> refers to the moment following a sneeze (3). "After bathing" <note>original Sanskrit: snātvā</note> refers to after a bath (4). "After drinking" <note>original Sanskrit: pītvā</note> refers to after consuming a beverage (5). "After changing" <note>original Sanskrit: vi-paridhāya</note> refers to after putting on garments or ornaments (6). "And" <note>original Sanskrit: ca</note> indicates further conditions. "Cremation ground" <note>original Sanskrit: śmaśānam</note> and "Road" <note>original Sanskrit: rathyām</note>—referring to a village street (7). "Traversing" <note>original Sanskrit: ākramya</note> means after walking through such places (8). "Even if one has already sipped" <note>original Sanskrit: ācantaḥ ca</note>—even if one has previously performed the purification for the sake of a sacrifice or ritual—"one should sip again" <note>original Sanskrit: punarācāmet</note>, meaning one must perform a second ritual sipping. 

The essential principle <note>original Sanskrit: tattvam</note> is this: one must sip water after sleeping and so on; in those cases, only one act of sipping is required. However, if a person has already begun a ritual and *then* happens to sleep or sneeze, a second ritual sipping is required. It is this specific instance of repeated purification that is termed "re-touching" <note>term: Pratyupaspārśana — a secondary purification required when a primary state of ritual purity is interrupted by natural bodily functions</note>. ॥ 32 ॥

->**Thus ends the commentary on the second section**<-
->**of the first chapter in the Illustrious Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra. 1, 2**<-

Commentary:—So, upon rising from sleep, after eating food, after a hiccup <note>The Hindi commentary interprets the Sanskrit 'kṣutvā' (sneezing) as a hiccup or similar reflex</note>, after bathing, after drinking juices, or to relieve the fatigue of putting on clothes and ornaments...

---

<note>
‡ The ritual sipping performed after sleeping and other such acts is called "re-touching." This means that upon waking, ritual sipping must certainly be performed. If, while performing some divine ritual or ceremony, one experiences laziness, drowsiness, or any kind of eating or sneezing, one should perform the sipping again in those instances. One should not think, "I have already performed the ritual sipping once, so what is the need for it again?" Similarly, after wandering in a street or in a place where corpses are cremated, or if ritual sipping was required previously for some other task, one should sip again in these situations. ॥ 32 ॥
</note>

->of the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra is complete. ॥ 1, 2 ॥<-

->**(Thus ends the section on general common rites)**<-

<summary>This page defines 'Pratyupaspārśana' (supplementary ritual sipping) and lists the specific interruptions—such as sleeping, eating, sneezing, or walking in impure places—that necessitate repeating the purification rite during a ceremony.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from general rules of purification (ācamana) to the specific procedures for preparing the sacred fire, beginning the section on the Brahma-Yajña. The text outlines the preliminary steps of sweeping and sprinkling water (Paryyukṣaṇa) around the fire altar.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>
<header>In the commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra—</header>

## ( Now, the Section on the Brahma-Yajña )
<note>Brahma-Yajña literally means the "Sacrifice to Brahman." In this context, it refers to the daily ritual study of the Vedas and the offerings associated with it. The section begins by detailing how to prepare the ritual space.</note>

# Having established the fire and swept around it, one should bend the right knee and pour a handful of water to the south of the fire, saying, "O Aditi, permit me." ॥ 1 ॥

"The fire" <note>original Sanskrit: agnim</note> refers to the fire established in the manner previously described (1, 27-28). "Having established" <note>original Sanskrit: upasamādhāya</note> means to properly set the fire in place. "Having swept around" <note>original Sanskrit: parisamūhya</note> refers to the ritual cleaning of the area around the altar with a specific mantra. "One whose right knee is bent" <note>original Sanskrit: dakṣiṇajānvaktaḥ</note> describes the practitioner whose right knee is touching the ground. "O Aditi, permit me" <note>original Sanskrit: adite'numanyasva</note>—O Goddess Aditi! Grant permission for the performance of this ritual. With this mantra, while keeping the fire to one's right, "one should pour a handful of water" <note>original Sanskrit: udakāñjaliṃ prasiñcet</note>. ॥ 1 ॥

Commentary:—After establishing the fire as previously directed (1, 27-28) and sweeping the area, rest the right knee on the ground and say, "O Aditi! Give me permission to perform this task." With this mantra, pour a handful of water on the southern side of the fire. ॥ 1 ॥

# Saying, "O Anumati, permit me," to the West. ॥ 2 ॥

"O Anumati, permit me"—O Goddess Anumati! Grant permission for this ritual. With this mantra, one should pour a handful of water "behind" <note>original Sanskrit: paścāt</note> the fire, meaning on its western side. ॥ 2 ॥

Commentary:—"O Anumati! Give me permission to perform this task"—with this mantra, pour a second handful of water on the back [Western] part of the fire. ॥ 2 ॥

# Saying, "O Sarasvatī, permit me," to the North. ॥ 3 ॥

"O Sarasvatī, permit me"—O Goddess Sarasvatī! Grant permission for this ritual. With this mantra, one should pour a handful of water to the "North" <note>original Sanskrit: uttarataḥ</note> of the fire. ॥ 3 ॥

Commentary:—And saying, "O Sarasvatī! Give me permission to perform this task," with this mantra, perform the third sprinkling of a handful of water to the North of the fire. ॥ 3 ॥

# Saying, "O Divine Savitṛ, impel the sacrifice," one should sprinkle water clockwise around the fire once or thrice. ॥ 4 ॥

"O Divine Savitṛ, impel the sacrifice, impel the lord of the sacrifice for good fortune. May the celestial Gandharva, the purifier of thought, purify our thoughts. May the Lord of Speech make our words sweet" <note>original Sanskrit: deva savitaḥ prasuva... This full mantra is cited from the Samhitā Brāhmaṇa 1, 1.</note>—with this mantra, one should "sprinkle water around" <note>original Sanskrit: paryyukṣeta</note> the fire in a clockwise direction <note>original Sanskrit: pradakṣiṇam</note>, either "once" <note>original Sanskrit: sakṛd vā</note> or "thrice" <note>original Sanskrit: trir vā</note>. This is to be done with continuous streams of water. ॥ 4 ॥

Commentary:—One or three times, using the mantra "O Divine Savitṛ, impel," pour a stream of water around the fire in a clockwise direction. This ritual act is known as <term>Paryyukṣaṇa: the ceremonial sprinkling of water to sanctify the space around the altar</term>. ॥ 4 ॥

# While performing the sprinkling and alternating the limbs of the ritual... for the oblation... ॥ 5 ॥

"Ending with the sprinkling" <note>original Sanskrit: paryyukṣaṇāntān</note> refers to the various preparatory steps of the ritual. "Alternating" <note>original Sanskrit: vyatiharann</note> refers to the specific sequence or handling of these parts. "For the oblation" <note>original Sanskrit: homīyam</note>—this refers to the items intended for sacrifice...

<meta>catchword: homī- (pertaining to the oblation)</meta>

<summary>This page details the preliminary purification of the sacrificial fire area, involving sweeping and the ritual sprinkling of water (Paryyukṣaṇa) in four directions while invoking the goddesses Aditi, Anumati, Sarasvatī, and the god Savitṛ.</summary>
<keywords>Brahma-Yajña, Aditi, Anumati, Sarasvatī, Savitṛ, Paryyukṣaṇa, ritual sprinkling, Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra, fire preparation</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific ritual procedures for the Brahma-Yajña. The previous page concluded with Sūtra 2, detailing the initial water offerings to the cardinal directions. This page picks up with the final step of the purification (Sūtra 5) and moves into the instructions for the actual fire oblation (Homa).</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>[ Chapter 1, Section 3, Sūtras 1–8 ] The Section on the Brahma-Yajña || 17</header>

...having collected all the necessary ritual items, "sprinkling" <note>original Sanskrit: abhiparyukṣan</note> means moistening them with drops of water. ॥ 5 ॥

Commentary:—After completing the preliminary actions up to the ritual sprinkling around the altar, one should then sprinkle drops of water on the food grains and other items intended for the sacrifice. This act of purification is known as "Paryukṣaṇa." ॥ 5 ॥

# 6. Now, one should sacrifice the oblation of food in the fire, whether it be cooked or uncooked.

"Now" <note>original Sanskrit: atha</note> means thereafter. "In the fire" <note>original Sanskrit: agnau</note> refers to the ritual fire already established. "Cooked" <note>original Sanskrit: kṛtasya</note> means boiled or prepared, while "Uncooked" <note>original Sanskrit: akṛtasya</note> means raw. "Oblation" <note>original Sanskrit: haviṣyasya</note> refers to a portion of food such as barley <note>original Sanskrit: yava</note> or similar grains. One should then "sacrifice" <note>original Sanskrit: juhuyāt</note> it. ॥ 6 ॥

Commentary:—Following the purification, one should offer the sacrificial material—whether it has been cooked or is still raw—into the fire. ॥ 6 ॥

# 7. If it is uncooked, one should sacrifice it after washing it and making it wet with water.

If the "uncooked" <note>original Sanskrit: akṛtam</note> material is used, one must first "wash" <note>original Sanskrit: prakṣālya</note> it with water, and while it is still "wet" <note>original Sanskrit: prodakaṃ</note>, "sacrifice" <note>original Sanskrit: juhuyāt</note> it. ॥ 7 ॥

Commentary:—If the material intended for the fire is not something like cooked rice, but rather raw rice grains or fruits, then these must be thoroughly washed and offered into the fire while they are still damp. ॥ 7 ॥

# 8. Now if it be curd, milk, or gruel, [sacrifice] with a vessel of bell-metal, or a pot for sacrificial food, or with a wooden ladle.

"Now" <note>original Sanskrit: atha</note> refers to a specific case: if "curd" <note>original Sanskrit: dadhi</note>, "milk" <note>original Sanskrit: payaḥ</note>, or "gruel" <note>original Sanskrit: yavāgū</note> is to be offered. In such cases, one should use a "bell-metal vessel" <note>original Sanskrit: kaṃsena</note>, a "sacrificial cooking pot" <note>original Sanskrit: carusthālyā</note>, or a "wooden ladle" <note>original Sanskrit: sruveṇa</note>. The offering should be made with these tools, not directly with the hand. ॥ 8 ॥

Commentary:—Specific Instruction: If the offering consists of liquids or semi-liquids like yogurt, milk, or thin rice gruel, they do not need to be washed. Instead, place them in a bell-metal vessel or a sacrificial pot and offer them using the vessel itself or a ritual ladle <note>term: sruvā; a small wooden ladle used for pouring clarified butter or liquid offerings</note>, rather than using one's hands. ॥ 8 ॥

# 9. In the evening, the first oblation is made in the middle with the words "Hail to Agni!" and the second silently in the North-East direction.

"In the middle" <note>original Sanskrit: madhye</note> of the fire, one offers the "first" <note>original Sanskrit: pūrvām</note> oblation with the mantra "Hail to Agni!" <note>original Sanskrit: agnaye svāhā</note>. The "second" <note>original Sanskrit: uttarām</note> oblation is offered in the "Unconquered" <note>original Sanskrit: aparājitāyām</note> direction—which refers to the North-East—and it is performed "silently" <note>original Sanskrit: tūṣṇīm</note>, without any mantra. This constitutes the "evening" <note>original Sanskrit: sāyam</note> sacrifice. ॥ 9 ॥

Commentary:—For the evening ritual, the first offering is poured into the center of the fire while reciting the mantra "Agnaye Svāhā." The second offering is then made in the North-East corner of the fire pit in silence. This completes the procedure for the evening oblation. ॥ 9 ॥

३ <note>Signature: 3</note>

**Vocabulary:**
<term>Paryukṣaṇa</term> (ritual sprinkling), <term>Haviṣya</term> (material fit for sacrifice), <term>Anna</term> (food/grain), <term>Akṛta</term> (uncooked/raw), <term>Dadhi</term> (curd), <term>Paya</term> (milk), <term>Yavāgū</term> (rice gruel), <term>Kāṃsyapātra</term> (bell-metal vessel), <term>Carusthālī</term> (pot for cooking sacrificial rice), <term>Sruva</term> (ritual ladle), <term>Āhuti</term> (oblation/offering), <term>Aparājitā</term> (the North-East direction), <term>Sāyam</term> (evening).

<summary>This page details the specific methods for offering food into the sacred fire, distinguishing between cooked and raw materials, and specifying the tools required for liquid offerings. It also provides the precise sequence and mantras for the evening oblation (Sāyaṃ-homa).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the detailed instructions for the daily fire sacrifice (Homa) within the Gobhila Gṛhyasūtra. The previous page concluded the evening ritual procedure and the specific methods for washing and preparing the sacrificial food. This page shifts to the morning ritual variations and the closing actions for both the morning and evening sacrifices.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhyasūtra—</header>

## 10. Now, in the morning: the first oblation is with "Hail to the Sun!", and the second is silent, offered in the middle and towards the Northeast.

"Now in the morning—" <note>original Sanskrit: atha prātaḥ</note> refers to the first oblation being offered with the mantra "To the Sun, Hail!" <note>original Sanskrit: sūryāya svāhā</note>. All other aspects of the ritual are the same as the previously described [evening] ritual. ॥ 10 ॥

**Explanation:** The procedure for the morning fire sacrifice is conducted in the same manner as the evening ritual. The only distinction is that instead of the mantra "To Fire, Hail!" <note>original Sanskrit: agnaye svāhā</note>, the oblation is offered with the mantra "To the Sun, Hail!" ॥ 10 ॥

---

## 11. Having placed a fuel stick and sprinkled water around as before, one should pour out handfuls of water; the specific change in the mantra is "You have permitted."

In both the evening and morning rituals, after the primary offerings—one should place a "fuel stick" <note>term: Samidh|A small piece of wood, typically from a sacred tree, used as fuel for the ritual fire</note> into the fire without any mantra. Then, proceeding to "sprinkle water around" <note>original Sanskrit: anuparyukṣya</note> once more, one should "pour out handfuls of water" <note>original Sanskrit: udakāñjalīn</note> just as before. In this instance, "You have permitted" <note>original Sanskrit: anvamaṃsthāḥ</note>—this use of the past tense—is the "specific change" <note>original Sanskrit: mantra-viśeṣaḥ</note> that must be applied to the mantra. ॥ 11 ॥

**Explanation:** In both the evening and morning rituals, after the offerings are completed, one should place a single fuel stick into the fire without reciting a mantra. Following this, one prepares to perform the "post-sprinkling" <note>term: Anuparyukṣaṇa|The ritual act of sprinkling water clockwise around the fire after the oblations are finished</note> by pouring handfuls of water as was done at the beginning. The specific detail here is that instead of the previous mantra [which asked for permission in the present/future tense], one should now use the form: "O Aditi! You have granted me permission for this work" (implying: I have performed the task according to your permission). This change in wording is the unique feature of this step. ॥ 11 ॥

---

## 12. Having circumambulated the fire clockwise, and having poured out the remaining water and refilled the ritual cup, one should set it down and proceed as required.

After the aforementioned post-sprinkling—having "circumambulated" <note>term: Pradakṣiṇa|The act of walking around a sacred object or person in a clockwise direction as a mark of respect</note> the "fire" <note>original Sanskrit: agniṃ</note>—one should "pour out" <note>original Sanskrit: ninīya</note> the "remainder" of the "water" <note>original Sanskrit: apām śeṣam</note> that was kept in the pitcher or collected. Then, "refilling" <note>original Sanskrit: pūrayitvā</note> the "ritual cup" <note>term: Chamasa|A wooden vessel or cup used in Vedic rituals for holding water or sacrificial liquids</note> with that same water and "setting it down" <note>original Sanskrit: pratiṣṭhāpya</note> or securing it, one should proceed "as required" <note>original Sanskrit: yathārtham</note> with the subsequent daily activities, such as the evening meal in the evening or the morning breakfast in the morning. ॥ 12 ॥

**Explanation:** Following the "post-sprinkling" described above, one should circumambulate the fire in a clockwise direction...

<vocab>Gobhila Gṛhyasūtra, Prātaḥ (Morning), Sūryāya Svāhā (Hail to the Sun), Samidham (Fuel stick), Anuparyukṣaṇa (Post-sprinkling), Pradakṣiṇa (Clockwise circumambulation), Chamasa (Ritual cup), Mantra-viśeṣa (Mantra variation)</vocab>

<summary>This page details the morning fire sacrifice (Homa) protocol, emphasizing the mantra change to Surya (the Sun) and describing the "Anuparyukṣaṇa" or post-offering water ritual which involves a grammatical shift in the mantras to signify the completion of the act.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the daily fire ritual (Homa) in the Gobhila Gṛhyasūtra. Having established the morning and evening mantras on the previous page, the text now details the lifelong obligation of these rites, the role of the wife, and the conclusion of the ritual meal.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>
-> [Section 1, Part 3, Sutras 10-16] &emsp;&emsp; **Chapter on the Brahma-sacrifice** <note>This section describes the "Domestic Fire Rituals," often categorized under the broader scope of daily duties or Brahma-yajna.</note> <-

...having done so, pour the remainder of the water held in the hand into the sacrificial vessel <note>original Sanskrit: camasi</note> and set it aside for any necessary future tasks. ॥ 12 ॥

## 13. From this day forward, one should either perform the sacrifice in the domestic fire oneself or have it performed by another, until the end of life or the final sacrificial bath.

"From this day forward" <note>original Sanskrit: ataḥ ūrdhvam</note> refers to the time following the first day of establishing the fire. "Until the end of life or the final sacrificial bath" <note>original Sanskrit: ā jīvitāvabhṛthāt</note> refers to the duration of this duty; it lasts either for the rest of one's life or until the completion of a major sacrifice like the Horse Sacrifice <note>term: Ashvamedha|A grand royal Vedic sacrifice</note>, which concludes with a ritual bath <note>term: Avabhṛtha|The purifying bath taken at the end of a major Vedic ceremony</note>. Every day, both in the evening and the morning, one must perform the ritual "in this manner" <note>original Sanskrit: evam</note> in the "domestic fire" <note>term: Gṛhya Agni|The sacred fire kept in the home for daily rituals</note>. One may do this "oneself" <note>original Sanskrit: juhuyāt</note> or "have it performed" <note>original Sanskrit: hāvayet</note> by a representative <note>term: Pratinidhi|A substitute, such as a priest or a student, who performs a ritual on behalf of the householder</note>. ॥ 13 ॥

**Explanation:** From the very first day the fire is established, the householder is obligated to ensure the morning and evening oblations are made every single day. This duty only ends with death or the completion of a grander sacrificial cycle. If the householder cannot do it, they may appoint someone else to act on their behalf. ॥ 13 ॥

## 14. Now, they also cite a further rule. ॥ 14 ॥

"Now" <note>original Sanskrit: atha</note> regarding this subject, "they also cite" <note>original Sanskrit: udāharanti api</note>—meaning other authorities provide a specific rule regarding who may act as a substitute for the householder. ॥ 14 ॥

**Explanation:** Several authorities provide additional guidance specifically concerning who is permitted to perform these sacrifices as a representative. ॥ 14 ॥

## 15. At her will, the wife may perform the evening and morning oblations in the domestic fire, for the wife is the "home" and this is the "domestic" fire.

"This fire" <note>original Sanskrit: eṣaḥ agniḥ</note> is called "domestic" <note>original Sanskrit: gṛhyaḥ</note> because it is beneficial for the house. The "wife" <note>original Sanskrit: patnī</note> is herself called "the home" <note>original Sanskrit: gṛhāḥ</note>. Therefore, because of this connection, the "wife" may "at her will" <note>original Sanskrit: kāmaṃ</note>—if she so desires—perform both the "evening and morning oblations" <note>original Sanskrit: sāyaṃprātarhomau</note> as previously described. ॥ 15 ॥

**Explanation:** There is a linguistic and spiritual link between the wife and the home. Since she is considered the heart of the household, she has the inherent right to perform these daily fire offerings herself. ॥ 15 ॥

## 16. When the evening or morning meal is ready, one should have it announced: "It is prepared."

Following the ritual, when the "evening or morning meal" <note>original Sanskrit: sāyaṃ-āśa-prātaḥ-āśe</note> has been "prepared" <note>original Sanskrit: niṣṭhite</note>—meaning the cooking is complete—one should then announce: "It is prepared" <note>original Sanskrit: bhūtam</note>. This signifies that the duty to be performed at that time is now accomplished. ॥ 16 ॥

<summary>This page establishes that the daily fire sacrifice is a lifelong commitment that can be performed by the householder, a representative, or specifically the wife, and concludes with the announcement of the ritual meal.</summary>
<keywords>Gṛhya Agni, domestic fire, Homa, wife's role, Avabhṛtha, daily ritual, ritual meal, Gobhila Gṛhyasūtra</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the daily ritual duties from the Gobhila Gṛhyasūtra. The previous page concluded the rules for the daily fire ritual (Homa) and began discussing the duty of teaching, which constitutes the "Brahma-sacrifice." <page-num>20</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhyasūtra—</header>

Having deliberated or informed the disciples, he should "instruct" <note>original Sanskrit: pravācayet</note> them in their daily sacred study <note>term: Svādhyāya|The practice of reciting and studying Vedic texts</note>; "his own disciples" is the implied meaning. This teaching itself is the Brahma-sacrifice. ॥ 16 ॥

> **Translation:** Afterward, when the evening meal is ready in the evening and the morning meal is ready in the morning, he should have the students (disciples) study. (This is what is called the 'Brahma-sacrifice') ॥ 16 ॥

### 17. Apart from auspicious speech, having become pure— ॥ 17 ॥

### 18. He repeats "Om" aloud and "Salutation to Him, let them not destroy me" in a whisper. ॥ 18 ॥

During the time of the Brahma-sacrifice, if one utters any other worldly, secular sentence "apart from auspicious speech" <note>original Sanskrit: bhagayā vācā ṛte</note>—meaning the Vedic verses—one becomes "impure." To remove that impurity, he should cast out <note>original Sanskrit: kṣiptva</note> the word "Om" "aloud" <note>original Sanskrit: uccaiḥ</note> and then "in a whisper" <note>original Sanskrit: upāṃśu</note>—meaning softly—he repeats the two mantras: "Salutation to Him, let them not destroy me" <note>original Sanskrit: tasmai namas tanmākṣāḥ</note>. He should repeat this as many times as he happens to speak worldly words during the rite. ॥ 17-18 ॥

-> This concludes the explanation of the third section of the first chapter in the Gobhila Gṛhyasūtra. ॥ 1, 3 ॥ <-

> **Translation:** (During the time of the Brahma-sacrifice) by using any speech other than that which brings welfare, one becomes impure. ॥ 17 ॥

> Upon becoming impure through the use of profane speech, one should openly say "Om," and in one's mind, repeat the mantra: "Salutation to them, they do not apply this again." <note>Note: The Hindi translation interprets 'tanmākṣāḥ' as a prayer to prevent the recurrence of the error.</note> ॥ 18 ॥

-> The translation of the third section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Gṛhyasūtra is complete. ॥ 1, 3 ॥ <-

---

## 1. Now, restrained in speech, he should offer the food oblations.

The word "Now" <note>original Sanskrit: atha</note> indicates the start of a new topic. "Restrained in speech" <note>term: Vāgyataḥ|Observing ritual silence or controlled speech</note> means abandoning irregular speech such as laughter, joking, or falsehood. He should "offer" <note>original Sanskrit: haret</note> the "oblations" <note>term: Balīn|Food offerings placed on the ground for various spirits and deities</note>, meaning he should perform the first duties such as the preparation of the cooked food. ॥ 1 ॥ The author now clarifies what is meant by this rule of restrained speech:

> **Translation:** Even for the sake of laughter or amusement, one should abandon irregular speech and, with a focused mind, perform the "Bali-ritual." It is proper to perform the cooking and preparation with careful consideration. ॥ 1 ॥

### 2. He may speak regarding the preparation of the food, and he may speak freely with guests. ॥ 2 ॥

"Regarding the preparation of the food" <note>original Sanskrit: annasaṃsiddhim</note> refers to topics concerning the readiness of the food to be cooked or the attainment of the ingredients...

<summary>This page concludes the third section of the Gobhila Gṛhyasūtra regarding the Brahma-sacrifice (teaching) and begins the fourth section concerning the Bali-karma (food offerings). It emphasizes the requirement for ritual purity and silence, providing a "reset" mantra if secular speech interrupts a sacred act.</summary>
