--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the Brahma-sacrifice. Having established the rules for speech and purity during teaching, the text now transitions to the fourth section of the first chapter, which details the Vaiśvadeva—the daily universal offering of food into the domestic fire.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>[ Chapter 1, Section 4, Sutras 1–5 ] Section on the Universal Food Offering (Bali-Vaiśvadeva) ॥</header>

Regarding questions and answers and similar matters, "one may speak" <note>original Sanskrit: bhāṣeta</note>; there is no prohibition in that regard. Furthermore, one may "speak at will" <note>original Sanskrit: kāmaṃ</note> with "guests" <note>original Sanskrit: atithibhiḥ</note> who have arrived, perhaps to show humility or courtesy; there is no prohibition there either. ॥ 2 ॥ The rule for the Universal Offering (Vaiśvadeva) is now stated—

**Translation:** Indeed, there is no prohibition against conversation related to the preparation of food; nor is there any obstacle to speaking with humility to guests who have arrived. ॥ 2 ॥

### 3. Then, having taken out some of the sacrificial food, and having sprinkled it with sacrificial side-dishes, he should offer it into the fire silently, using only his hand. ॥ 3 ॥

"Then" <note>original Sanskrit: atha</note> refers to the time after the cooking is completed. From that "sacrificial food" <note>term: haviṣya|Food fit for an offering, typically pure, vegetarian, and prepared specifically for the ritual</note>, one should "take out" <note>original Sanskrit: uddhṛtya</note> a small portion of the cooked meal. After "sprinkling" <note>original Sanskrit: upasiçya</note> or mixing that portion with "sacrificial side-dishes" <note>original Sanskrit: haviṣyair vyañjanaiḥ</note> such as dal or soup, he should offer it into the "fire" <note>term: Gṛhya fire|The domestic hearth fire</note> described previously. He should do this "silently" <note>original Sanskrit: tūṣṇīm</note>, meaning without speaking clearly or loudly, and "using only his hand" <note>original Sanskrit: pāṇinā</note>; there is no requirement for a sacrificial ladle <note>term: sruva|A wooden spoon used for liquid offerings</note> here. ॥ 3 ॥ Now, the deities for the mantras are prescribed—

**Translation:** When the meal is prepared, one should take a portion of that sacrificial food, mix it with sacrificial side-dishes, and offer a single oblation into that same fire without reciting a mantra. For this offering, a sacrificial ladle is not required; the hand alone will suffice for the task. ॥ 3 ॥

### 4. The first offering belongs to Prajāpati, the second to Sviṣṭakṛt. ॥ 4 ॥

The "first offering" <note>original Sanskrit: pūrvāhutiḥ</note> is "dedicated to Prajāpati" <note>original Sanskrit: prājāpatyā</note>. Thus, meditating in the mind on Prajāpati—the Lord of creatures, the master of the people, and the supreme God who performs the creation, maintenance, and dissolution of the universe—and whispering "To Prajāpati, hail!" <note>original Sanskrit: Prajāpataye svāhā</note>, the first offering is accomplished. The "second" <note>original Sanskrit: uttarā</note> offering is "dedicated to Sviṣṭakṛt" <note>original Sanskrit: sauviṣṭakṛtī</note>, meaning the one who makes the sacrifice well-offered and fulfills noble desires. Meditating in the mind on that same Supreme Lord who is the inner ruler of all, and whispering "To Sviṣṭakṛt, hail!" <note>original Sanskrit: Sviṣṭakṛte svāhā</note>, the second offering is made. This practice is also known as the "Sacrifice to the Gods" <note>term: Deva-yajña</note>, the "Daily Fire-offering" <note>term: Nitya-homa</note>, or the "Universal Offering" <note>term: Vaiśvadeva</note>. ॥ 4 ॥

**Translation:** The first offering is to the deity Prajāpati—that is, the Supreme Lord who is the true King of this entire realm and who protects the world and its people. Meditating on Him in the mind, one gives the first oblation. Then, meditating on the deity Sviṣṭakṛt—the one who is the sole inner witness of the whole world and the fulfiller of all auspicious desires—one gives the second oblation. This is what is called the "Sacrifice to the Gods," the "Daily Offering," and the "Universal Offering." ॥ 4 ॥

### 5. Then he should present the portions for the spirits (Bali), having prepared a clean spot of ground either outside or inside. ॥ 5 ॥

<sig>4</sig>

<vocab>Section on the Universal Offering, Lord of Creatures (Prajāpati), Fulfiller of Sacrifices (Sviṣṭakṛt), Universal Offering (Vaiśvadeva), Daily Offering (Nitya-homa), Sacrifice to the Gods (Deva-yajña), Oblation (Āhuti), Sacrificial food (Haviṣyānna), Sacrificial ladle (Sruva)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the daily Vaiśvadeva ritual where a householder offers a portion of the prepared meal into the domestic fire. It specifies that two silent offerings are made by hand to Prajāpati and Sviṣṭakṛt before placing food portions on the ground for other beings.</summary>
<keywords>Vaiśvadeva, Prajāpati, Sviṣṭakṛt, Deva-yajña, daily ritual, food offerings, Agni, domestic fire, householder duties</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for the Vaiśvadeva (the daily universal offering). Having completed the silent offering of food into the domestic fire (Deva-yajña), the ritual now moves to the Bali-karma—the placement of food portions on the ground for various beings, including animals, spirits, and deities.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra—</header>

"Then" <note>original Sanskrit: atha</note> refers to the time after the fire offering to the gods, otherwise known as the Universal Offering (Vaiśvadeva); "outside or inside" <note>original Sanskrit: vāhyataḥ vā antarvā</note> the fire-sanctuary. Having "prepared" <note>original Sanskrit: kṛtvā</note> a "good ground" <note>original Sanskrit: subhūmi</note> by sweeping and purifying the earth, one should "present" <note>original Sanskrit: haret</note> the "offerings" <note>term: Bali|Portions of food placed on the ground, rather than in the fire</note>. This constitutes the sacrifice to all beings <note>term: Bhūta-yajña|The ritual of feeding the visible and invisible creatures of the world</note>, which takes the form of providing food for animals, birds, ants, and others. ॥ 5 ॥

**Explanation:** Following the fire sacrifice called the Deva-yajña, one should go either inside or outside the fire-house. In any suitable location, after thoroughly cleaning the ground with a broom or similar tool, one should complete the "Offering Ritual" <note>original Sanskrit: Balikarma</note> by giving food to animals, birds, ants, and other creatures in those prepared spots. ॥ 5 ॥

---

### 6. Having poured water once, one should place the offering in four parts; finally, one should sprinkle around it once. ॥ 6 ॥

"Once" <note>original Sanskrit: sakṛt</note> means a single time; "having poured" <note>original Sanskrit: ninīya</note> "water" <note>original Sanskrit: apaḥ</note> as a cleansing sprinkle on the ground, one should "place" <note>original Sanskrit: nidadhyāt</note> the "offering" <note>original Sanskrit: baliṃ</note>—which is a gift intended for the beings of the earth—in "four parts" <note>original Sanskrit: caturdhā</note> or four distinct ways. These should be gathered on the spot that was cleaned and moistened with water. "Finally" <note>original Sanskrit: antataḥ</note>, at the conclusion of placing the offerings, one should again "sprinkle around" <note>original Sanskrit: pariṣiñcet</note> with water "once" <note>original Sanskrit: sakṛt</note>, just as before. ॥ 6 ॥

**Explanation:** On the cleaned ground, first sprinkle water once, then place the four portions of the offering separately, and then sprinkle water over them once more. ॥ 6 ॥

---

### 7. Or, one may sprinkle both before and after each individual placement. ॥ 7 ॥

"Or" <note>original Sanskrit: vā</note> suggests an alternative; "in sequence" <note>original Sanskrit: anuvidhānam</note> means placing the four offerings one after another in order. Furthermore, for "each individual" <note>original Sanskrit: ekaikam</note> placement, one should "sprinkle" <note>original Sanskrit: pariṣiñcet</note> "on both sides" <note>original Sanskrit: ubhayataḥ</note>—that is, once before and once after the food is set down. ॥ 7 ॥

**Explanation:** Alternatively, one may place the offering part by part, sprinkling water once before and once after placing each individual portion. ॥ 7 ॥

---

### 8. That which he places first becomes the offering to Earth; the second is to Wind; the third is to the All-Gods; the fourth is to the Lord of Progeny. ॥ 8 ॥

"He" <note>original Sanskrit: sa</note> refers to the person engaged in giving the offerings. "That which he places first" <note>original Sanskrit: yat prathamaṃ nidadhāti</note>, "that" <note>original Sanskrit: saḥ</note> first "offering" <note>original Sanskrit: baliḥ</note> "belongs to the Earth" <note>term: Pārthiva|Relating to Pṛthivī, the Earth Goddess</note>. "Then" <note>original Sanskrit: atha</note>, "that which he places second" <note>original Sanskrit: yad dvitīyaṃ</note>, "that" offering "belongs to the Wind" <note>term: Vāyavya|Relating to Vāyu, the deity of air and wind</note>. "That which he places third" <note>original Sanskrit: yat tṛtīyaṃ</note>, "that" offering "belongs to the All-Gods" <note>term: Vaiśvadeva|Relating to the Viśvedevas, the collective assembly of deities</note>. "That which he places fourth" <note>original Sanskrit: yac caturthaṃ</note>, "that" offering "belongs to the Lord of Progeny" <note>term: Prājāpatya|Relating to Prajāpati, the creator deity</note>. ॥ 8 ॥

<summary>This page describes the Bali-karma ritual, where food is placed on the ground for animals and deities after the daily fire sacrifice. It details the methods of purifying the ground with water and identifies the four primary deities to whom these portions are dedicated: Earth, Wind, the All-Gods, and the Lord of Progeny.</summary>
<keywords>Bali-karma, Bhūta-yajña, Earth (Pṛthivī), Wind (Vāyu), All-Gods (Viśvedevas), Lord of Progeny (Prajāpati), ritual purity, domestic sacrifice</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the Bali-karma (the placement of food offerings) as part of the daily household ritual described in the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra. Having established the method of dividing the food, the text now identifies the specific deities and locations for these portions.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>The Chapter on the Bali-Vaiśvadeva Ritual</header>
<meta>[Chapter 1, Section 4, Sutras 6-11]</meta>

**Commentary:** Of the four portions of the offering <note>term: Bali|Portions of food placed on the ground for various beings</note> mentioned previously, the first portion is for the Goddess Earth, the second is for the deity Vāyu <note>the god of wind</note>, the third is for the Viśvedevas <note>the "All-Gods" or Universal Deities</note>, and the fourth is for the deity Prajāpati <note>the Lord of Procreation and the Creator</note>. ॥ 8 ॥

## 9. Then he should present other offerings; at the middle of the door of the water-reservoir, the first offering is to the Water-deities, the second to the Herbs and Trees, and the third to Space. ॥ 9 ॥

"Then" <note>original Sanskrit: atha</note> refers to the time after the prescription of those first four offerings. "Of this" <note>original Sanskrit: asya</note> refers to the person performing the ritual. "Of the water-reservoir" <note>original Sanskrit: udapānasya</note> refers to the place where water is kept and used in this house. At the "middle" of the "door" <note>original Sanskrit: dvārasya madhyam</note> of that place, he should "present" <note>original Sanskrit: haret</note> or perform three "other offerings" <note>original Sanskrit: aparān balīn</note>. There, the "first offering" is "dedicated to the Water-deities" <note>original Sanskrit: abdaita-vatah</note>; the "second" is "for the Herbs and Trees" <note>original Sanskrit: oṣadhi-vanaspatibhyaḥ</note>; and the "third" is "for Space" <note>original Sanskrit: ākāśasya</note>. The phrase "for their satisfaction" should be understood as the conclusion in every instance. ॥ 9 ॥

**Commentary:** After placing these four offerings, the one performing the ritual should place three other offerings in the middle of the doorway of the house where the "serviceable" water is kept. Of these, the first is to the Water Deity, the second to the Herbs and Trees, and the third to Space <note>original Sanskrit: Ākāśa|The element of ether or the sky</note>. ॥ 9 ॥

## 10. Then he should present another offering at either the bed or the place of excretion; that offering is either for Desire or for Anger. ॥ 10 ॥

"Then" <note>original Sanskrit: atha</note> refers to the time after presenting the three aforementioned offerings. He should "present" <note>original Sanskrit: haret</note> yet "another" <note>original Sanskrit: aparam</note> single "offering" <note>original Sanskrit: balim</note>. The text points out its location: "at the bed or the place of excretion" <note>original Sanskrit: śayanaṃ vā adhivarcam vā</note>. This indicates either the sleeping spot in the middle of the bedroom, or the place for urination and waste within that same house. The text then prescribes the deity: "that" <note>original Sanskrit: saḥ</note> offering, if placed in the sleeping area, is "for Desire" <note>term: Kāma|The god of love and longing</note>; if placed in the area of excretion, it is "for Anger" <note>term: Manyu|The personification of wrath or passion</note>. ॥ 10 ॥

**Commentary:** After placing these three offerings, one should place one more offering in the bedroom—either at the actual place of sleep or in the place used for the evacuation of waste <note>note: ancient Indian domestic architecture often included specific designated areas for these functions within the household compound</note>. The offering at the sleeping place is for the deity "Desire," and the offering at the place of excretion <note>original Sanskrit: adhivarcam</note> is for the deity "Anger." ॥ 10 ॥

## 11. Then, at the rubbish heap, that is for the Demon-folk. ॥ 11 ॥

"Then" <note>original Sanskrit: atha</note> afterward, one should also cast an offering at the "rubbish heap" <note>original Sanskrit: sastūpa</note>, which refers to the place where the sweepings and refuse of the house are thrown. "That" <note>original Sanskrit: saḥ</note> offering is "for the Demon-folk" <note>term: Rakṣojana|The class of spirits or demons known as Rakshasas</note>. ॥ 11 ॥

**Commentary:** Following this, one should cast an offering at the place where the house-sweepings are collected. That offering is for the Rakshasa people. ॥ 11 ॥

<note>original vocabulary: bali (offering), vaiśvadeva (universal gods), udapāna (water-well/reservoir), oṣadhi-vanaspati (herbs and trees), ākāśa (space/ether), śayana (bed), adhivarcam (place of excretion), kāma (desire), manyu (anger), sastūpa (rubbish heap), rakṣojana (demonic beings)</note>

<summary>This page details the specific locations for household food offerings (Bali), extending the ritual from the hearth to the water source, the bedroom, the latrine, and the rubbish heap. It identifies the deities associated with these daily spaces, including the personifications of Desire, Anger, and various nature spirits.</summary>
<keywords>Bali offerings, domestic ritual, Gobhila Grhya Sutra, Kama (Desire), Manyu (Anger), Rakshas, water-deities, household shrines</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the daily Bali-karma (food offerings). The previous page detailed the first seven offerings to the Earth, Vāyu, the Universal Gods, Prajāpati, the Water-deities, the Trees, and Space. We now move to the final offerings to the spirits and ancestors, and the physical requirements for the ceremony.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra—</header>

Commentary:—Thereafter, one should give an offering in the place where rubbish is discarded; this offering is for the demons <note>term: Rakshasas|Malevolent spirits or demons in Vedic mythology</note>. ॥ 11 ॥

## 12. Having sprinkled the remainder of this offering with water, he should pour it out toward the south in an anti-clockwise direction; it is for the Ancestors. ॥ 12 ॥

"Now" <note>original: atha</note> means thereafter. "The remainder of this offering" <note>original: etad-bali-śeṣam</note> is made nearly clean by the "sprinkling of water" <note>original: adbhiḥ abhyāsicya</note>. "Anti-clockwise" <note>original: apasalavi</note> means using the part of the hand sacred to the ancestors <note>term: Pitṛ-tīrtha|The area of the hand between the thumb and forefinger</note> toward the "south" <note>original: dakṣiṇā</note> direction; one should "pour" <note>original: ninayet</note> or scatter it. This very scattered offering "is" <note>original: bhavati</note> dedicated to the "Ancestors" <note>term: Pitṛs|Deceased progenitors or forefathers</note>. ॥ 12 ॥

Commentary:—Following this, after washing the food remaining in the vessel with water, one should throw it toward the south using three fingers of the hand; that offering belongs to the Ancestors. ॥ 12 ॥

[ From this, according to the view of Sage Gobhila, ten offerings to the spirits <note>term: Bhūta-bali|Offerings of food placed on the ground for all beings</note> have been determined. Among them: five are in the room of the sacred fire, three are at the door of the water-room, one is in the place of the bed or the place for urination, one is in the side-chamber of the bedroom, and one is in the place where rubbish is kept, with the remainder in the southern part of the house. However, commonly nowadays, a practice is seen of making 14 offerings: three lines of water are made from north to south, and the offerings are placed in an ascending order—five in each of two lines, then others—totaling 14. * ]

## 13. He should perform the fire sacrifice only while sitting. ॥ 13 ॥

## 14. He should give to the Ancestors while sitting, and to the others as is convenient. ॥ 14 ॥

"Sitting" <note>original: āsīnaḥ</note> means seated. "Only" "in the fire should he sacrifice"; this refers to the Universal Deity Fire Sacrifice <note>term: Vaiśvadeva-homa|The daily ritual of offering food into the domestic fire</note> using the previously mentioned method of taking up the sacrificial food. For the "Ancestors" <note>original: pitṛbhyaḥ</note> as well, the "remainder of the offering" mentioned previously should be "given" while "sitting" "only." "The others" <note>original: itarān</note> refers to the offerings to the water-reservoir and so on; these should be given "as is convenient" <note>original: yathopapādam</note>, meaning one may give them while standing or moving about as the situation requires. ॥ 13, 14 ॥

Although this system of 14 offerings is not strictly in accordance with the scripture, because Sage Gobhila did not prescribe it, it is not understood to be a duty for the twice-born <note>term: Dvija|Members of the three upper social classes who have undergone the sacred thread ceremony</note> of the Kauthumī branch <note>term: Kauthumī Śākhā|A specific lineage of scholars of the Sāma Veda</note>. ॥ 12 ॥

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the daily offerings (Bali-karma). While the previous page focused on the specific locations and deities for the ten spirit-offerings, this page clarifies the physical posture of the practitioner and who is eligible to perform the ritual.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>

# Section on Offerings to All Deities and Beings
<meta>[ Chapter 1, Section 5, Sutras 12–20 ]</meta>

Commentary:—The previously mentioned offering to all gods <note>term: Vaiśvadeva Homa|The daily ritual of offering food into the sacred domestic fire</note> should be performed only while sitting; the remainder of the food intended for the ancestors <note>original: bali-śeṣa</note> (Sutra 12) should also be offered while sitting. Other offerings, such as those given to the household deities or planets mentioned previously, may be performed in whatever way is feasible—whether standing, sitting, or stooping (wherever and however it is most convenient). ॥ 13, 14 ॥

->**15. One should personally perform these offerings for as long as one resides [at home].**<-
->**16. Or else, another Brahmin [may do so].**<-
->**17. Both the husband and wife [are eligible].**<-

"These" "offerings" "for as long as" one "resides" in one's own home, one "should personally" "perform" <note>original: svayam-eva haret</note>. "Or else," in cases of illness or difficulty, "another Brahmin" may act as a representative <note>term: pratinidhi|A substitute authorized to perform a ritual on behalf of another</note> and is eligible for this. In this duty, the "husband and wife" <note>original: dampatī</note>—both the wife and the husband—are "exactly" equal in their authority. ॥ 15, 16, 17 ॥

Commentary:—These offerings should be completed by oneself while one is living at home. However, if one is unable (due to illness, etc.), it can also be accomplished by having another Brahmin perform it. For this ritual, both the man and the woman are equally entitled. Therefore, the wife may also perform the scattering of the food offerings. ॥ 15, 16, 17 ॥

->**18. This is the vow of the householder.**<-

"This" <note>original: iti</note> ritual of offering to all gods and beings mentioned in this third section is the "vow of the householder" <note>original: gṛhamedhi-vratam</note>; it is a mandatory daily duty that must be observed by the master of the house. ॥ 18 ॥

Commentary:—This (everything stated from the beginning of this section until now) is a mandatory duty for householders. ॥ 18 ॥

->**19. The woman in the evening, and the man in the morning.**<-

"In the evening the woman" and "in the morning the man" should perform this food-scattering ritual; "thus" <note>original: iti</note> is the rule favored by certain other teachers. Even here, the author Gobhila does not disagree with this view. ॥ 19 ॥

Commentary:—"The master of the house in the morning, and his wife in the evening should perform the food-scattering"—this is also the opinion of certain other sages. ॥ 19 ॥

->**20. One should perform these offerings from all types of food, or from the ancestral food, or for the sake of prosperity, or for secular purposes.**<-

---

<note>* The teacher has not said that the wife should perform the food-scattering ritual without reciting the Vedic mantras. In the *Laws of Manu* <note>term: Manu Smriti|An influential legal and ethical text</note>, it is written that women should perform the food-scattering without mantras, but that is intended for those who possess [only] meditative power.</note>

<vocab>Bali-vaiśva-prakaraṇa, Vaiśvadeva Homa, Bali-śeṣa, Gobhila, Gṛhamedhi-vrata, Bali-haraṇa, Manu, Dampatī</vocab>

<summary>This page details the physical requirements and eligibility for the daily food-scattering ritual, establishing that the householder or his wife are equally entitled to perform it, and that the husband and wife may divide the duties between morning and evening.</summary>
<keywords>daily ritual, householder duties, women's ritual rights, Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra, religious posture, Brahmin, Bali-karma</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions regarding the Bali-karma (daily spirit-offerings). The previous page concluded by establishing that both the husband and wife, or a designated Brahmin substitute, are eligible to perform these daily rites while residing at home.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhyasūtra ॥</header>

"Or of the ancestral sacrifice" <note>original: pitṛyajñasya vā</note> means food cooked for the purpose of ancestral rites; "or of the well-being rite" <note>original: svastyayanasya vā</note> means food cooked for a rite of well-being, for prosperity, or for feeding Brahmins; "or for any other purpose" <note>original: apyārthasya vā</note> means cooked food intended for any purpose such as one's own meal. From "every" <note>original: sarvasya eva</note>—meaning from every type of food—"one should offer these portions" <note>original: etān balīn haret</note>. There is no strict rule that only a specific type of food must be taken for the spirit-offering ritual. ॥ 20 ॥

Commentary:—Whether it is for ancestral rites, or for welfare rites like feeding Brahmins, or even just for filling one’s own stomach, one can complete the spirit-offering ritual using all types of cooked food. ॥ 20 ॥

### It ceases during a grand sacrifice. ॥ 21 ॥

From a "sacrifice" <note>original: yajñāt</note>, meaning beginning from a major sacrifice such as the Jyotiṣṭoma <note>term: Jyotiṣṭoma|A primary seven-day Vedic ritual centered on the offering of the Soma plant</note>, the person "ceases" <note>original: nivarttate</note> or turns away from this [daily] duty. The intent is that for one who is consecrated <note>term: dīkṣita|A person initiated into a specific major Vedic ceremony and bound by its unique restrictions</note>, this daily spirit-offering ritual is not required during that time. ॥ 21 ॥

Commentary:—Regardless of which sacrifice it is, such as the Jyotiṣṭoma, once the ritual performance has begun, it is not proper to perform this daily spirit-offering. ॥ 21 ॥

### If at one time both rice and barley are prepared, having performed it with either one, he should consider it completed. ॥ 22 ॥

"If" "at one time" "rice and barley" <note>original: vrīhiyavau</note>—meaning both types of food—have been prepared and made ready, then "having performed" the offering with "either one" (of either the rice or the barley), one "should consider" it "completed" <note>original: kṛtam</note>, meaning the spirit-offering has been accomplished according to the prescribed rules. ॥ 22 ॥

Commentary:—If at the same time both rice and barley grains are prepared, one should not perform the spirit-offering ritual with both types of food; rather, the ritual can be accomplished by using just one of the two types of food. ॥ 22 ॥

### If food is cooked repeatedly at one time, he should perform this offering ritual only once. ॥ 23 ॥

"If" "at one time" "food is cooked" "repeatedly" or "frequently," then with the first batch of food cooked (or a later one), one "should perform" "this" "offering ritual" <note>term: balitantra|The systematic procedure or framework of the daily spirit-offerings</note> "only once." ॥ 23 ॥

Commentary:—If food is cooked two, three, or even more times during a single mealtime, one does not need to perform the spirit-offering ritual every single time; it should be done only once. ॥ 23 ॥

### If in one family food is cooked in many places, he should perform this offering ritual only from the master's kitchen. ॥ 24 ॥

<vocab>Gobhilagṛhyasūtra, Jyotiṣṭoma, Baliharaṇa, Vrīhiyava, Gṛhapati-mahānasa</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines practical rules for the daily spirit-offering (Bali), specifying that any cooked food is acceptable, the ritual is suspended during major sacrifices, and it only needs to be performed once even if multiple grains or batches of food are prepared.</summary>
<keywords>Bali-karma, ritual purity, Jyotiṣṭoma, food offerings, rice and barley, domestic ritual rules</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific regulations for the Bali-vaishvadeva (daily food offerings). The previous page ended with the rule that the daily ritual ceases when a householder is initiated into a major sacrifice like the Jyotiṣṭoma.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>[Chapter 1, Section 4, Sutras 21-27] The Section on Daily Food Offerings ॥</header>

"If" <note>original: yadi</note> "in one family" <note>original: ekasmin kule</note> consisting of many brothers and dependents, and even if they share one property, there happen to be "many kitchens" <note>original: bahu-mahanaseṣu</note> where food is cooked in various ways, then "from the kitchen of the head of the house" <note>original: gṛhapati-mahānasāt</note>—meaning the kitchen of the person who holds the primary ownership or seniority—"only" <note>original: eva</note> from that one "ritual of offerings" <note>original: bali-tantram</note> "should be performed" <note>original: kurvīta</note>, and not from every individual kitchen. ॥ 24 ॥

Commentary:—If many people of the same lineage live in one house but cook their meals separately, then the person who is the eldest or the recognized master of the house should perform this offering from their kitchen; the ritual should not be repeated in every "kitchen" <note>original: mahānasa</note>. ॥ 24 ॥

# But for whichever of these the food is ready first, they should make an offering in the fire, give the first portion to a Brahmin, and then eat. ॥ 25 ॥
# But if it is of poor quality, they should simply eat it. ॥ 26 ॥

"Of these" <note>original: eṣām</note> brothers or relatives living in one house but cooking separately, "for whichever" <note>original: yasya tu</note> of them the food "is ready first" <note>original: agrataḥ sidhyet</note>—specifically for one who does not maintain a formal sacred fire—he should "place" <note>original: niyuktam</note> some food "in the fire" <note>original: agnau</note>, "having done so" <note>original: kṛtvā</note>, he should "give" <note>original: dattva</note> the "first portion" <note>original: agram</note> of the cooked food "to a Brahmin" <note>original: brāhmaṇāya</note> or a guest, and "then eat" <note>original: bhuñjīta</note>. "But for whom" <note>original: yasya u</note> the first batch of cooked food is "inferior" <note>original: jaghanyam</note>—meaning it is unappetizing or poorly prepared due to some cooking error—he "should just eat it" <note>original: bhuñjīta eva</note>. He should not serve that food to a guest; instead, he should serve the guest from a subsequent, better-prepared batch of food. ॥ 25, 26 ॥

Commentary:—If multiple people are cooking in one house, the one whose food is ready first should offer a small amount to the fire, serve a guest, and then eat; however, if that food is spoiled or unpalatable, he should not serve it to a guest but eat it himself, and then serve the guest from a fresh preparation. ॥ 25, 26 ॥

## Now they also cite a traditional verse. ॥ 27 ॥

"Now further" <note>original: adyāpi</note> the "ancient teachers cite" <note>original: udāharanti</note> or speak of another specific rule regarding this matter. As follows:— ॥ 27 ॥

Commentary:—The ancient teachers say something more specific regarding this "distribution of offerings" <note>original: bali-vaharaṇa</note>. ॥ 27 ॥ For example:—

---

<margin>* In this regard, it is a daily duty: whichever person has food ready first on a given day, and if that food is of good quality, they must perform the service for the guest on that day. It is proper to do so regardless of whether the others wish to or not. ॥ 25, 26</margin>

<vocab>वलिवैश्वप्रकरणम् (bali-vaiśva-prakaraṇam): The section on the ritual of daily food offerings to all deities., गृहपति (gṛhapati): The master or head of the household., महानस (mahānasa): A kitchen or place where food is cooked., बलितन्त्रम् (bali-tantram): The system or procedure of the spirit-offering ritual., पाकशाला (pākaśālā): A cooking-hall or kitchen., अग्रतः (agrataḥ): First or in front., ब्राह्मणाय (brāhmaṇāya): To a Brahmin (often implying an unexpected guest in this context)., भुञ्जीत (bhuñjīta): One should eat., जघन्यम् (jaghanyam): Low quality, inferior, or spoiled., पूर्वाचार्याः (pūrvācāryāḥ): The ancient teachers or authorities of the past., बलिवहरण (bali-vaharaṇa): The act of carrying out or distributing the daily offerings., सत्कुर्य्यात् (satkuryyāt): One should treat with respect or hospitality.</vocab>

<summary>This page clarifies how the daily food offerings should be managed in households with multiple kitchens, emphasizing that the head of the house leads the ritual, while also providing rules for who should serve a guest first based on whose meal is ready and its quality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific regulations for the daily food offerings (Bali-vaishvadeva). The previous page discussed the rules for communal kitchens and the priority of offerings. This page focuses on the specific prayer for one's desires and a special seasonal offering for longevity.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra ॥</header>

<warning>Some characters are slightly blurred due to the scan quality, but the text remains legible.</warning>

# At the end of this very food offering, let him declare his desire; it will surely be granted. ॥ 28 ॥

"At the end of this very food offering" <note>original: etasyaiva baliharaṇasya</note>—meaning immediately following it—he should "declare" <note>original: prabrūvīta</note> his "desire" <note>original: kāmam</note> or personal wish. For "this" person who prays, it "becomes" <note>original: bhavati</note> a "certainty" <note>original: ha</note>—that the prayer will be fulfilled. ॥ 28 ॥

To answer the question of how one should declare their desire, it is said "while performing the hopeful offering." To clarify this for himself, the author establishes the rule for the "hopeful" offering <note>The "hopeful offering" or āśasya-bali is a specific ritual performed for a desired outcome, often longevity.</note>:—

Translation:—After performing this food offering <note>original: bali</note>, one should ask for a "boon" according to their own desire (meaning, silently in the mind from the Supreme Being); this prayer will surely be fulfilled. ॥ 28 ॥

# He should personally make the hopeful offering; between the barley and rice harvests, and between the rice and barley harvests, this is called the "hopeful" offering. ॥ 29 ॥

# He indeed becomes long-lived. ॥ 30 ॥

"He should make the hopeful offering" <note>original: āśasyaṃ baliṃ haret</note>—by this, the prayer for one's desire is accomplished. And he should make that offering "personally" <note>original: svayam</note>; no proxy <note>original: pratinidhi</note> should be used here. What kind of offering is this "hopeful" offering? He explains: "from the barley" <note>original: yavebhyaḥ</note>—meaning before the rice crop is born—upon a base of barley; and "from the rice" <note>original: vrīhibhyaḥ</note>—meaning before the barley crop is born—upon a base of rice, he should make the offering. "That indeed" <note>original: sa tu</note> is what "is called the hopeful offering" <note>original: āśasyo nāma balirbhavati</note>. "Indeed" <note>original: ha</note>, with certainty, "in this way" <note>original: evam</note> by giving this offering, the person "becomes long-lived" <note>original: dīrghāyuḥ bhavati</note>. ॥ 29, 30 ॥

Translation:—If one wishes to make the aforementioned prayer for a boon, then one must personally (not through a representative) provide an offering called "Ashasya." Until the winter grain crop <note>original: hemanta-ṛtu-dhānya</note> is ready in the field, one should make an offering near the source of the rice before the barley is ready, and similarly for the barley. This is called the Ashasya offering. From this offering, one will certainly attain the benefit of a long life. ॥ 29 ॥ 30 ॥

# When the winnowed grains are being cooked, he should offer the scum of the water; that belongs to Rudra, that belongs to Rudra. ॥ 31 ॥ 4 ॥

Now he prescribes the material for the foremost offering: "of the winnowed grains" <note>original: phalīkaraṇānām</note>—meaning of rice or barley that has had the husks removed—"when they are cooked" <note>original: visrāṇite</note> and the cooking is complete, one should take a portion "of the scum" <note>original: ācāmasya</note> or the starchy water <note>original: maṇḍasya</note>, and "from that water" <note>original: apām</note>—meaning the liquid portion—one should "make the offering" <note>original: baliṃ haret</note> for hope. Right there, he designates the deity:—"that" <note>original: saḥ</note> offering "belongs to Rudra" <note>original: raudraḥ</note>, meaning its presiding deity is Rudra <note>Rudra is the fierce form of Shiva, often associated with the elements and protection.</note>. By this, it is indicated that the mantra to be used is "Salutations to Rudra" <note>original: rudrāya namaḥ</note>. The repetition <note>original: abhyāsaḥ</note> of the phrase "belongs to Rudra" indicates the completion of this section <note>original: khaṇḍa</note>. ॥ 31 ॥

Thus ends the explanation of the fourth section of the first chapter in the commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra. ॥ 2, 4 ॥

The section concludes with specific instructions to offer the starchy water from cooked grains to the deity Rudra.</summary>

Commentary:—This offering <term>bali|a ritual food offering</term> should be prepared with barley or the scum of boiled rice, and he should recite the mantra "Salutation to Rudra" <note>original: Rudrāya namaḥ</note>. ॥ 31 ॥

The translation of the fourth section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra is now complete. ॥

---

### Now, concerning the New and Full Moon sacrifices. ॥ 1 ॥
->This is an introductory rule <term>adhikāra-sūtra|a governing rule that establishes the topic for the following verses</term>. It should be understood as applying until the end of this chapter. ॥ 1 ॥<-

Commentary:—From here begins the instruction regarding the New Moon <note>original: Darśa</note> and Full Moon <note>original: Paurṇamāsa</note> sacrifices. ॥ 1 ॥

### One should fast on the Full Moon day that occurs at the junction; some say on the following day. ॥ 2, 3 ॥
"Full Moon at the junction" <note>original: sandhyāṃ pauṛṇamāsīm</note> refers to the day when the Full Moon begins at or before the morning twilight; on that very day, they say "one should fast" <note>original: upavaset</note>. "Some" <note>original: eke</note> teachers say one should fast on the "following day" <note>original: uttarām</note>—meaning when the moon rises after sunset or when it is high in the sky. The implication is that there is no fault in either practice. ॥ 2-3 ॥

Commentary:—If one is to perform the New and Full Moon sacrifices, one must fast on the day preceding the ritual. Regarding this, it is said that one should fast on the Full Moon day occurring at the "junction" <gloss>*</gloss>; however, some teachers say it is proper to fast only on the "following" Full Moon <note>original: uttarā pauṛṇamāsī</note>, which is to say when the moon rises at sunset <gloss>*</gloss> or higher in the sky <gloss>*</gloss>. According to the specific view of Sage Gobhila, if the Full Moon is present at sunrise, and later in the afternoon or night the first day of the lunar fortnight <note>original: pratipat</note> begins, or if the Full Moon lasts only until dawn, one should fast on that day. According to others, the "following" Full Moon is better for fasting—meaning the day when the fourteenth day of the lunar cycle ends and the Full Moon begins at or after sunset. ॥ 2, 3 ॥

### Now, that day on which the moon is not seen is the New Moon. ॥ 4 ॥
->The instruction "one should fast" is carried over from the previous verse. By this, it results that the New Moon known as the "passed moon" <note>original: gatākhyā</note> should not be observed for fasting. ॥ 4 ॥<-

Commentary:—On the day when there is no possibility of seeing the moon, whether the New Moon lasts the entire day from sunrise or the first day of the fortnight follows, that is the day for the New Moon fast. By this, fasting is naturally forbidden on the day when the fourteenth day of the lunar cycle ends and the New Moon begins later, which is called "the passed moon" <note>original: gatākhyā</note>. The merit lies in the fasting for the Full and New Moons, and in both cases, the day on which the lunar phase is present at sunrise <note>original: udayātithi</note> is to be accepted. Thus, the sacrifice of the first half of the month and the second half of the month... [continues to next page]

---

<gloss>*</gloss> By looking at the tenth and eleventh sutras, these distinctions will be understood.

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the fifth section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra. It specifically addresses the mandatory nature of fasting and sacrifice during the lunar cycle and provides an astronomical definition for the New and Full Moon dates.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra—</header>

...on whichever day that lunar date occurs, that day is to be accepted. ॥ 4 ॥

## The ends of the fortnights are for fasting; the beginnings of the fortnights are for sacrifice. ॥ 5 ॥

Throughout one's life, the "ends of the fortnights" <note>original: pakṣāntāḥ</note>—referring to the New Moon <term>Amāvāsyā|the final day of the dark fortnight</term> and the Full Moon <term>Pūrṇimā|the final day of the bright fortnight</term>—are times when "one should fast" <note>original: upavastavyāḥ</note>; fasting must be performed on these days. Furthermore, on the "beginnings of the fortnights" <note>original: pakṣādayaḥ</note>—referring to the first lunar day <term>pratipad|the first day of a lunar fortnight</term> of every fortnight, whether dark or light—one should perform the "sacrifice" <note>original: abhiyaṣṭavyāḥ</note> according to the ritual characteristics to be described later. ॥ 5 ॥

Translation:—As long as one lives, one should fast at the end of each fortnight—namely on the New Moon and Full Moon—and perform a sacrifice at the beginning of each fortnight—namely on the first day of both the waxing and waning halves of the month. ॥ 5 ॥

## With the New Moon offering, one sacrifices for the waxing fortnight; with the Full Moon offering, for the waning fortnight. ॥ 6 ॥

Having fasted on the New Moon, the ritual offering <term>havis|an oblation or sacrificial gift</term> that is poured on the first day of the waxing fortnight is the "New Moon offering" <note>original: āmāvāsyena haviṣā</note>. By this single act, the "waxing fortnight" <note>original: pūrvapakṣam</note>—the entire fifteen-day period—is "encompassed" <note>original: abhi-vyāpya</note>, and it is accepted that the "sacrifice" <note>original: yajate</note> has been performed for the whole period. Similarly, with the "Full Moon offering" <note>original: pauṛṇamāsyena haviṣā</note>, the "waning fortnight" <note>original: aparapakṣam</note> is likewise entirely covered. ॥ 6 ॥

In this context, to determine the proper timing for fasting and for the Full and New Moons, the author now provides the definitions and distinctions for these lunar dates:

Translation:—After fasting on the New Moon, the sacrifice performed on the first day of the waxing fortnight should be considered a sacrifice extending over the entire waxing half. Similarly, after fasting on the Full Moon, the sacrifice performed on the first day of the waning fortnight should be understood as completing the sacrifice for the entire waning half. ॥ 6 ॥

## That which is the maximum distance between the Sun and Moon is the Full Moon; that which is the maximum proximity is the New Moon. ॥ 7 ॥

On whichever lunar date there is the "maximum" <note>original: paramaḥ</note> "distance" <note>original: vikarṣaḥ|astronomical opposition</note> between the two celestial bodies, the "Sun and Moon" <note>original: sūryācandramasoḥ</note>—meaning they are positioned at their furthest points from each other because they are in the seventh zodiac sign relative to one another—"that" date is the "Full Moon" <note>original: pauṛṇamāsī</note>. On whichever date there is "maximum" "proximity" <note>original: saṅkarṣaḥ|astronomical conjunction</note>—meaning they are extremely close because they are both in the same zodiac sign—"that" date is the "New Moon" <note>original: amāvāsyā</note>. ॥ 7 ॥

Translation:—The lunar date on which the Sun and the Moon reach their maximum distance <note>original: vikarṣa</note>—that is, they are situated in the seventh sign relative to each other and are thus at their furthest point—is called the "Full Moon." Similarly, the date on which these two bodies reach their maximum proximity <note>original: saṅkarṣa</note> (staying very close) is called the "New Moon." ॥ 7 ॥

<vocab>End of the fortnight (pakṣānta), To be fasted (upavastavyāḥ), First day of the fortnight (pratipad), New Moon (amāvāsyā), Full Moon (pūrṇimā), Sacrifice (yāga), New Moon offering (āmāvāsyena haviṣā), Waxing fortnight (pūrvapakṣa), Waning fortnight (aparapakṣa), Distance/Opposition (vikarṣaḥ), Proximity/Conjunction (saṅkarṣaḥ)</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the timing for the New and Full Moon sacrifices, explaining that a single sacrifice at the start of a fortnight covers the entire fifteen-day period. It also provides an astronomical definition of the New Moon as a conjunction and the Full Moon as an opposition of the Sun and Moon.</summary>
<keywords>Amavasya, Purnima, Pratipad, lunar sacrifice, fasting, astronomy, Sun-Moon conjunction, Sun-Moon opposition, Gobhila Grhya Sutra</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the fifth section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra. Having established the general rule for fasting and sacrifice at the transition of the lunar fortnights, the text now moves to the precise astronomical definitions of the New Moon (Amāvāsyā) and Full Moon (Pūrṇimā) to ensure rituals are performed at the correct time.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>Section on the New and Full Moon Sacrifices ॥</header>
<meta>[ Chapter 1, Section 5, Sutras 9-11 ]</meta>

## On whichever day the moon is not seen at all, one should observe that day as the New Moon. Even if it is seen once, it is called "Gatadhvā" [having completed its path]. ॥ 8, 9 ॥

The phrase "on whichever day" <note>original: yad-ahaḥ</note> refers to the specific day on which "the moon is not seen at all" <note>original: candramā na dṛśyeta eva</note>; one should "observe" or accept "that" lunar date as the "New Moon" <term>Amāvāsyā|the day of the conjunction of the sun and moon</term>. If the moon is "seen once" <note>original: ekadā dṛśyamāne'pi</note> at some point during the day or night, that New Moon is called "Gatadhvā" <note>original: gatādhvā</note>, meaning it has already obtained its portion of the fortnight. This concludes the definition of the Full and New Moons. ॥ 8, 9 ॥

The text now explains that the Full Moon is of three types:—

Translation:—The day on which there is no possibility of seeing the moon at night is called the New Moon <term>Amāvāsyā</term>. Even in a case where there is a possibility of seeing the moon just once for a short time, that day is accepted as the New Moon and is called "Gatadhvā." In other words, when the New Moon begins at the time of sunrise or after evening twilight while the fourteenth lunar day <term>Caturdaśī</term> is still active, but the New Moon occurs during the night, it is called "Gatadhvā." Furthermore, if the New Moon starts from sunrise and lasts through the entire night, or if the first day of the next fortnight <term>Pratipada</term> begins after some part of the night has passed, that is also called the New Moon. Thus, two types of New Moon are determined. ॥ 8, 9 ॥

## There are three times for the Full Moon: at twilight, risen at sunset, or high in the sky; alternatively, it is the day the moon becomes full. ॥ 10, 11 ॥

"Furthermore," "on whichever day" the moon "becomes full" <note>original: pūrṇo bhavati</note>, that day is the Full Moon <term>Pūrṇamāsī</term>. There "are three times" or categories of the "Full Moon." These are: Twilight, and so on. Where the Full Moon begins at sunrise or just before it, that is the "Twilight Full Moon" <note>original: sandhyā-paurṇamāsī</note>. If the full moon rises exactly at the same time as the sun sets, that is the "Sunset-risen Full Moon" <note>original: astamitoditā-paurṇamāsī</note>. If the moon becomes full later in the night, high above the horizon after sunset, that is called the "High Full Moon" <note>original: uccaiḥ-paurṇamāsī</note>. These are the three specified times. ॥ 10, 11 ॥

Translation:—The day on which the moon is expected to be full at night is the day of the Full Moon. This Full Moon is of three types. First, the **Twilight Full Moon**, which begins before the morning twilight, meaning the Full Moon or the first day of the next fortnight <term>Pratipada</term> occurs during the night. Second, the **Sunset-risen Full Moon**; this begins from the time of sunset, meaning the fourteenth day <term>Caturdaśī</term> occurs during the day and the Full Moon occurs at night and continues into the next day. Third, the **High Full Moon**, which occurs when the fourteenth day is left behind after sunset and the Full Moon begins, lasting late into the following night. ॥ 10, 11 ॥

<summary>This page defines the astronomical conditions for identifying the New Moon and the three specific categories of the Full Moon—Twilight, Sunset-risen, and High—to determine the correct timing for Vedic sacrifices.</summary>
<keywords>Amāvāsyā, Pūrṇimā, Gatadhvā, lunar calendar, Caturdaśī, Pratipada, ritual timing, Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section concerning the timing of the New and Full Moon sacrifices. Having defined the lunar days, the text now addresses the necessity of astronomical knowledge and begins the specific ritual preparations for the day of fasting (Upavasatha).</meta>

<page-num>३२</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra—</header>

### There is indeed a separate branch of study for this knowledge; one should either study it, or a learned expert should make the timing of the lunar juncture known. ॥१२॥

"This knowledge" <note>original: etasya jñānasya</note> refers to the understanding of the positions and movements of the planets, constellations, and time. There is a "separate" <note>original: pṛthag-eva</note> "branch of study" <term>Adhyāya|here referring to a body of literature or science</term> for this, which is known as the science of astronomy/astrology <term>Jyotiḥ-śāstra</term>. "One should study" <note>original: adhīyīta vā</note> that entire science from a "learned expert" <note>original: tad-vidvyaḥ</note>, such as an astronomer. If one is not engaged in studying the entire science, at the very least the "lunar juncture" <term>Parva|the transition point between fortnights</term>—the exact time the lunar phase ends—"should be known" <note>original: avagamayet</note>. The author’s intent is to explain that a detailed description of these celestial movements is not provided here because it belongs to a different field of study. ॥१२॥

**Commentary:—** By knowing the specific positions and movements of the planets and constellations, these matters can be understood thoroughly. If one wishes to know this, there is a separate science of Jyotisha for it; one should study it under a scholar of Jyotisha, or it can also be achieved by knowing the general principles to some extent. ॥१२॥

### Now, on whichever day the fast is to be observed, on that very day in the forenoon, after having offered the morning oblation, he smears the sacrificial altar of the fire all around with cow dung. He prepares the fuel, either of Khadira wood or Palāśa wood. With the exception of Vibhītaka, Tilvaka, Bādhaka, Nīvan, Vimba, Rājavṛkṣa, Śālmalī, Aralu, Dadhittha, Kovidāra, and Śleṣmātaka, the fuel may be from any fruit-bearing trees as required. For the fire of the Viśākhā, the Kuśa grass is cut near the top; for the ancestors, it is cut near the root. If Kuśa is unavailable, any grass may be used except for Śuka, Śara, Śīrya, Balvaja, Mutavana, Naḍa, and Śuṇṭha. He prepares the clarified butter, the rice or barley for the pot-offering, the cooking pot, the stirring-spoon, the large ladle, and the protected water. Regarding the rules we shall describe hereafter, he should not neglect them on that day. He should not go far from his home; he may buy but should not sell any livestock; he should not speak much, and if he speaks, he should wish to speak the truth. In the afternoon, having bathed, the husband and wife should eat the preparatory meal, whatever they desire, mixed with clarified butter, with skill. ॥१३-२६॥ ५

"Now" <note>original: atha</note> signifies that after determining the correct time, we shall describe the duties for the day of fasting. "On whichever day" <note>original: yad-ahaḥ</note> the "fast" <term>Upavasatha|a day of physical and spiritual preparation before a sacrifice</term> is "to be observed" <note>original: bhavati</note>, on "that day," meaning for the sacrifice of the waxing fortnight one prepares on the New Moon <term>Amāvāsyā</term>, and for the sacrifice of the waning fortnight one prepares on the Full Moon <term>Paurṇamāsī</term>...

<vocab>Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra, Jyotiḥ-śāstra (Astronomy/Astrology), Upavasatha (Fasting/Preparation day), Prātarāhuti (Morning oblation), Sthaṇḍila (Sacrificial altar/mound), Idhma (Sacrificial fuel), Sthālīpāka (Pot-offering of cooked grains), Amāvāsyā (New Moon), Paurṇamāsyā (Full Moon)</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that precise timing for rituals requires knowledge of Jyotiḥ-śāstra (astronomy) and details the physical preparations and behavioral codes for the Upavasatha (fasting day) preceding a sacrifice.</summary>
<keywords>Jyotisha, Upavasatha, ritual preparation, sacrificial wood, prohibited trees, kusha grass, sthālīpāka, ritual purity</keywords>

It moves from the timing discussed on the previous page to the physical gathering of materials and the behavioral codes for the sacrificer and his wife.</meta>

<page-num>३३</page-num>
<header>(Mandala 1, Section 5, Sutras 12–26) Section on the New and Full Moon Sacrifices ॥</header>

And **'in the forenoon itself,'** after having offered the morning oblation <note>original: prātarāhuti</note> and completed the morning fire-sacrifice <term>Agnihotra|the daily fire ritual</term>, these tasks must be performed. They are as follows: **'For this fire'**—the fire that serves as the means for the morning oblation and other rites—one **'smeares the ritual mound'** <note>original: sthaṇḍila</note> **'all around'** **'with cow dung'** <note>original: gomaya</note>. This means one should apply a coating everywhere, on all sides; this is the first task. **'Then'** after that. **'Either Khadira or Palāśa'** <note>Khadira is the Cutch tree; Palāśa is the "Flame of the Forest"</note> **'firewood'** <note>original: idhma</note>—meaning the wood for fuel—one **'prepares'** <note>original: upakalpayate</note>. One should make them ready and present; this is the second task. In that regard, if Khadira or Palāśa wood is not available, then **'of all trees'**—except for those few trees like the Vibhītaka <note>the Beleric Myrobalan, considered inauspicious</note> excluded in this sutra—the **'firewood'** **'according to the need'** <note>original: yathārthaṃ</note>—meaning so that the purpose is fulfilled—should be taken and **'used'**. 

**'Toward'** those places where the branches are distinct—those joining points—**'cut'** or **'severed'** **'Kuśa'** grass <note>original: kuśāstṛṇāni</note> should be prepared as the **'sacrificial seat'** <term>Barhis|a layer of grass spread on the altar for the deities to sit upon</term> for the work of the gods; this is the third task. **'Cut near the roots'**—meaning Kuśa grass severed close to the ground—should be prepared **'for the ancestors'** <note>original: pitṛbhyaḥ</note> for the sake of ancestral rites; this is the fourth task. There, if **'those'** specific Kuśa grasses are **'not available, except for prickly trees and similar plants'**, **'all grasses'** may indeed be taken. 

**'Clarified butter'** <term>Ājya|ghee used in Vedic sacrifices</term> is to be prepared; this is the fifth task. **'Either rice or barley'** from the stock used for **'pot-cooking'** <term>Sthālīpāka|an offering of grain cooked in a pot</term> should be prepared; this is the sixth task. A **'cooking pot'** <note>original: carusthālī</note>—the vessel for cooking—is to be prepared; this is the seventh task. A **'stirring paddle'** <note>original: mekṣaṇa</note>—a specific type of wooden ladle—should be fetched; this is the eighth task. An **'offering spoon'** <note>original: sruva</note>—the tool for making the oblation—should be fetched; this is the ninth task. **'Unprotected water'** <note>original: anaguptā āpaḥ</note>—possessing the characteristics previously described—must be fetched; this is the tenth task. **'And whichever'** other **'substitute'** or additional items for the New and Full Moon sacrifices **'we shall mention'** <note>original: udāhariṣyāmaḥ</note> or describe later, all of those should also be prepared and placed near the ritual mound.

Now the author states the rules of conduct to be observed on that day: **'On that day'**—meaning the day of the fast—**'one should not leave home'**; this is the first rule. If one happened to be far away previously, then on that day, **'even from afar'**, one **'should return'** to one's own **'home'**; this is the second rule. **'From another'**, such as a merchant, one **'may buy'** **'wealth'** or goods, but **'one should not sell'**; this is the third rule. One **'should speak little'** <note>original: abahu-vādī</note>—meaning be a speaker of few words; this is the fourth rule. 

One **'should desire to speak'** the **'truth'**; as the scripture says: "The gods are joined with truth, while humans are joined with falsehood" <note>original: satya-saṃhitā vai devā anṛta-saṃhitā manuṣyāḥ (Aitareya Brāhmaṇa 3.1.3)</note>. From this scriptural familiarity, it is certain that humans do not possess the power to maintain truth in all ways; therefore, as much as possible, one should speak only the truth; this is the fifth rule. 

**'Then'** after preparing the firewood and other items, **'in the afternoon itself'**, the **'couple'**—the sacrificer and his wife—having both performed their bath, should begin the rules for the day of fasting, such as wearing garlands and so on. Also, **'as follows'**: "Honey, meat, and anything containing alkaline or salt should not be eaten during the fast, nor should anything be eaten during the specified night"—as follows the teachings of another domestic ritual manual <note>referring to the Grihya-Sutras</note>. (The "specified night" means the night of the ritual observance).

<summary>This page details the ten physical preparations for the New and Full Moon sacrifices, including gathering specific woods and grasses, and outlines the behavioral restrictions for the couple, emphasizing truthfulness and domestic presence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the ritual preparations on the day of fasting (Upavasatha) preceding the New or Full Moon sacrifices. It concludes the list of ritual materials and transitions into the behavioral code and dietary rules for the sacrificer and his wife.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra</header>

...because by eating too much at night, physical distress may occur, and if that happens, the next day’s duties would be hindered—this is the intention. Regarding **'these two'** (the husband and wife), whatever **'desire'** <note>original: kāmyam</note> they may have, they should **'eat'** that very thing **'mixed with clarified butter'** <note>original: sarpirmiśraṃ</note> with a **'skilful'** <note>original: kuśalena</note> or satisfied mind. ॥ 13-26 ॥

The explanation of the fifth section of the first chapter in the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra is now complete. ॥ 1-5 ॥

**Translation and Commentary:** 
Following this, the teacher instructs on the duties for the day of fasting <term>Upavasa|the day preceding a major sacrifice where the couple observes specific restrictions</term>. On the day when the fast is to be observed—that is, on the day when the Full Moon <note>original: Pūrṇimā</note> begins at sunrise, or on the day when the New Moon <note>original: Amāvasyā</note> begins at sunrise—one should perform all these tasks in the forenoon after finishing the morning oblation of the daily fire-sacrifice <term>Agnihotra|the daily ritual of pouring offerings into the sacred fire</term>. 

First, one should thoroughly smear the fire-room with cow dung. 

Second, one should gather firewood from the Khadira <note>Cutch tree</note> or Palāśa <note>Flame of the Forest</note> trees. If it is difficult to collect Khadira or Palāśa, then any other sacrificial wood may be used, excluding these eleven: the Vibhītaka <note>Beleric Myrobalan</note>, Lodhra, Bādhaka (?), Kadamba, Nimba <note>Neem</note>, Rāja-vṛkṣa <note>Indian Laburnum</note>, Śālmali <note>Silk-cotton tree</note>, Aralu, and Dadhittha <note>Wood-apple</note>. 

Third, for the rituals concerning the gods <note>original: deva-kārya</note>, one should gather several blades of Kuśa grass cut from the "shoulder" <note>the upper portion of the blade</note>. 

Fourth, for the rituals concerning the ancestors <note>original: pitṛ-kārya</note>, one should gather several blades of Kuśa grass cut from the root. If there is difficulty in gathering Kuśa grass, any other grass may be used for the sacrifice, excluding these seven: Śuru-vṛṇā, Śara <note>arrow grass</note>, Śīrya, Aśva-gaja, and Mutava (?). 

Fifth: clarified butter <note>original: ghṛta</note>. 
Sixth: certain grains or barley suitable for the cooked offering <term>Sthālīpāka|a dish of rice or barley cooked in a pot for a sacrifice</term>. 
Seventh: the cooking pot <note>original: caru-sthālī</note>. 
Eighth: the stirring spoon <note>original: mekṣaṇa</note>. 
Ninth: the offering ladle <note>original: sruva</note>. 
Tenth: protected water. 

One should arrange these ten items, along with two more to be mentioned later, and bring them into the fire-room. On that day, one must also observe several rules which will be described. 

First, do not leave the home. 
Second, if one is far away, one should return home for such an occasion. 
Third, one may buy items from others, but must not sell one's own belongings. 
Fourth, be "measured in speech" <note>original: mita-bhāṣī</note>, meaning do not speak more than necessary. 
Fifth, maintain a total desire to speak only the truth. 

Thereafter, in the afternoon, both the husband and wife should bathe and, according to the rules for the day of fasting,* eat to their satisfaction whatever they desire, mixed with clarified butter. ॥ 13-26 ॥

The translation of the fifth section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra is now complete. ॥ 1, 5 ॥

# Mānatantavya said, "The human offerings of this person are not sacrificed..."

---
* That is to say, on the day of fasting, one should not eat salt, honey, meat, or any other items that produce <unclear>disorders</unclear> in the body. Aside from these, all other things may be eaten, but one should not eat excessively (at night). This means one should not eat to the point that indigestion occurs and creates an obstacle for the duties of the following day.

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>Rules for Fasting and Vows in the Rituals</header>

### [Chapter 1, Section 6, Sutras 1-9]

It happens that he who does not eat the <term>Fast-day meal|Aupavasathika: the specific food permitted on the day of preparation before a sacrifice</term> becomes powerless and <term>famished|Kshodhuka: someone suffering from extreme hunger</term>; he becomes unloved by the people, and his offspring become more wretched. He who eats the <term>Fast-day meal</term> becomes powerful and not famished; he becomes loved by the people, and his offspring become more prosperous. Therefore, whatever he desires, he should eat of the fast-day meal. They <note>the sacrificer and his wife</note> should sleep on the floor during this night. They should spend this night in a state of partial wakefulness, passing the time through the telling of <term>History|Itihāsa: ancient legends or sacred narratives</term> or anything else, but they must certainly guard themselves against actions that violate the vow. They say one should not observe the fast while traveling, but the wife's vow still remains. ॥ 9--6 ॥

---

The teacher named 'Mānatantavya' <note>an ancient authority on ritual</note> spoke with certainty, saying: "It is so." This refers to the passage starting from 'He who' and ending with 'the wife's vow.' 

The 'he' refers to the <term>Sacrificer|Yajamāna: the person who institutes and pays for the ritual</term>. If he 'does not eat' the food meant for the day of the fast and remains entirely without food, the 'human offering'—that is, the sacrificial action intended for the benefit of humans—is certainly 'offered' <note>original: āhutā</note> fruitlessly. In this way, a person who is 'famished' or joined with hunger becomes 'powerless' <note>original: anīśvara</note> and unable to perform the ritual acts due to weakness. Furthermore, he becomes 'unloved' or unpleasant even to common people. Also, it is certain that the 'offspring' or descendants of such a hungry man become 'more wretched' <note>original: pāpavasiyasī</note>, falling under the influence of an evil intellect.

On the other hand—'he who eats the fast-day meal' becomes 'not famished,' meaning he is not empty or weak. He becomes 'powerful' in performing the ritual duties because of his strength. Moreover, he becomes 'dear' or loved even by common people. Also, the offspring of such a person become 'more prosperous' <note>original: vasiyasī</note> and self-controlled. Therefore, since there is scriptural condemnation of a hungry sacrificer, 'whatever they desire' of the food meant for the fast-day, 'they two should eat'—referring to the husband and wife. 

On 'this' night of the fast-day, they should 'sleep' 'below'—meaning on the floor. Furthermore, those two 'certainly' should spend 'this night' in 'partial wakefulness'—meaning sleeping for part of the time and staying awake for part of the time. 

To suggest a way to stay awake, it is said: 'through the telling of history.' <term>History|Itihāsa</term> is defined in the Veda as stories such as "The one who did this killed a Brahmin, let him be radiant," and so on. They should spend the night in the company of such contemplation; 'or' they should spend the remaining part of the night awake, discussing the Dharma <note>sacred law and duty</note> with any learned person. They must not...

<vocab>Aupavasathikam (Fast-day meal), Kshodhuka (Famished), Yajamana (Sacrificer), Itihasa (History/Legend), Manatantavya (Teacher's name), Vrateyupavasadiniyamah (Rules for vows and fasting)</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the nutritional and behavioral rules for a sacrificer and his wife on the eve of a ritual, arguing that eating a permitted meal is better than total fasting to maintain strength and prosperity. It also prescribes sleeping on the floor and staying partially awake by listening to sacred legends (Itihāsa).</summary>
<keywords>fasting rules, ritual meal, Mānatantavya, offspring, prosperity, Itihāsa, floor-sleeping, partial wakefulness, sacrificer’s duties</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the ritual fast (Upavasatha) from the previous page, specifically focusing on the teachings of the sage Mānatantavya. It concludes the section on the dietary and behavioral rules for the sacrificer and his wife on the eve of the New or Full Moon sacrifices, emphasizing that physical well-being is essential for spiritual success.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>Commentary on the Gobhila Grihya Sutra</header>

<meta>The following is the Sanskrit commentary on the preceding rules regarding the ritual vigil.</meta>

They should remain awake throughout the night, behaving as if they were just about to shake off sleep. The word "but" (tu) implies that in this waking state, they must "guard themselves" <note>original Sanskrit: jugupseyātām</note> against "unholy behaviors" <note>original Sanskrit: avratyebhyaḥ dharmebhyaḥ</note> such as sexual intercourse; in other words, they must protect their own spiritual state. Regarding the one "away from home" <note>original Sanskrit: pravasan</note> or traveling, some say he "should not fast." However, even in that case, if the wife is at home, the "vow" <term>Vrata|a religious duty or restricted lifestyle taken for a specific period</term> is maintained through her, and the spirit of the vow is not broken. This entire section, from the start of this chapter to this point, represents the views of the teacher Mānatantavya, and it is shown that these views are also acceptable to me <note>the commentator</note>. ॥ 9-9 ॥

> **Explanation:** — An authority named "Mānatantavya" says: "If a <term>Sacrificer|Yajamāna: the person for whom the ritual is performed</term> does not eat on the day of the ritual fast according to the rules of that day, then all the sacrificial actions he performs for the benefit of humanity will be fruitless. By remaining without food on the previous day, he will surely be unable to perform the ritual actions on the following day due to the restlessness caused by the distress of hunger. * Furthermore, he will become unpleasant to ordinary people. Likewise, his children, grandchildren, and subjects will fall under the influence of a sinful mindset (1). (2). The point is that by working without hunger—that is, by eating and keeping the mind steady—all ritual actions will be successfully completed, he will be liked by ordinary people, and his children and subjects will live in glory. Therefore, one should not perform any work while tormented by hunger; both the husband and wife should eat as they desire (of the permitted fast-day food). On the night of the fast, they should not sleep on a bed. Whether by discussing <term>History|Itihāsa: ancient legends and epics</term> or by debating <term>Dharma|Sacred Law and Ethics</term> with others, they should pass the night from beginning to end in a state of wakefulness. This means they should not be overcome by deep sleep throughout the entire night; they may sleep a little, but they must save themselves from actions that break the vow, such as sexual intimacy. One should not keep the fast while traveling, or the vow can be fulfilled by the wife staying at home." ॥ 9-9 ॥

### He may do as he desires. ॥ 10 ॥

The results of both eating and not eating on the day of the ritual fast have been stated. Therefore, "he may do as he desires." If he wishes for powerlessness and poverty, he may abstain from eating; if he wishes for lordship and power, he should eat. ॥ 10 ॥

---

<note>Footnotes from the bottom of the page</note>

* A person with a hungry and restless mind is not fit for any work. (1) Seeing the suffering of their own bodies, the subjects/offspring will turn to unrighteousness such as theft. (2) Following the example of the ancestors, they will realize that performing work while starving is unrighteous; knowing this, the children will distance themselves from the parents' lineage because of the suffering of hunger—meaning they will abandon them. Consequently, it is not incorrect to say that those offspring will be inferior in quality and subject to sin.

<summary>This page concludes the debate on whether to fast completely before a sacrifice, arguing that moderate eating preserves the mental clarity and social standing necessary for successful rituals. It emphasizes that extreme asceticism can lead to spiritual and familial decline.</summary>
<keywords>Mānatantavya, Gobhila Grihya Sutra, ritual fasting, domestic rituals, spiritual psychology, family lineage, Upavasatha</keywords>

<header>Section on the New and Full Moon Sacrifices</header>
<meta>[Chapter 1, Section 6, Sutras 10-14]</meta>

**Commentary:** — It has been explained what the fruit of performing the sacrifice is after eating, and what the specific merit is of performing it while hungry. Between these two options, one may do as they desire. ॥ 10 ॥

### In the same way, the ritual vigil applies to the one who maintains the sacred fires, as prescribed by the Sacred Tradition. ॥ 11, 12

"In the same way" <note>original Sanskrit: evam</note> refers to the method already described. "Even for the one who maintains the sacred fires" <term>Ahitagni|A person who has formally established and maintains the three perpetual Vedic fires</term>, the "ritual vigil" <note>original Sanskrit: upavasathaḥ</note> or the rule of fasting applies. Whatever is required for the ritual vigil—such as smearing the ritual ground with cow dung—is what the "Sacred Tradition" <term>Amnaya|The Vedas or traditional sacred learning</term> is fit to "prescribe" <note>original Sanskrit: vidadhyāt</note>. It is by remembering those very rules that we have established these procedures to produce faith. ॥ 11, 12 ॥

**Commentary:** — All the aforementioned rules of the ritual fast and its regulations also apply to the *Ahitagni*; this is how the Vedic injunction is understood. ॥ 11, 12 ॥

### Then, in the forenoon, having offered the morning oblation, and having passed around the front of the fire, one should spread Darbha grass to the south of the fire with their tips pointing eastward. Standing in front of them facing west, one should take a blade of grass from the seat of the Brahma priest using the thumb and the ring finger of the left hand, and discard it toward the south-western corner with the mantra: "Pāravasu is cast away." ॥ 13, 14

"Then" <note>original Sanskrit: atha</note> refers to the following day, the first day of the lunar fortnight <note>Pratipada</note>. In the forenoon, having offered the morning oblation, "around the front of the fire" <note>original Sanskrit: agreṇa</note> means keeping the fire in front of oneself while "circumambulating" <note>original Sanskrit: parikramya</note> it. To the "south" of the "fire," one should "spread" <note>original Sanskrit: āstīrya</note> or lay down "Darbha grass" <term>Darbha|A sacred grass used in rituals, valued for its purifying properties</term> with its "tips pointing east" <note>original Sanskrit: prāgagrān</note>. Standing "in front" of that laid grass "facing west," using the "thumb" and the "ring finger" <note>original Sanskrit: upakaniṣṭhikayā; literally "the one next to the little finger"</note> of the "left hand," one should "take" a single "blade of grass" from the "Seat of the Brahma" <term>Brahmāsana|The designated seat for the Brahma priest, who acts as the silent supervisor of the entire ritual</term>—which is a pile of grass set down for the priest's seat—and "discard" <note>original Sanskrit: nirasyati</note> it toward the "south-west corner" <note>original Sanskrit: naiṛtaṃ koṇam</note>, which is the eighth direction between south and west. This is done with the mantra: "*Nirastaḥ parāvasuḥ*" <note>Meaning: "The enemy/demon Parāvasu is thrown away"</note>. ॥ This is the ritual of discarding the grass. ॥ 13, 14 ॥

**Commentary:** — Thereafter, on the following day—that is, on the first day of the lunar fortnight—having completed the morning oblation according to the rules in the forenoon, one should keep the fire in front, walk around it, and lay down several blades of Kusha grass to the south of the fire. The tips of those blades of Kusha should point toward the east. Facing west in front of that seat, using the thumb and ring finger of the hand, [one takes a blade] from the Brahma's...

6
