<meta>This page continues the preparation for the New and Full Moon sacrifices. While the previous page described preparing the seat for the Brahma priest and discarding a blade of grass, this section details how the Brahma priest formally takes his seat and the conduct he must maintain throughout the ritual.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila-Grihyasutra—</header>

From the Kusha grass seat laid out for him, he should take one blade of grass and throw it toward the southwest corner <note>the direction of the deity Nirriti, associated with destruction; this act removes obstacles</note> with the mantra "Paravasu is cast away." This specific act is known as the "discarding of the grass" <term>Trinanirasana|A ritual cleansing of the priest's seat</term>. ॥ 13, 14 ॥

### After touching water, the Brahma priest sits on the seat, saying "I sit in the seat of the wealth-giver." Facing the fire, he remains silent with folded hands until the completion of the ritual. He should not speak non-ritual speech. If he speaks non-ritual speech, he should whisper a verse to Vishnu or a prose formula, or he may simply say, "Salutations to Vishnu." ॥ 15-20 ॥

"Then" <note>original: atha</note> afterward, the priest known as the "Brahma"—who acts as the supervisor of all ritual activities—should "touch" <note>original: upasprysha</note> "water" <note>original: apah</note>. He then "sits" <note>original: upavishati</note> on the prepared seat while reciting the "mantra" <note>original: iti mantramuccaran</note>: "I sit in the seat of the wealth-giver" <note>original: avasoh sadane sidami</note>. 

"Until the completion of the ritual" <note>original: a karmanah paryavasanat</note>, he should "sit" <note>original: asita</note> "facing the fire" <note>original: agnimabhimukhah</note>—specifically facing north—with "folded hands" <note>original: pranjalih</note> and "restrained speech" <note>original: vagyatah</note>. Having restrained his speech means he is devoid of any talk other than words related to the sacrifice. The text clarifies this: he should "speak" <note>original: bhasheta</note> only "ritual speech" <note>original: yajnasamsiddhim</note> which is helpful to the sacrifice, and "must not speak non-ritual speech" <note>original: ayajniyam vacam na vadet</note>. 

"If" <note>original: yadi</note>, even by mistake, he "speaks non-ritual speech," he should "recite" <note>original: japet</note> or read any "verse" <note>original: rcam</note> or "prose formula" <note>original: yajurva</note> dedicated to the "deity Vishnu" <note>original: vaishnavim</note>. "Or rather" <note>original: apiva</note>, simply saying "Salutations to Vishnu" <note>original: namo vishnave</note> will suffice. ॥ 15-20 ॥

**Explanation:** After this, the eminent person named the Brahma, who supervises all tasks, washes his hands and feet with water. He sits on the prepared Kusha grass seat facing the fire (specifically facing North). He folds both hands and says, "I shall remain in this place until the conclusion of the sacrifice." He should then firmly resolve in his mind to speak only regulated speech until the work is finished. Whatever instructions need to be given regarding the sacrifice, he may say those; he should not speak any other sentences. If by error he says something else, he must immediately recite a verse from the Rigveda or Yajurveda that brings the deity Vishnu to mind; otherwise, simply saying "Salutations to Vishnu" will be enough to manage the error. ॥ 15-20 ॥

### If one person desires to perform both roles—that of the Reciter and the Brahma priest—then by that same procedure, he should place an umbrella, an upper garment, a water pot, or a bundle of Darbha grass on the Brahma's seat. Having returned by that same path, he may then perform the other duties. ॥ 21-26 ॥

"If indeed" <note>original: yadi u vai</note> a single person "desires to perform" <note>original: cikirset</note> "both" <note>original: ubhayameva</note> roles—namely the "duties of the Hotri" <term>Hautra|The role of the priest who recites the Vedic hymns</term> and the "duties of the Brahma" <term>Brahmatva|The role of the presiding supervisor</term>...

<page-num>39</page-num>

<header>[ Chapter 1, Section 6, Sutras 15-21; Section 7, Sutras 1-3 ] Procedure for the New and Full Moon Sacrifices</header>

...if he is prevented from staying, then "by this same method" <note>original: etenaiva kalpena</note> as previously described, he should place a "parasol" <note>original: chatra</note>, or an "upper garment" <note>original: uttarasanga</note>, or a "water-pot" <note>original: udakamandalu</note> filled with water, or a <term>Darbhabatu|A ritual effigy made of sacred Kusha grass representing a Brahmin priest</term> in the seat of the <term>Brahma</term>. Having placed it there and "returned" <note>original: pratyavartya</note> in the same manner as before (by circumambulating to the right), "then" <note>original: atha</note> afterward, he should perform all the general ritual acts described in the <term>Agnihotra|The daily fire-offering ritual</term> section—such as the prayers to the earth—excluding those specific tasks related to the cooking of the grain offering <note>original: charu</note> which will be explained later. ॥ 21 ॥

**Thus ends the commentary on the sixth section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Grihya Sutra. ॥ 1-6 ॥**

**Translation:** If a single person wishes to perform the duties of both the <term>Hotri</term> <note>the reciter of hymns</note> and the <term>Brahma</term> <note>the ritual supervisor</note>, he should place a parasol, an upper garment, a water-pot full of water, or a Brahmin figure made of <term>Kusha</term> grass upon the designated seat for the <term>Brahma</term>. He should then return to his own seat as the <term>Hotri</term> by walking around it clockwise. Following this, he performs all the standard fire rituals mentioned in the Agnihotra section, such as the prayers to the earth. (The specific rules regarding the cooking of the rice-pot offering will be described later). ॥ 21 ॥

**The translation of the sixth section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Grihya Sutra is complete. ॥ 1.6 ॥**

***

### Now, after washing the mortar, the pestle, and the winnowing basket, he places them to the west of the fire upon Kusha grass laid with the tips pointing eastward. ॥ 1 ॥

"Now" <note>original: atha</note> indicates the beginning of the next sequence. After "washing" <note>original: prakshalya</note> the <term>Ulukhala|A wooden mortar used for de-husking grain</term>, the <term>Mushala|A heavy wooden pestle</term>, and the <term>Shurpa|A wicker winnowing basket</term>, he "places them down" <note>original: upasadayati</note> to the "west of the fire" <note>original: agneh pashchat</note> upon "sacred grass" <term>Darbha</term> that has been spread out with the "tips pointing east" <note>original: pragagran</note>. ॥ 1 ॥

**Translation:** Next, after thoroughly washing the mortar, the pestle, and the winnowing basket with water, he should place them behind (to the west of) the sacred fire. He must first spread several blades of <term>Kusha</term> grass with their tips pointing toward the east and then set the tools upon them. ॥ 1 ॥

### Now he pours out the oblation—either rice or barley—using either a bronze vessel or the pot for the grain-offering, saying "To [Deity Name], I pour thee, acceptable," naming the deity once aloud and twice silently. ॥ 2-3 ॥

"Now" <note>original: atha</note> that the tools are prepared, he "pours out" <note>original: nirvapati</note> the "oblation" <note>original: havih</note> into the mortar. This consists of either "rice" <term>Vrihi</term> or "barley" <term>Yava</term>. He uses either a "bronze vessel" <note>original: kamsena</note> or the <term>Charusthali|The specific pot used for cooking the sacrificial grain</term>.

<vocab>Darshapurnamasa, Gobhila-Grihyasutra, Ulukhala (mortar), Mushala (pestle), Shurpa (winnowing basket), Havi (oblation), Vrihi (rice), Yava (barley), Darbha (sacred grass)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the procedure for a single priest to fulfill two roles by using a symbolic substitute (like a parasol or grass figure) for the Brahma priest. It also begins the preparation of grain for the sacrifice, detailing the cleaning and placement of the mortar and pestle.</summary>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Grhya Sutra ॥</header>

The act of throwing <note>original: prakshepa</note> the grain should be performed. The mantra used is "I pour you out, acceptable to so-and-so" <note>original: amushmai tva jushtam nirvapami</note>. Here, because the word "so-and-so" <note>original: amushmai-pada</note> is heard in the scripture, whichever deity the offering is intended for should be mentioned by name. For example, when pouring an offering for Agni, one says, "I pour you out, acceptable to Agni." The text says "with this mantra" and mentions "naming the deities" <note>original: devatanam-adesham</note>. The pouring <note>original: nirvapati</note> is connected with doing this "once" <note>sakit</note> with the mantra, and "twice" <note>dvih</note> "silently" <note>tushnim</note>, meaning without the mantra and without naming the deities. This concludes the pouring ceremony. ॥ 2, 3 ॥

**Translation of the Commentary:**
Thereafter, for the purpose of cooking the offering—whether it be rice-paddy or barley—one should throw it from a bronze vessel or from the offering-pot <note>original: charu-sthali</note>. However much grain is required for the offering should be thrown in only three handfuls. Out of these, one time should be done with the mantra: "I put you into this mortar, O rice (or barley), to make you fit for the enjoyment of [Name of Deity]." The other two times, the grain should be put in without any mantra. ॥ 2–3 ॥

# Next, standing facing the east, he begins to husk the grain; he should wash the grains, which have been husked three times, three times for the gods, twice for men, and once for the ancestors. ॥ 4, 5 ॥

"Then" <note>atha</note>—meaning after the pouring—standing "behind" the mortar and "facing east" <note>prang-mukhah</note>, he "begins to husk" <note>avahantum upakramate</note> with "both hands" <note>dakshina-uttarabhyam panibhyam</note>, the right being above the left. There, having taken the "rice grains" <note>tandula</note> of either paddy or barley which have been "husked three times" <note>tri-phalikritan</note> (meaning the husks have been removed), he should "wash" <note>prakshalayet</note> them. As the "ancients say" <note>ahuh purvatanah</note>: wash them "three times" for the sake of divine rituals <note>devebhyah</note>, "twice" for the sake of human rituals like feeding Brahmins <note>manushyebhyah</note>, and "once" for the sake of ancestral rituals <note>pitribhyah</note>. This concludes the husking and washing. ॥ 4–5 ॥

**Translation of the Commentary:**
Afterward, standing behind the mortar facing the east and grasping the pestle with both hands, he should pound the grain. After pounding, the rice or barley grains <term>Tandula|Grains that have been freed from their husks</term> should be cleaned three times. Then, wash them in water: three times for a ritual for the gods, two times for a ritual for humans (such as feeding Brahmins), and only once for a ritual for the ancestors. This is the opinion of the ancient teachers. ॥ 5 ॥

# He should pour the rice grains into the pot while they are still between the strainers; he should cook the pot-offering so it is well-cooked, stirring it clockwise, and after sprinkling butter on it, he should remove it towards the north and sprinkle butter on it again. ॥ 6—8 ॥

---
<margin>* Grain that is merely the seed, devoid of the husk or outer covering <note>original: tusha</note>, is called <term>Tandula</term>. ॥</margin>

<summary>This page describes the ritual preparation of grain offerings, including the specific mantras for dedicating grain to deities and the rules for husking and washing rice based on whether the ceremony is for gods, humans, or ancestors.</summary>

<page-num>41</page-num>
# Section on the New and Full Moon Sacrifices ॥
### [Chapter 1, Section 7, Sutras 4-15]

One should take the "rice grains" which have been "placed within the purifiers" <note>original: pavitrantarhitan; the grains are placed inside a special ritual strainer or sieve made of Kusha grass blades</note> for the purpose of washing them, and "pour them" from that strainer into the cooking pot <note>original: sthalyam</note>. Moving in a "clockwise direction" <note>original: pradakshinam</note> and "mixing them" with a wooden stirring ladle <note>original: mekshana</note>, one should "cook" that "pot-offering" <note>original: sthalipaka</note> until it is "perfectly cooked," as if prepared by a master chef. After the cooking is complete, "sprinkle clarified butter" <note>original: ghrita</note> over the cooked food; then, move it "to the north" of the fire, "set it down," and "sprinkle it with clarified butter again." This is how the pot-offering is prepared. ॥ 6-8 ॥

**Translation of the Commentary:**
The rice grains, which were taken for washing and held in the "purifier" (a well-known ritual object made of Kusha grass with many gaps), should be taken from there and placed in the cooking pot. During the cooking process, one should stir the grains up and down using the "wooden ladle" <note>original: mekshana</note>. It is essential that this dish be prepared as skillfully as if made by an expert cook. Once the offering is ready, a stream of clarified butter (ghee) should be poured over it. It should then be removed from the fire and placed to the north, and finally, clarified butter should be added again according to the prescribed portions. ॥ 6, 7, 8 ॥

**Having established the fire, one should spread Kusha grass all around it—first to the east, then the south, then the north, and finally the west. This should be done in three layers or five layers, using a large amount of grass, ensuring they are not bunched in even-numbered pairs. The tips of the grass should face east, with the tips of each subsequent layer covering the roots of the previous ones. Alternatively, after spreading them in the west, one should extend them toward the south and then the north, making sure the tips overlap from south to north. This is the standard rule for spreading grass in all rituals involving burnt offerings. ॥ 6-15 ॥**

Although the instruction for the pot-offering on the sacrificial grass follows shortly (in Sutra 18), the method for "spreading the grass" <note>original: paristarana</note> is described here because it must be performed before the pot-offering is removed from the fire. First, "having established the fire" by kindling it with fuel sticks, one should "spread" or cover the area "all around" the blazing fire in all directions with bundles of "Kusha grass." The sequence is: "in the east," then "in the south," then "in the north," and finally "in the west." Furthermore, in all these directions, the spreading should be "three-layered or five-layered." One should use "a large amount" of grass, but in such a way that the blades do not form "even pairs" or "clumps." The distinction is that a union of two blades is a "pair," while a union of three or more is a "clump." Furthermore, the grass should be laid with "tips facing east," and with these "tips," one should "cover the roots" of the grass previously laid down; this continues for the subsequent layers. "Or," if one does not cover the roots with the tips, one should "first spread" in the "west," then the "south," and then the "north," moving "eastward"...

---

<vocab>Sthalipaka (pot-offering), Pavitra (ritual purifier/strainer), Mekshana (stirring ladle), Paristarana (strewing grass), Kusha (sacred grass), Ghrita (clarified butter/ghee)</vocab>

<summary>This page details the expert cooking of the Sthalipaka rice offering, including stirring and anointing with ghee, followed by the precise ritual of spreading Kusha grass around the sacrificial fire in specific layers and directions.</summary>
<keywords>Sthalipaka, pot-offering, Kusha grass, Paristarana, sacrificial fire rituals, Gobhila Grhya Sutra, ritual purity, Vedic cooking</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the New and Full Moon sacrifices. While the previous page detailed the cooking of the grain offering (Sthalipaka), this page describes the ritual preparation of the sacred space, specifically the arrangement of sacred grass and boundary sticks around the sacrificial fire.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>On the Commentary of the Gobhila Grhya Sutra—</header>

Regarding the eastern direction, the phrase 'should draw' <note>original: prakarshet</note> means one should mix or arrange the grass by pulling it forward. In doing so, the 'tips' <note>original: agrani</note> of the Kusha grass should be 'made' to face 'south and north' <note>original: dakshinottarani</note>. This specific 'method' <note>original: nyayah</note> of <term>paristarana</term> <note>the ritual strewing of sacred grass around the sacrificial fire to create a clean, sacred boundary</note> is to be 'understood' as applicable 'in all ritual procedures involving burnt offerings' <note>original: sarveshu ahutimatsu</note>. ॥ 9-15 ॥

> **Commentary:** In the nineteenth sutra, a different ritual preparation for the pot-offering <note>sthalipaka</note> will be mentioned; therefore, one should perform the "**paristarana**" <note>strewing of grass</note> specifically before removing the pot-offering from the fire. For example, after kindling the fire by tossing in the fuel sticks <note>samit</note>, one should cover the area around said fire with Kusha grass. First in the east, then in the south, followed by the north, and finally in the west—on all sides, one should cover the area three or five times with Kusha grass. However, this covering should be done with such skill that the grass blades do not clump together in groups of two, three, or more in one spot. All the tips of the Kusha grass must point toward the east, and the roots <note>mula</note> of those same blades should be covered by the tips of the preceding ones. Alternatively, if the supply of Kusha grass is limited, one may skip the western side and instead draw the grass toward the east from the south (using south-pointing grass) and from the north (using north-pointing grass). This means that instead of a circular or square covering, a triangular arrangement may be formed. This act is called "**paristarana**"; it is to be utilized in all types of rituals that feature burnt offerings. ॥ 9, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14, 15 ॥

-> **Some also employ boundary-sticks made of Shami or Palasha wood. 16** <-

'Some' <note>original: eke</note> teachers 'employ' <note>original: kurvanti</note> 'boundary-sticks' <note>original: paridhin; ritual wooden sticks placed around the fire to contain its energy</note> which serve as borders, made either of 'Shami' <note>original: shamila; Prosopis cineraria, a tree sacred in Vedic ritual</note> wood 'or' 'Palasha' <note>original: parnan; Butea monosperma, known as "Flame of the Forest"</note> wood, according to the characteristics described in the *Karmapradipa* <note>a classical manual on ritual procedure</note>. ॥ 16 ॥

> **Commentary:** Certain teachers also establish a <term>paridhi</term> <note>a boundary or fence</note> using sticks of Shami wood or Palasha wood. ॥ 16 ॥

-> **To the north, a ladle full of water serves as the Pranita; or, according to some, it is used if the vessel is unavailable. 17, 18** <-

'To the north' of the fire, 'a ladle' <note>original: sruvah</note> 'full of water' should be 'known by the name' '<term>pranita</term>' <note>sacred water used to sanctify the ritual area</note>. 'Some' teachers say that 'if' the previously mentioned ritual cup <note>chamasa</note> 'is not available,' the water-filled <term>sruva</term> serves as the <term>pranita</term>; this view is not contradictory. ॥ 17-18 ॥

> **Commentary:** One should keep a <term>sruva</term> <note>a small offering ladle</note> filled with water to the north of the fire; this is called the <term>pranita</term>. Some teachers state that if water is already kept in the ritual cup <note>chamasa</note> mentioned earlier, there is no harm in not placing water in the ladle. ॥ 17, 18 ॥

It also defines the Pranita water offering, which may be held in a ladle (Sruva) placed to the north of the fire.</summary>

<header>[ Section 1, Lesson 7, Sutras 19-23 ] The Section on the New and Full Moon Sacrifices ||</header>

# Having placed the pot-offering on the sacred grass and adding fuel sticks to the fire, one prepares the ritual fats—
# Clarified butter, oil, curd, milk, or barley-gruel. 19, 20 ||

Regarding the word 'on the sacred grass' <note>original: "barhishi"</note>, it refers to the collection of Kusha grass already strewn. There, one should 'place' <note>original: "asadya"</note> the 'pot-offering' <note>original: "sthalipakam"</note>, which refers here to the vessel containing the grain cooked in a pot. Then follows the 'consecration of the ritual fat' <note>original: "ajya-samskarah"</note>. Having 'added' <note>original: "abhyadhaya"</note> the 'fuel sticks' <note>original: "idhmam"</note>—the previously mentioned wood from the Palasha tree or others—to the fire, one kindles the fire well. Then, in that blazing fire, one 'prepares' <note>original: "samskurute"</note> the <term>Ajya</term> <note>a general term for sacrificial fats, usually clarified butter</note>, which is one of the substances mentioned immediately after: 'clarified butter' <note>original: "sarpis"</note>, 'sesame oil' <note>original: "tailam"</note>, 'curd' <note>original: "dadhi"</note>, 'milk' <note>original: "payas/dugdham"</note>, or 'barley-gruel' <note>original: "yavagu"</note>. 19, 20 ||

**Translation of the Commentary:** After placing the pot-offering on those strewn tips of Kusha grass, and again adding fuel to light the fire, one should perform the consecration of the ritual fat within it. In this context, the **Ajya** <note>ritual fat</note> can be performed with whichever of these five items is available: clarified butter, oil, curd, milk, or barley-gruel. ॥ 19 ॥ 20 ॥

---

Since the ritual fat must then be purified with two strainers, the method for making the strainers is described first;

# From that same sacred grass, one makes two strainers a span long; placing a blade of herb in between, one cuts them—not with the fingernail—saying, "You two are strainers, belonging to Vishnu." He then wipes them with water, saying, "You two are purified by the mind of Vishnu." 21-23 ||

'From that' <note>original: "tatah"</note> refers to the 'same sacred grass' <note>original: "barhishah eva"</note> collected previously. One 'makes' <note>original: "kurute"</note> a 'strainer' <note>original: "pavitram"</note> of a 'span's length' <note>original: "pradeshamatre," the distance from the tip of the thumb to the tip of the index finger</note>. To the question of "how," the text says: 'placing in between' <note>original: "antardhaya"</note> an 'herb' <note>original: "oshadhim," such as a blade of rice-grass</note>, one 'cuts' <note>original: "chhinatti"</note> them with the mantra: "You two are strainers, belonging to Vishnu," but 'not with the fingernail' <note>original: "na nakhena"</note>. Thereafter, one 'wipes' <note>original: "anumarshti"</note> or cleanses 'these' <note>original: "ete"</note> two strainers 'with water' <note>original: "adbhir"</note> while reciting the mantra: "You two are purified by the mind of Vishnu." 21-23 || Having prepared these two strainers, the text then ordains the purification of the ritual fat;—

**Translation of the Commentary:** Next, taking two blades of Kusha grass of a span's length (about 9 inches) from the previously gathered grass, one should cut them in the middle using another herb while reciting the mantra: "You belong to the deity Vishnu, and thus you are pure." One must not use the fingernail for this cutting. After that, wash them in water while reciting the mantra: "By the very intent of the deity Vishnu, you two are purified." ॥ 21-23 ॥

# Having purified [the fat], he strains it upwards with the two strainers held by the thumbs...

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the ritual purification of sacrificial substances, specifically the clarified butter (Ajya), following the preparation of the ritual strainers mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>On the Commentary of the Gobhila Grihya Sutra ||</header>
<meta>image quality: clear, slightly clipped on the left edge</meta>

<unclear>H</unclear>aving grasped [the strainers] with the thumb and ring fingers, he purifies [the butter] three times toward the east, saying: "May the divine Savitri purify you with a flawless strainer, with the rays of the sun, the vessel of wealth." This is done once with the <term>Yajus</term> <note>a prose sacrificial formula from the Yajurveda</note> and twice silently. || 24, 25 ||

'Having purified' <note>original: "sampuya"</note> means that after cleaning those two "strainers" <note>pavitra</note> in the manner previously described, and using those very 'strainers'—which should have their tips pointing north—he 'purifies' <note>original: "utpunati"</note> the clarified butter; this is the intended meaning. It means that any straw or debris that has fallen into the butter should be lifted out and thrown into the fire. How should it be done? How many times? With which mantra? The text answers these three questions: Having 'grasped' <note>original: "samgruhya"</note> them 'on both sides' <note>original: "abhi"</note> using the 'thumbs' and the 'ring fingers' <note>original: "upakanishthikabhyam/anamikabhyam"</note>, he 'purifies' <note>original: "utpunati"</note> them 'three times' <note>original: "trih"</note> in such a way that the motion 'goes eastward' <note>original: "prakshas"</note>. Out of those three times, he does it 'once' <note>original: "sakrit"</note> with the 'Yajus' formula "May the divine..." and 'twice' <note>original: "dvih"</note> 'silently' <note>original: "tushnim"</note>—that is, without a mantra. This is the process of purifying the butter. || 24, 25 ||

**Vernacular Commentary:** Having cleaned the two "strainers" <note>pavitra</note> as described and pointing their tips northward, one should perform the purification of the sacrificial butter. This means removing any straw that has fallen into the butter and throwing it toward the east. During this "purification of the butter," one should hold the two strainers with the thumb and ring finger, reciting the <term>Yajus</term> mantra "May the divine..." once, and then performing the purification twice more without a mantra. || 24, 25 ||

Then, having sprinkled these two [strainers] with water, he should throw them into the fire. After placing this clarified butter over the fire to heat it, he should set it down to the north. In this way, the ritual procedure for the consecration of the butter is completed. || 26-28 ||

'Then' <note>original: "atha"</note> refers to the time after the purification of the butter. 'These two' <note>original: "ene"</note> strainers, after being 'sprinkled' <note>original: "abhyukshya"</note> with water—implying they are washed—should be 'thrown' <note>original: "utsrijet"</note> 'into the fire' <note>original: "agnau api"</note>. The word 'also' <note>original: "api"</note> suggests that there is no fault in discarding them elsewhere if needed. 'Then' <note>original: "atha"</note> afterward, having 'placed over' <note>original: "adhishritya"</note> that same 'butter' <note>original: "ajyam"</note>—meaning putting it directly on the glowing coals—he should 'set it down' <note>original: "udvasayet"</note> to the 'north' <note>original: "udak"</note> of the fire. 'In this way' <note>original: "evam"</note> the 'ritual procedure for the consecration' <note>original: "samskarakalpah"</note> of the 'butter' 'is completed' <note>original: "bhavati"</note>. || 26-28 ||

Thus ends the explanation of the seventh section of the first lesson in the Gobhila Grihya Sutra. || 1, 7 ||

**Vernacular Commentary:** After purifying the butter, wash these two "strainers" in water and throw them into the fire. Afterward, place the "purified butter vessel" on several glowing coals to the north of the fire. Perform the consecration of the butter and so on. || 26, 27, 28 ||

The vernacular translation of the seventh section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Grihya Sutra is complete. || 1, 7 ||

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the ritual of the New and Full Moon sacrifices, moving from the purification of the butter to the physical arrangement of the ritual vessels and the specific methods of making offerings into the fire.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>[Chapter 1, Section 7, Sutras 25; 25, Section 8, Sutras 1-4] The Chapter on the New and Full Moon Sacrifices</header>
<meta>Catchword: 7</meta>

To the north of the fire itself, the 'front' <note>original: "purva"</note> position—that is, toward the east—is the place for the 'clarified butter' <note>original: "ajyam"</note>, meaning the vessel containing the butter. However, in that same northern area, the 'rear' <note>original: "apara"</note> position—to the west of the butter—should be the place for the 'pot of cooked rice' <note>original: "sthalipakha"</note>, the vessel that holds the cooked ritual offering. || 1 ||

Explanation: An arrangement has been established for placing both the pot of cooked rice and the vessel of clarified butter to the north of the ritual fire. (1.4.5, 1.7.26-28) Among these, the butter vessel shall be placed first, and the rice pot should be placed behind it. || 15 ||

## Having sprinkled water around, poured clarified butter into the cooked rice, he should begin the sacrifice by making an 'Upaghata' offering with the stirring ladle. || 2 ||

'Having sprinkled water' <note>original: "paryukshya"</note> refers to the sequence of actions starting from the mantra "Aditi, please permit..." and ending with the formal sprinkling around the fire. In the 'cooked rice' <note>original: "sthalipake"</note> offering, after 'pouring' <note>original: "aniya"</note> or adding any of the fats, such as 'clarified butter' <note>original: "ajyam"</note>, he should 'commence' <note>original: "upakramate"</note> to perform the 'offering' <note>original: "hotum"</note>. This specific offering is called an 'Upaghata' <note>a "striking-up" or direct scoop offering</note>, performed with a 'stirring ladle' <note>original: "mekshanena"</note>, which is a special kind of wooden spoon. If one first takes butter into the large offering spoon using the small dipping spoon, then takes the rice, and then takes butter again, that is called an 'under-and-over-poured' <note>original: "upastirnabhigharitam"</note> offering. In that method, the butter taken before the rice is called the "under-layer" <note>upastarana</note>, and the butter added afterward is called the "over-topping" <note>abhigharana</note>. However, a sacrifice where this layering and topping do not occur is known specifically as an 'Upaghata' sacrifice. || 2 ||

Explanation: In any fire ritual, after completing the previously mentioned tasks (1.3.1-5) ending with the 'sprinkling' <note>original: "paryukshana"</note> accompanied by the mantra "Aditi, please permit...", one should add butter to the cooked rice and prepare to perform the 'Upaghata' sacrifice.* || 2 ||

## If, however, he should wish to offer with the under-and-over-poured method, he should first offer the two portions of clarified butter. Having taken the butter in four helpings—or five helpings for the Bhrigu lineage—he should offer it to the east, saying "To Agni, Svaha!" on the north side, and "To Soma, Svaha!" on the south side. || 3, 4 ||

'If, however' <note>original: "yadi u va"</note> means that whenever 'he should wish to offer' <note>original: "juhuvet"</note> the rice using the 'under-and-over-poured' <note>original: "upastirnabhigharitam"</note> method, he must 'first' <note>original: "prathamau"</note> 'offer' <note>original: "juhuyat"</note> the 'two butter portions' <note>original: "ajyabhagau"</note>, which serve as the foundation for the main sacrifice. These two butter offerings themselves are part of the Upaghata procedure when they act as a prelude to the layered offering. Here, the text describes the procedure: 'taken in four helpings' <note>original: "chaturgrihitam"</note> means the butter is scooped four times with the dipping spoon into the offering spoon.

---

<note>* Within the context of the Sutras, taking butter with the dipping spoon before scooping the rice is called "under-pouring" <note>upastarana</note> or laying a bed, and taking butter again after scooping the rice is called "over-pouring" <note>abhigharana</note> or covering. Accordingly, the sacrifice performed by taking butter, then rice, then butter again is called 'under-and-over-poured' <note>upastirnabhigharita</note>. A sacrifice where this under-pouring or over-pouring is not required is called an 'Upaghata' sacrifice. This explanation is provided so the terminology is not confused.</note>

<sig>7</sig>

<vocab>Darshapurnamasa (New and Full Moon rituals), Sthalipaka (cooked rice offering), Ajya (clarified butter), Mekshana (stirring ladle), Upaghata (direct scoop offering), Upastarana (under-layering with butter), Abhigharana (over-topping with butter), Chaturgrihita (four-fold scooping), Bhrigu (a prominent priestly lineage)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the spatial arrangement of ritual vessels north of the fire and distinguishes between two methods of offering: the direct 'Upaghata' method and the layered 'Upastirnabhigharita' method involving clarified butter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the ritual offerings of the New and Full Moon sacrifices. The previous page ended with the introduction of the Upaghata offering (the "striking" or direct scoop offering). This page details the placement of these offerings and the transition to the more complex layered offering (Upastirnabhigharita).</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>Commentary on the Gobhila Grhyasutra</header>

'Clarified butter' <note>original: "ajya"</note>, which refers to any of the sacrificial fats like ghee, 'having taken' it 'in the middle of the fire' itself, he should offer it to the north—on the northern side—with the mantra 'To Agni, Hail!' <note>original: "Agnaye Svaha"</note>. Then, 'to the south'—on the southern side—he should offer with the mantra 'To Soma, Hail!' <note>original: "Somaya Svaha"</note>. He should perform the offering 'forward-moving' <note>original: "prakshah"</note>, meaning in a way that moves toward the east. These two specific offerings are known as the 'Upaghata' <note>a "striking-up" or scooping offering made with a ladle</note> offerings. Here there is a distinction: for the 'Bhrigus' <note>original: "Bhrigunam"</note>, those born of the Bhrigu ancestral lineage, there is a 'fivefold scooping' <note>original: "panchavattam"</note>, meaning the clarified butter must be taken five times. || 3. 4 || Now, the author describes the procedure for the layered and sprinkled offering <note>original: "upastirnabhigharita"</note>.

Explanation: At the time one wishes to perform the offering known as "layered and sprinkled" <note>original: "upastirnabhigharita"</note>, one must first perform the two "Upaghata" offerings. In performing these "Upaghata" offerings, one must take the clarified butter into the middle of the sacrificial ladle <note>original: "sruj"</note> four times, pouring it from the smaller ladle <note>original: "sruva"</note> each time. This butter, having been taken four times, should first be offered into the middle of the fire-pit toward the north using the mantra "To Agni, Hail!" and afterward into the south of the fire-pit using the mantra "To Soma," while moving toward the eastern direction. A special note: For those belonging to the Bhrigu lineage, the clarified butter should be taken five times for the offering. || 3, 4 ||

### Now, having spread a layer [of butter] for the oblation, he scoops from the middle and from the front part if he is a "four-scooper"; if he is a "five-scooper," he scoops from the middle, from the front part, and from the back part. He then sprinkles butter over the portions and anoints the places from which the portions were taken to preserve their ritual potency. He should offer this into the middle with the mantra "To Agni, Hail!" either once or three times according to the threefold procedure. || 5-10 ||

'Now' <note>original: "atha"</note> signifies the sequence following the Upaghata offerings. 'Having spread a layer' <note>original: "upastirya"</note> means making the sacrificial ladle oily by pouring butter into it first. Upon that layer, he 'scoops' <note>original: "avadyati"</note> the 'oblation' <note>original: "havishah"</note>, which is the pot of cooked rice <note>original: "charu"</note>. He describes the method of scooping: if he 'is a four-scooper' <note>original: "chaturavatti"</note>, meaning anyone other than the Bhrigu lineage, he scoops from the 'middle' and then from the 'front' <note>original: "purvaddhat"</note>. Furthermore, if he 'is a five-scooper' <note>original: "panchavatti"</note>, meaning a member of the Bhrigu lineage, he scoops from the 'middle,' the 'front,' and then from the 'rear' <note>original: "pashchaddhat"</note>; 'this' <note>original: "iti"</note> is the fixed rule.

He then 'sprinkles' <note>original: "abhigharayati"</note> those 'portions' <note>original: "avadanani"</note>—whether they consist of four or five scoops—meaning he should again let a stream of butter fall from the small ladle over them. Furthermore, he 'anoints' <note>original: "pratyanakti"</note> the 'places of the scoops' <note>original: "avadanast-hanani"</note> in the pot of cooked rice from which the four or five portions were taken. This is done for the 'preservation of ritual potency' <note>original: "ayatayamata," literally "non-exhaustion"</note>; since the removal of portions might render the remainder unfit for sacrifice, this action protects its sacrificial readiness. Wherever the wooden stirrer <note>original: "mekshana"</note> left a mark from scooping, he should apply or anoint it with butter. 

Then, taking that oblation which has been layered and sprinkled, he 'should offer' it 'into the middle' <note>original: "madhye"</note> of the fire while reciting the mantra "To Agni, Hail!" This specific act is what is called the 'Upastirnabhigharita' offering here. 'By this procedure' <note>original: "etena kalpena"</note> as described, he may offer it 'once' <note>original: "sakrit"</note> 'or three times' <note>original: "trirva"</note>. || 5-10 ||

<summary>This page details the specific techniques for making offerings into the ritual fire, distinguishing between the initial scooping offerings (Upaghata) and the more formal layered offerings (Upastirnabhigharita). It highlights ancestral differences in ritual practice, specifically the "four-scoop" versus "five-scoop" rule for the Bhrigu lineage.</summary>

<keywords>Upaghata offering, Upastirnabhigharita, Bhrigu lineage, clarified butter, Agni, Soma, ritual scooping, sacrificial ladle, cooked rice offering, ritual potency</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the New and Full Moon sacrifices. While the previous page introduced the "Upaghata" (scooping) offerings, this page focuses on the "Upastirnabhigharita" (layered and sprinkled) offering and the subsequent "Svishtakrit" (the offering to the Agni who makes the sacrifice well-offered).</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
# Section on the New and Full Moon Sacrifices

->**Commentary**<-

Following the scooping offering <note>original: "upaghata homa"</note>, using the same large sacrificial ladle <note>original: "srucha"</note> and small pouring ladle <note>original: "sruva"</note>, one should take the clarified butter <note>original: "ajya"</note> <unclear>once</unclear>. Over that butter, one should take the sacrificial porridge <note>original: "charu"</note> using the **'stirring spoon'** <note>original: "mekshana"</note>. The specific rule here is: if the practitioner belongs to the Bhrigu lineage <note>original: "Bhrigu gotra"</note>, they should take the porridge from the back half of the center of the pot, performing the scooping five times. If they belong to any other lineage, they should take the porridge from the front half of the center of the pot, scooping only four times. Afterward, wherever the porridge was removed using the stirring spoon, one should sprinkle clarified butter on those spots so that the remaining porridge does not dry out and remains fit for sacrifice. Then, one should pour more **'clarified butter'** over the porridge already taken, and with this porridge (now layered between butter), offer it into the fire with the mantra: "To Agni, Hail!" <note>original: "Agnaye Svaha"</note>. This is known as the "layered and sprinkled offering" <note>original: "upastirnabhigharita homa"</note>. This procedure is performed either once or three times. || 5-10 ||

### Now, having layered the clarified butter for the "well-offered" sacrifice, one scoops from the north-eastern part.
### One should scoop the majority once, then sprinkle it twice with butter. If one follows the five-fold scooping rule, one should layer twice, scoop twice, and sprinkle twice.
### One does not re-anoint the place where the portion was taken, to avoid the loss of ritual potency. One should offer in the north-eastern part with the mantra: "To Agni, the one who makes the sacrifice well-offered, Hail!" || 11-14 ||

In the text above, the word 'Now' <note>original: "atha"</note> indicates a transition to a new section of the ritual, rather than immediate sequence, because the primary sacrifice is described later (in verse 16). The term 'For the well-offered' <note>original: "svishtakrite"</note> refers to the successful completion of the sacrifice. As before, 'having layered' <note>original: "upastirya"</note> means spreading the butter from the small ladle into the larger ladle. Then, from the very center of the porridge pot, specifically from the 'north-eastern part' <note>original: "uttarardhapuvardhat"</note> (the front of the northern half), one 'scoops' <note>original: "avadyati"</note> a 'large amount' <note>original: "bhuyishtham"</note> 'only once' <note>original: "sakrit eva"</note>. Following this, one should 'sprinkle' <note>original: "abhigharayet"</note> that scooped porridge 'twice' <note>original: "dvih"</note> with a stream of butter from the small ladle. 

A specific distinction is made here: 'If' <note>original: "yadi, u"</note> the sacrificer is one who follows the 'five-fold scooping' <note>original: "panchavatti"</note> (typical of the Bhrigu lineage), then he should 'layer' the butter twice, 'scoop' the porridge twice, and 'sprinkle' it twice. Furthermore, in this specific sacrifice, 'to avoid the loss of ritual potency' <note>original: "yatayamata-bhaya"</note>—that is, the fear of the ritual becoming stale or ineffective—one 'does not re-anoint' <note>original: "na pratyanakti"</note> the spot in the pot where the portion was taken. This is because this is the final offering of this particular porridge, and there is no need to keep the remaining spot "active" or moist for further scoops. The text then identifies the mantra and the physical location for this offering: with the mantra 'To Agni, the one who makes the sacrifice well-offered, Hail!' <note>original: "Agnaye Svishtakrite Svaha"</note>, he should 'offer' <note>original: "juhuyat"</note> it into the 'north-eastern part' of the sacred fire. This completes the "well-offered" sacrifice. || 11–14 || 

Now, there is another offering common to all rituals, which is prescribed as follows:

(After the aforementioned "layered and sprinkled offering," the remaining parts of the primary sacrifice...)

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the ritual offerings. The previous page detailed the "layered and sprinkled" offering (upastirnabhigharita). This page transitions into the final stages of the sacrifice: the "Svishtakrit" (the offering that makes the sacrifice complete), the "Mahavyahriti" (Great Utterances), and the "Avapa" (the core ritual insertion).</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Grihya Sutra—</header>

*— <unclear>●</unclear> To perform the **"Svishtakrit"** sacrifice <note>original: "svishtakrit homa"; the sacrifice to Agni in his role as the one who makes the ritual successful</note>, as before, one should take the clarified butter <note>original: "ajya"</note> using the small ladle <note>original: "sruva"</note>. After placing it in the large ladle <note>original: "suc"</note>, one should scoop only once from the sacrificial porridge <note>original: "charu"</note>. Specifically, this scoop is taken from the north-eastern part of the middle of the porridge, but in a slightly larger quantity. Then, one should sprinkle clarified butter over it again. 

The specific distinction is that if the practitioner belongs to the **Bhrigu lineage** <note>original: "Bhrigu gotra"</note>, they must perform the "under-layering" <note>original: "upastarana"</note> twice. Subsequently, one takes the porridge in this manner and sprinkles the butter twice. (Furthermore, since the Svishtakrit portion is the final part—meaning no more porridge is needed for further sacrifices—) after scooping the porridge for the Svishtakrit, it is not necessary to sprinkle more butter on the remaining porridge in the pot to keep it "correct." 

One should offer this gathered sacrificial material into the north-eastern part of the fire using the mantra: **"To Agni, the maker of good sacrifice, Hail!"** <note>original: "Agnaye Svishtakrite Svaha"</note>. This is what is called the "Svishtakrit sacrifice." || 11-14 ||

## One should offer clarified butter with the Great Mystical Utterances. || 15 ||

**"With the Great Mystical Utterances"** <note>original: "Mahavyahritis"</note> refers to the mantras **"Bhuh, Bhuvah, Svah."** Using **"clarified butter,"** one **"should offer."** This is the general method for the sacrifice. || 15 || Now, the text establishes the proper time for the primary sacrifice—

**Explanation:** One should perform the offering with clarified butter using the mantra **"Bhuh Bhuvah Svaha."** This is known as the "Mahavyahriti sacrifice." || 15 ||

## The primary sacrifice occurs before the Svishtakrit sacrifice. || 16 ||

**"Before the Svishtakrit"** means prior to the Svishtakrit sacrifice itself. The **"Avapa"** <note>original: "Avapa"; literally 'the insertion'</note> refers to the primary ritual of the occasion, such as the New and Full Moon sacrifices or the Wedding sacrifice, which must be performed at this stage. || 16 ||

**Explanation:** Before the Svishtakrit sacrifice, one must perform the "Avapa," which is the core sacrifice of the New and Full Moon rituals, or a wedding, etc. || 16 ||

## In groups, the sweeping of the altar, the firewood, the sacred grass, the sprinkling, the clarified butter, and the two butter portions are done once; having scooped from all of them, one performs the Svishtakrit sacrifice only once. 17, 18

**"In groups"** refers to when there are many primary sacrifices <note>original: "avapa"</note> or ritual acts. To ensure that the preparatory and concluding acts do not become excessive due to the number of primary sacrifices, the text says—**"the sweeping of the altar, etc."** 

All these previously mentioned acts should be performed **"once"** (only a single time). However, one should **"scoop from all"** the various offerings. After the individual scooping and offering, one **"performs the Svishtakrit sacrifice"** **"once"** (only one time), rather than repeating it for every single primary sacrifice in the group. || 17, 18 ||

---

* The New and Full Moon rituals, or the primary sacrifice, will be described later (Sutras 23, 24). The primary ritual of all ceremonies like weddings is called the "Avapa." The mantra is the foundation; thus, first the preliminary mystical utterances are completed, and at the end, the Svishtakrit sacrifice must be performed. These four types of offerings are what constitute the completed sacrifice. || 17-18 ||

<summary>This page details the technical procedures for the Svishtakrit (the "well-offered" sacrifice) and the Avapa (the primary ritual). It specifies that even when multiple core sacrifices are performed, the preparatory acts and the final Svishtakrit offering are only performed once.</summary>
<keywords>Svishtakrit, Mahavyahriti, Avapa, Bhrigu lineage, clarified butter, sacrificial porridge, New and Full Moon rituals, Gobhila Grihya Sutra</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the ritual offerings of the New and Full Moon sacrifices. The previous page concluded the instructions for the "Svishtakrit" offering—the final act that makes the sacrifice "well-performed." This page now addresses the disposal of ritual implements and the specific deities to be invoked based on the practitioner's ritual status.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>Part 1, Chapter 8, Sutras 15-25 ] The Ritual of the New and Full Moon Sacrifices ||</header>

**Commentary:** In a ritual context where many **Avapa** <note>original: "avapa"; the core ritual insertions or main offerings of a sacrifice</note> are required, one does not need to repeat preparatory tasks like gathering **Idhma** <note>original: "idhma"; the fuel wood used for the sacred fire</note> multiple times just because there are many offerings. Instead, for all these main offerings, one should first perform the taking of the **Charu** <note>original: "charu-grahana"; the sacrificial porridge</note> and the subsequent libations as previously described. Finally, at the very end of all offerings, a single **Svishtakrit** <note>the final "completion" offering to Agni</note> sacrifice should be performed. || 17, 18 ||

## Having offered this, one should throw the stirrer into the fire, or having washed it, use it to eat. 19, 20

After performing "this" **Svishtakrit** sacrifice, and considering the **Mekshana** <note>the wooden stirring paddle used to manage the porridge</note> to be no longer necessary for the ritual, one should "throw" (*praharet*) it into the fire. "Or" (*va*) alternatively, one may "wash" the stirrer and keep it for the appropriate time. With "this" very stirrer, the **Yajamana** <note>the patron or performer of the sacrifice</note> may "scoop" the food and "eat" it. The underlying sentiment is that if eating with the stirrer is comfortable or practical for the person, they should not throw it away. || 19, 20 ||

**Commentary:** After this "Svishtakrit" offering, if the stirrer is no longer needed, throw it into the fire; or, if it is decided that it is needed for eating, wash it and keep it, using it for the meal at the proper time. || 19, 20 ||

## Some say one should not throw the small ladle into the fire. || 21 ||

"Some" (*eke*) teachers and authorities say that one should not throw the **Sruva** <note>original: "sruva"; the small wooden ladle used for pouring clarified butter</note> into the fire—this view is also considered valid. || 21 ||

Now, the text describes the difference in the mantras used for the main offerings of the New and Full Moon sacrifices, depending on whether the practitioner is an **Ahitagni** <note>one who has formally consecrated the three perpetual Shrauta fires</note> or an **Anahitagni** <note>one who has not consecrated those fires, performing only domestic Grihya rituals</note>:

**Commentary:** Some teachers state that "at the end of the work, the small ladle should also be washed and kept; it is not a fault if it is not thrown into the fire." || 21 ||

## For one who has not consecrated the fires, the pot-cooked offering for both the New and Full Moon is for Agni alone. For one who has consecrated the fires, it is for Agni or Agni-Soma on the Full Moon; and for Indra, Indra-Agni, or Mahendra on the New Moon. Alternatively, for the one with consecrated fires, it may also be for Agni alone on both occasions. || 22-25

For the **Anahitagni** <note>the practitioner without the three Shrauta fires</note>, the **Sthali-paka** <note>original: "sthali-paka"; literally "pot-cooking," the ritual porridge prepared in a single pot</note> for "both" the **Darsha** <note>New Moon</note> and **Purnamasa** <note>Full Moon</note> should be "dedicated to Agni" (*Agneya*). This means that after taking the porridge and performing the preliminary pouring of butter, the main offering for both the New and Full Moon rituals is performed with the mantra: **"To Agni, Hail!"** <note>original: "Agnaye Svaha"</note>. 

However, for the **Ahitagni** <note>the practitioner with consecrated fires</note>, on the **Full Moon** (*Paurnamasya*), the offering should be "dedicated to Agni" (*Agneya*) "or" (*va*) "dedicated to Agni-Soma" (*Agnishomiya*). Furthermore, on the **New Moon** (*Amavasya*), the porridge offering should be dedicated to "Indra," "Indra-Agni," or "Mahendra." 

"Alternatively" (*api va*), even for the **Ahitagni**, it "may be dedicated to Agni alone" for "both" the New and Full Moon rituals. In this specific option, there is no distinction between the practitioner with consecrated fires and the one without...

<vocab>Darshapurnamasa (New and Full Moon rituals), Svishtakrit (completion offering), Mekshana (stirring stick), Sruva (small ladle), Ahitagni (one with consecrated fires), Sthalipaka (pot-cooked offering), Avapa (core offering), Idhma (sacred fuel), Charu-grahana (scooping the porridge)</vocab>

<summary>This page details the disposal of ritual implements like the stirring stick and ladle after the Svishtakrit offering, and specifies which deities (Agni, Indra, etc.) receive the main porridge offering based on the sacrificer's ritual status.</summary>
<keywords>ritual disposal, Svishtakrit, Mekshana stirrer, Sruva ladle, Ahitagni, Anahitagni, New Moon sacrifice, Full Moon sacrifice, Agni, Indra</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page concludes the specific sacrificial mantras for the New and Full Moon rituals and introduces the "Yajnavastu" ceremony, which involves the ritual disposal of the sacred grass used during the ceremony. It marks the end of Section 8 of the first chapter.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary of the Gobhila Grhya Sutra—</header>

The result is that there is no difference [in the ritual effect]. || 22-25 || He also prescribes another action called the **Yajnavastu** <note>original: "yajñavāstu"; literally 'the site or residue of the sacrifice,' a concluding rite to appease the gods and manage ritual remains</note>.

**Commentary:** Next, the "insertion mantras" for the New and Full Moon sacrifices are described. If the **Yajamana** <note>the patron or performer of the sacrifice</note> is an **Agnihotri** <note>one who maintains a perpetual sacred fire</note>, then in both the New Moon and Full Moon sacrifices, he should offer the **Charu** <note>sacrificial porridge</note>, which has been seasoned with ghee, using the mantra: "To Agni, Hail!" <note>original: "agnaye svāhā"</note>. If he is an Agnihotri, he should use the mantra "To Agni, Hail!" or "To Agni and Soma, Hail!" <note>original: "agnīṣomābhyāṃ svāhā"</note> during the Full Moon sacrifice. In the New Moon sacrifice, he should use "To Indra, Hail!" <note>original: "indrāya svāhā"</note> or "To Indra and Agni, Hail!" <note>original: "indrāgnībhyāṃ svāhā"</note>. Alternatively, an Agnihotri may simply offer the oblation in both sacrifices with the mantra "To Agni, Hail!", just as he does in his daily fire offerings. || 22-25 ||

## Having placed the fuel stick and sprinkled water around it, he performs the Yajnavastu. From that very heap of sacred grass, he takes a handful of grass and dips it three times—the tips, the middle, and the roots—into either the clarified butter or the sacrificial food while reciting: "May the birds, licking what is smeared, depart." Then, after sprinkling it with water, he should cast it into the fire with the mantra: "That Rudra who is the lord of animals, the bull who moves among the herds—may he not harm our animals. Let this be offered to you. Hail!" This is what they call the Yajnavastu. || 26-29 ||

"Having added the fuel stick and sprinkled around it" means that after completing the act of adding fuel and the ceremonial sprinkling as described previously, he should "perform" the act called "**Yajnavastu**" during the New and Full Moon sacrifices. How should he do it? The text says: "From that very **Barhis**" <note>original: "barhiṣaḥ"; the sacred grass spread on the altar</note>—meaning from the group of **Kusha** grass already spread out—he should "take" or gather a "handful of grass" consisting of well-formed blades. He should "dip" or smear these "three times" in the "clarified butter" <note>original: "ājye"</note> or in the "sacrificial food" <note>original: "haviṣi"</note> (the cooked porridge), following the sequence of "the tips, the middle, and the roots." He does this while reciting the mantra: "May the birds, licking what is smeared, depart." <note>original: "aktaṃ rihāṇā vyantu vayaḥ"</note>

"Then" <note>original: "atha"</note>, after that, having "sprinkled" or washed those blades "with water," he should "cast" or throw them "into the fire" while reciting the mantra beginning with "He who is the lord of animals" and ending with "Hail." "This" entire action is "called" the "**Yajnavastu**." || 26-29 || 8 ||

Thus ends the explanation of the eighth section of the first chapter in the Gobhila Grhya Sutra. 1.8

**Commentary:** In the New and Full Moon sacrifices, another task must be performed called the **Yajnavastu**. It is to be done after the sequence of adding fuel and sprinkling water. Specifically: take a handful of grass from the altar, dip the tips, middle, and roots into the ghee or porridge while reciting the *Aktarihana* mantra, and sprinkle it three times with water. After cleaning it with water and reciting the mantra "He who is the lord of animals..." <note>original: "yaḥ paśūnāmadhipatiḥ"</note>, release it into the fire. This is known as **Yajnavastu**. || 26-29 || 1.8

The Hindi translation of the eighth section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Grhya Sutra is now complete. || 1.8

| **Yajnavastu** | A concluding ritual act involving the disposal of the sacred grass. |
| **Darshapurnamasa** | The New and Full Moon sacrifices, central to Vedic domestic ritual. |
| **Agnihotri** | A person who performs the daily Agnihotra fire sacrifice. |
| **Samidadhana** | The ritual act of placing fuel sticks into the sacred fire. |
| **Paryukshana** | The ritual sprinkling of water around the sacred fire. |
| **Gobhila Grhya Sutra** | An ancient text detailing domestic rituals for the followers of the Samaveda. |

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the New and Full Moon sacrifices from Section 8 into Section 9. <page-num>51</page-num>
<header>[Chapter 1, Section 8, Sutras 26-29; Section 9, Sutras 1-12] Section on the New and Full Moon Sacrifices</header>

## Now, he should move this leftover sacrificial food to the north, take it out, and give it to the Brahman priest; he should desire to satisfy him, for it is known in the sacrifice that “following the satisfaction of the Brahmin, I am satisfied,” and whatever other food is prepared for him should also be given. || 1–4 ||

**Commentary:** "Now" refers to the time after the offerings of the Great Mystic Syllables <term>Mahavyahriti-homa; offerings made with the sacred sounds Bhuh, Bhuvah, and Svah</term>. "This leftover" refers to the remaining sacrificial food <term>charu; a porridge of grain, usually rice or barley, cooked in milk or water</term>. Having placed it to the north of the fire and transferred it to another vessel, he should present it to the priest known as the **Brahman**. He should desire to satisfy him thoroughly. The sages declare the intention of the **Yajñapurusha** <note>the 'Person of the Sacrifice,' a personification of the ritual itself or Vishnu</note>: "I am satisfied following the satisfaction of the Brahmin." The essence is that the performance of the sacrifice is truly for the sake of satisfying the Brahmin. Furthermore, any "other food" like boiled rice that the patron <term>Yajamāna; the person who institutes and pays for the sacrifice</term> has prepared should also be offered to him. || 1–4 ||

**Hindi Commentary Translation:** The remaining work of the sacrifice is described. First, after the offering of the Great Mystic Syllables, one should place the remaining porridge to the north of the fire. Taking the porridge from that cooking pot into another vessel, the patron should give it to the **Brahma** priest. At that time, if the patron has other food nearby, such as boiled rice, he should give that as well. In whatever way possible, he should wish to satisfy the priest; for the sages say that "we are satisfied according to the satisfaction of the Brahmin"—this is the intention of the Spirit of Sacrifice. || 1–4 ||

---

->### Then he should seek to satisfy the Brahmins with food. || 5 ||<-

**Commentary:** "Then," after the previous step, he should seek to connect with the invited Brahmins through "food"—meaning he should feed them until they are satisfied. || 5 ||

**Hindi Commentary Translation:** Next, the second task: he should satisfy the invited Brahmins and others by feeding them boiled rice and other dishes. || 5 ||

## The sacrificial fee is a full bowl; he should give it to the Brahman priest, whether a cup or a ladle filled with food, either cooked or uncooked, or even fruits—this they call a "full bowl." In domestic sacrifices, the Brahman is the only priest, as the patron acts as his own Hotar; the full bowl is the minimum fee for domestic sacrifices, but the maximum is unlimited—indeed, Sudas, the son of Pijavana, after sacrificing with a pot-offering to Indra and Agni, gave hundreds of thousands. || 6–12 ||

<note>This section details the **Dakshina** (sacrificial fee). A "Full Bowl" (Purnapatra) is a technical measure of grain or food given as a minimum payment. The text emphasizes that while there is a minimum, there is no limit to generosity, citing the legendary King Sudas who gave immense wealth even for a simple domestic rite.</note>

<summary>This page explains the concluding acts of the New and Full Moon sacrifices, focusing on the ritual feeding of the Brahman priest and the invited Brahmins, and defining the "full bowl" as the standard sacrificial fee.</summary>
<keywords>Brahman priest, Charu, Mahavyahriti-homa, Dakshina, Purnapatra, Paka-yajna, King Sudas, ritual satisfaction</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the New and Full Moon sacrifices, specifically detailing the "dakshina" or priestly fee. It concludes the instructions for the standard ritual and begins a new section addressing "expiations" or what to do when ritual timing or requirements are missed.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
->### Commentary on the Gobhila Gṛhyasūtra<-

A **‘full vessel’** <note>original: *pūrṇapātra*</note> constitutes the **‘priestly fee’** <term>dakshiṇā; a gift or payment given to a priest for performing a ritual</term> for the New and Full Moon sacrifices. One should **‘give’** that full vessel, acting as the fee, to the **Brahman**, who is the **‘priest of that name.’** What exactly is this "full vessel"? He explains: it is a **‘bronze vessel’** <note>original: *kāṃsya-pātra*</note> or a **‘drinking cup’** <note>original: *camasa*</note> that one has **‘filled’** with **‘heaps of cooked’** or **‘uncooked food,’** or **‘even with heaps of fruit.’** This is what they call the **‘full vessel.’** In rites like the New and Full Moon sacrifices, **‘the Brahman alone is the one priest’** to be chosen; there is no need for many priests.

How should the role of the invoker be handled? He says: In **‘domestic sacrifices’** <term>pākayajña; literally "cooked sacrifices," referring to simpler household rituals</term> such as the New and Full Moon rites, the **‘sacrificer himself’** <term>yajamāna; the person who institutes and pays for the sacrifice</term> **‘is the invoker’** <note>original: *hotṛ*</note>. Now, it is prescribed that a full vessel is the fee for these domestic sacrifices—does a fault arise if one gives more than that? He replies: No. For the **‘domestic sacrifices’** involving the cooking of sacrificial porridge <term>caru</term>, the **‘full vessel’** is the **‘lowest’** <note>original: *avama*</note> fee, meaning it is the minimum requirement. An **‘unlimited’** amount of gold and other such wealth is the **‘highest’** <note>original: *parārdhya*</note>, meaning it is the most excellent and praiseworthy fee. 

An example of this practice of giving great wealth is shown: Indeed, **‘Sudās’**, the sage and son of Pijavana, **‘having sacrificed with a pot-offering’** <term>sthālīpāka; a dish of rice or barley boiled with milk</term> dedicated to the gods Indra and Agni, **‘gave’** a fee of a **‘hundred’** multiplied by a **‘thousand’**—effectively **‘one hundred thousand’** <note>a "lakh" of coins or cattle</note>. || 6–12 ||

**Commentary:** The priestly fee for this New and Full Moon sacrifice shall be a "full vessel." That full vessel should be given to the priest named the Brahman. Filling a bronze vessel or a drinking cup with raw or cooked grain, or certain fruits, is called a "full vessel." In the New and Full Moon rites, only the Brahman should serve as the priest. In domestic sacrifices—that is, those performed by merely cooking sacrificial porridge—the patron of the sacrifice himself should act as the invoker. 

There is one more thing to understand here: for the domestic sacrifice, the aforementioned full vessel should be considered the minimum (lowest) fee. If one has the means, it is proper to give an unlimited fee. The sage Sudas, a descendant of the sage Pijavana, having performed a sacrifice with a pot-offering dedicated to the deities Indra and Agni—that is, a sacrifice performed after the New Moon—gave a fee of one hundred thousand (gold pieces, coins, or cows). || 6–12 ||

---

### Now, if at the domestic fire, during the evening or morning offerings, 
### or at the New and Full Moon sacrifices, one should fail to 
### obtain the sacrificial substance or the invoker, how should 
### he act? The morning offering must not pass without the 
### evening offering; the evening offering must not pass 
### without the morning offering; the New Moon sacrifice 
### must not pass without the Full Moon sacrifice...

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>[Part 1, Section 9, Sutras 13-17] Final Acts of the Sacrifice</header>

### Within this same grace period, one should seek to obtain either the sacrificial material or the priest; or, having cooked the fruits or leaves of trees suitable for sacrifice, he should offer them; or, as a last resort, he should offer even just water; thus says the sage Reda regarding the domestic sacrifice, for it is indeed considered offered. 13-17. ###

**"Now"** <note>original: *atha*</note> indicates a new topic. **"If"** in the **"domestic fire"** <note>original: *gṛhyāgnau*</note> for the **"evening and morning offerings"** or the **"New and Full Moon sacrifices,"** one **"does not find"** the **"sacrificial material"** <term>havya; materials like clarified butter (ghee) or grain intended for the fire</term> or the **"priest"** <note>original: *hotṛ*</note>—referring to a substitute if one is personally unable to perform it—then **"how"** and in what manner should he **"act"**? This section removes that doubt. The time for the **"morning offering"** **"does not expire"** until the time for the **"evening offering"**; similarly, the time for the **"evening offering"** does not expire until the **"morning offering."** Furthermore, the timeframe for the **"New Moon sacrifice"** does not expire from the start of the **"New Moon"** until the **"Full Moon"**; likewise, the timeframe for the **"Full Moon sacrifice"** does not expire from the start of the **"Full Moon"** until the next **"New Moon."** 

Thus, to find the material or the priest, there is a grace period of four **"watches"** <term>yāma; a traditional unit of time lasting about three hours</term> for the morning and evening offerings, and fifteen days for the New and Full Moon sacrifices. **"Within this"** period of four watches or fifteen days **"exactly,"** one should **"seek to obtain"** the material or the priest. **"Alternatively,"** even if a priest is found, one may **"cook the fruits or leaves of trees suitable for sacrifice and offer them."** And **"even"** if those are not available, **"as a last resort"** <note>original: *antataḥ*</note>, he should **"offer"** nothing but **"water."** The sage named **"Reda"** **"has said"** that **"this"** is the rule for the **"domestic sacrifice"** <term>pākayajña; literally "cooked sacrifice," referring to simpler household rituals</term>. Therefore, even an offering of fruit or water **"must indeed be accepted"** by us as **"offered."** 13-17.

**Commentary:** If, due to some divine accident, the **"materials"** for the evening and morning offerings or the New and Full Moon sacrifices in the domestic fire are not gathered, or if due to illness or other constraints both the husband and wife are unable to perform them and a substitute (another person to act on their behalf) is difficult to find at that moment, then in such a situation: the time for the morning offering will not be considered past until the time for the evening offering, and the time for the evening offering will not be considered past until the time for the morning offering. In such a state, the **"New Moon sacrifice"** can be performed on any day within the 15 days from the New Moon until the day before the Full Moon. And the **"Full Moon sacrifice"** can be performed on any day within the 15 days from the Full Moon until the day before the New Moon. During this time, whatever material is missing should be gathered, and an invoker should be sought from somewhere. If sacrificial food such as grain cannot be gathered, then with those...

<sig>5</sig>

<vocab>Final Acts of the Sacrifice, sacrificial material, invoker/priest, domestic sacrifice, New Moon, Full Moon, evening and morning offerings, substitute</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the "grace periods" for household rituals, allowing a householder up to 12 hours for daily offerings and 15 days for monthly ones if materials or priests are missing. It establishes that in emergencies, fruits, leaves, or even water can be offered instead of the standard grains or ghee.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of emergency procedures for the domestic fire sacrifice. <page-num>54</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Grihya Sutra—</header>

# In the Commentary on the Gobhila Grihya Sutra—

There is no loss; the fire sacrifice can also be performed with fruit. If even this is unavailable, one should complete the sacrifice with grain, crops, or even leaves from trees such as the Mango. The sage named Reda says that if absolutely nothing can be found, one should perform the sacrifice with only water—but he must not break the rule of daily practice. 13–17.

> For one who has not offered the sacrifice, there is penance; therefore, a Brahmin should not be without a vow. 18, 19.

> Furthermore, they cite an example: as long as the sacrifice is not offered, one should maintain the continuity of the vow by fasting. Whenever one obtains the materials, he should then perform the sacrifice; in this way, his vow remains continuous. 20-23.

**"For one who has not offered"** <note>original: *ahutasya*</note> refers to a householder who has not performed the morning or evening offerings in the **domestic fire** <note>original: *gṛhyāgnau*</note>, nor the **New and Full Moon sacrifices** <term>darśapaurṇamāsa; major fortnightly rituals marking the lunar phases</term>. For him, **"penance"** <term>prāyaścitta; an act of atonement or rectification</term> must be performed. For this reason, the command is: a **"Brahmin"** **"should not be"** **"without a vow"** <term>avrata; one who has ceased their mandatory religious duties</term>, meaning he should not be void of the daily offerings. 18, 19.

**"Furthermore"** <note>original: *atha api*</note> the teachers **"cite"** or state another perspective. And so—for **"as long"** a time as the sacrifice **"is not offered"**—whether the morning and evening fire-offerings or the New and Full Moon offerings—for **"that long"** one should **"maintain continuity"** by **"not eating"** <note>original: *abhojanena*</note>; that is, he should pass the time by fasting. **"Then"** afterward, even if the prescribed time has passed, at **"whenever"** or whatever time he **"obtains"** the **sacrificial material** <note>original: *havya*</note> or the **priest** <note>original: *hotṛ*</note>, he should **"offer in return."** He should perform the offering while looking back to the missed time (such as the evening). **"In this way,"** even by passing the day without food, the **"vow"** or daily practice **"becomes"** **"continuous"** or uninterrupted. Thus ends this section on daily practice. 20-23.

**Commentary:** If such a terrible calamity occurs that even water is difficult to find or unavailable, then one should remain hungry until a means for the fire-offering is found. Afterward, the moment he obtains the offering materials or finds a priest, he should provide the morning or evening offerings at that time and perform the New Moon or Full Moon sacrifices. In this manner, he should preserve the regular duty of said acts. However, a Brahmin should not remain without a **"vow"** <note>original: *vrata*</note> under any circumstances; if he becomes void of his vow, he will have to perform penance. 18–23.

> Henceforth, this is the rule for the grain offerings. 24.

**"Henceforth"** <note>original: *ata ūrdhvaṃ*</note> from this point on, **"in the grain offerings"** <term>havirāhuti; offerings made with prepared food rather than just liquids</term> consisting of **sacrificial porridge** <term>caru; rice or grain boiled with milk or water for ritual use</term>, whether they are occasional or for specific desires, **"this"** is the **"rule"** or method. In principle, one should first perform the two preliminary offerings and then proceed with the main sacrifice after the ritual preparation of the materials. 24.

**Commentary:** Following this, in all places where sacrifices are said to be performed via sacrificial porridge, the tasks of setting up the **mortar** <term>ulūkhala</term> and **pestle** <term>musala</term> and the placement of materials should be done in exactly the same manner. 24.

<vocab>Gobhila Grihya Sutra, fire sacrifice (havana), penance (prayaschitta), Brahmin, vow (vrata), New and Full Moon sacrifices (darshapurnamasa), sacrificial porridge (charu), mortar (ulukhala), pestle (musala)</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines emergency protocols for maintaining daily sacrifices when materials are missing, emphasizing that fasting can bridge the gap to maintain a "continuous vow." It also establishes that the procedural rules for preparing grain with a mortar and pestle apply to all subsequent porridge-based offerings.</summary>
