<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>[ Chapter 1, Section 9, Verses 18-28 ] Conclusion of Ritual Duties</header>

## The "Svāhā" utterance at the end of the mantra. || 25 ||

**"At the end of the mantra"** <note>original: *mantrānte*</note>—at the conclusion of the sentences used as tools for offering the oblation, in all fire sacrifices whether performed earlier or later—the **"Svāhā-utterance"** <term>svāhākāra; the ritual act of saying "Svāhā" to consign an offering to the fire</term>, meaning the word "Svāhā," must be employed. || 25 ||

**Commentary:** At the end of every mantra used for an offering, one should add and speak the word **"Svāhā."** || 25 ||

## In offerings of clarified butter, one should refine only the butter and perform the secondary sacrifice; neither the two butter-portions nor the "Final Offering to Agni" are required. In butter offerings, where there is no specific instruction, a sacrifice with the "Great Utterances" is performed both before and after the main rite. As in the Marriage ceremony, so also in the Tonsure ceremony, the Initiation, and the Gift of Cows. || 26-28 ||

**"In offerings of clarified butter"** <term>ājyāhuti; sacrifices where ghee is the primary substance rather than cooked food</term> refers to rituals where the oblation is commanded to be made with butter alone. In such cases, **"refining only the butter"** <note>original: *ājyameva saṃskṛtya*</note> is sufficient; preparing the cooked rice <term>caru</term> is unnecessary. Therefore, without performing the preliminary arrangement of the mortar and pestle, one should perform the **"Upaghata"** <term>upaghāta; a specific auxiliary or "striking" offering that precedes a main rite</term>, which acts as a single introductory sacrifice for the ritual. **"Not the two butter-portions"** <note>original: *na ājyabhāgau*</note> means that the dual sacrifice consisting of two portions of butter (usually taken in four helpings) should not be performed. **"No Svistakrit"** <term>sviṣṭakṛt; the "offering to the maker of right-sacrifice," usually the concluding portion of a ritual</term> means that this final offering is also not required in these instances.

Furthermore, in all **"offerings of clarified butter,"** whenever there is **"no specific instruction"** <note>original: *anādeśe*</note> or special rule—certainly in rites like the **Conception ceremony** <term>garbhādhāna</term> <margin>* That is, in the Conception ceremony etc.</margin>—a single **"sacrifice"** should be performed **"before"** the main ritual and **"after"** it, using the **"Great Utterances"** <term>mahāvyāhṛti; the sacred syllables Bhūḥ, Bhuvaḥ, and Svaḥ</term>, meaning all of them recited together. 

One might ask: since there is no specific instruction for the **Tonsure** ceremony <term>cūḍākaraṇa; the first haircutting</term> and similar rites, should they also only have a single sacrifice with the Great Utterances? This is refuted by this rule of extension: **"As in the Marriage ceremony"** <term>pāṇigrahaṇa</term>—where a specific set of four offerings is prescribed (the first three with the Great Utterances individually, and the fourth with them combined)—**"so"** also should the fourfold sacrifice be performed in the **"Tonsure ceremony, the Initiation, and the Gift of Cows."** This is an extension rule to ensure the proper number of offerings. || 26-28 ||

**Commentary:** For those sacrifices that are performed only with clarified butter, it is proper to perform only the refinement of the butter; the placement of the mortar and other tools is not required. In such instances, unlike in the cooked rice sacrifice, it is not necessary to perform two "Upaghata" sacrifices using butter taken four or five times; perform the "Upaghata" sacrifice only once. The "pouring over" <note>original: *upastīrṇābhighārita*</note> offering is also unnecessary, and one should not perform the "Svistakrit" sacrifice. There is another specific rule for butter offerings: in places where there is no specific instruction, such as the Conception ceremony, one should offer "Bhūḥ, Bhuvaḥ, and Svaḥ"—these three... <note>The text continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page defines the use of the word "Svāhā" in sacrifices and outlines simplified procedures for offerings made with clarified butter (ghee), specifically reducing the number of auxiliary offerings required in certain life-cycle rituals.</summary>
<keywords>Svāhā, Clarified Butter, Ājyāhuti, Upaghāta, Sviṣṭakṛt, Mahāvyāhṛti, Marriage ceremony, Tonsure, Initiation, Conception ceremony</keywords>

# Now Begins the Second Chapter

->❦<-

### || Om || One should take a wife under an auspicious star. || 1

**"Under an auspicious star"** <note>original: *punye nakṣatre*</note> refers to the lunar mansions mentioned in the science of astrology. **"Take a wife"** <note>original: *dārān kurvīta*</note> means one should accept or marry a bride. || 1

**Commentary:** One should perform the marriage ceremony at a time when the Moon's conjunction with the constellations is favorable (such as the star Rohini and others). || 1 ||

### [The bride should be] praised for her physical marks, [examined] by an expert. || 2

The text explains what kind of wife one should seek: **"Praised for her marks"** <note>original: *lakṣaṇapraśastān*</note> means someone endowed with excellent physical characteristics. **"By an expert"** <note>original: *kuśalena*</note> means after being examined by a person who is well-versed in the science of physiognomy <term>lakṣaṇa-śāstra; the ancient study of bodily marks to determine character or destiny</term>. || 2

**Commentary:** A person who understands how to distinguish between favorable and unfavorable physical marks should conduct an examination; one should then marry a girl who possesses these praiseworthy traits. || 2 ||

### In the absence of that, [use] the clay clods. || 3

**"In the absence of that"** <note>original: *tad-alābhe*</note> means if an expert in examining physical marks is not available, or if the influence of her marks cannot be determined through observation. In such cases, one should perform the examination by having her choose **"clods"** <note>original: *piṇḍān*</note> or balls of earth. || 3

**Commentary:** If, at that time, a person capable of identifying the physical marks of a woman cannot be found... <note>The text continues on the next page describing the ritual of the eight clay clods used to determine the bride's suitability through divination.</note>

<vocab>examination of the bride, second chapter, auspicious star, wife/bride, expert/skilled, clay clod, Samaveda, physical marks</vocab>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the second chapter, shifting focus to the requirements for marriage. It emphasizes selecting a bride during an auspicious astrological time and performing a physical examination of her traits, suggesting a ritual alternative using clay clods if an expert examiner is unavailable.</summary>
<keywords>marriage rituals, astrology, Nakshatra, physiognomy, Samaveda chanting, divination, auspicious signs</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page concludes the first chapter (Prapāṭhaka) of the Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra. It finishes the general instructions for domestic fire rituals (Homa) and introduces the specific musical chants from the Samaveda that must conclude every ceremony to ensure peace and ward off misfortune.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>Commentary on the Gobhila Domestic Ritual Sutras ||</header>

Having recited the Great Utterances <note>original: 'Mahāvyāhṛtis' - the sacred syllables Bhū (Earth), Bhuvaḥ (Atmosphere), and Svaḥ (Heaven)</note>, one should offer one oblation each. However, the specific arrangement that will be established for the marriage ritual <insert>\* \*</insert> shall also be applied to the tonsure ceremony <note>Cūḍākaraṇa</note>, the initiation ceremony <note>Upanayana</note>, and the hair-cutting ceremony <note>Godāna</note>. || 26–28 ||

->**At the conclusion of the ritual, the Vāmadevya chant is performed for the sake of peace, for the sake of peace. || 28 <note>The text labels this as 28, though it follows 26-28 above</note> ||**<-

The term 'in the ritual' refers to daily <note>Nitya</note>, occasional <note>Naimittika</note>, or rituals performed for a specific desire <note>Kāmya</note>. In all these cases, 'at the conclusion' <note>Apavṛtte</note> means at the very end of the entire procedure. The 'Vāmadevya chant' <note>Vāmadevyagānam</note> refers to the singing of the Sāma melody of that name (found in the *Uha Gāna* 1, 1, 5). It should be understood that this is performed 'for the sake of peace' <note>Śāntyarthaṃ</note>. The repetition of the word 'peace' signifies the completion of the chapter. || 29 ||

->Thus ends the explanation of the ninth section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Domestic Sutras.<-

# Thus ends the first chapter of the Gobhila Domestic Sutras.

Commentary:—Whether the ritual is a daily obligation, one prompted by a specific occasion, or one performed to fulfill a wish, at the end of every type of fire oblation <note>Homa</note>, one should perform the 'Vāmadevya' <insert>\*</insert> chant. By doing so, all types of calamities and obstacles are pacified. || 29-9 ||

The translation of the ninth section of the first chapter of the Gobhila Domestic Sutras is now complete. || 9, 9 ||

# The first chapter is also completed. || 1 ||

---

->—:०:|| The Great Vāmadevya Chant ||0:—<-

<gloss> 3 2 4 2 42 5 </gloss>
->**The Great Vāmadevya || Ka-a-5-ya | Na-nna-3 Indra-3 abhuuvat.**<-
<note>The numbers and elongated vowels above represent musical notations and pitches for the Samavedic chanting style.</note>

<insert>
\* The specific method for the marriage ritual is this: one should recite the three 'Great Utterances' (Bhū, Bhuvaḥ, Svaḥ) separately three times, and then recite them all together. Thus, four oblations should be made. The act of reciting these utterances separately for oblations is called 'Distributed Oblation' <note>Vyastahoma</note>, and reciting them as a combined group is called 'Unified Oblation' <note>Samastahoma</note>. In marriage and similar ceremonies, both types of oblations—distributed and unified—are performed.
</insert>

<insert>
\* In the second half of the second chapter of the *Ārcika* section of the Samaveda, specifically the fifth verse of the third set of ten beginning with 'With what favor...' <note>original: 'Kayā naścitra ā'</note>, three Sāma verses are traditionally sung. These appear in the first half of the fifth chapter of the *Geya Gāna* as numbers 23, 24, and 25. Among these, according to the *Ārṣeya Brāhmaṇa* (1, 16), the third is known as the 'Vāmadevya.' In the first half of the first chapter of the *Uttarārcika*, the first verse of the twelfth hymn is also 'With what favor...'. According to the *Tāṇḍya Brāhmaṇa* scripture which states, 'That which he sings as the later verse...', these two verses are also used for the 'Vāmadevya' chant. Because these Vāmadevya chants are performed collectively, it is called the 'Great Vāmadevya Chant' <note>Mahāvāmadevyagāna</note>. This Great Vāmadevya is the fifth Sāma melody in the first half of the first chapter of the *Ūha Gāna*.
</insert>

<summary>This page concludes the first chapter of the manual, mandating the Vāmadevya chant as the universal conclusion for all domestic fire rituals to ensure peace. It also provides technical musicological details regarding the source of these chants within the Samaveda tradition.</summary>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general conclusion of the first chapter into the tenth section (Khaṇḍa), which focuses on the specific rituals for selecting a bride and the purification ceremony known as the Ritual for the Kin (Jñāti-karma).</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>[ Book 1, Section 10, Sutras 4-11 ] Ritual for the Kin ||</header>

'O so-and-so! <note>The speaker addresses the prospective bride by her name</note> Pick up whichever of these clods of earth you desire.' After saying this, if she picks up one of the four clods—from the sacrificial altar, a plowed field, a pool, or a cow-pen—then she should be considered to have auspicious qualities, and he may marry her. Some authorities say that if she picks the ninth clod—the one made of eight types of soil mixed together—one may also marry that girl. However, if she picks the clods from a crossroads, a gambling place, a cremation ground, or from barren soil, she is by no means fit for marriage. ->The examination of the maiden is thus completed.<- || 7–9 ||

### With barley or beans that have been purchased, or with water, a friend should pour water over the girl’s body three times—over her head—while she is bathed and rubbed with the powders, reciting: "Desire, I know your name; Intoxication is your name..." 
### In the mantra, one should substitute the word "so-and-so" with the husband's name; then, with the two following verses ending in "Svāhā," he should wash her lap. This is the Ritual for the Kin. || 10, 11 ||

'With purchased' <note>original: 'krītakaiḥ'</note> refers to powders made from 'barley or black grams.' A 'friend' <note>original: 'suhṛt'</note>—specifically a female friend of the girl—should take 'excellent water' and 'three times' 'pour' it over the girl's 'head' including her 'entire body.' There, the pouring of water is accompanied by the verses from the *Mantra Brāhmaṇa* (1.1.2–4): 

> "Desire, I know your name! Your name is Intoxication! Bring this man [so-and-so] to her; the wine was for you. You were created from the highest heat <note>original: 'tapas'</note> in the beginning of birth. Svāhā!" || 2 || "I smear this lap of yours with honey; this is the second mouth of the Lord of Creatures <note>original: 'Prajāpati'</note>. Through it, may you overcome all men and, being independent, rule over them as a queen. Svāhā!" || 3 || "The ancient fire that consumes flesh, hiding in the lap of women, has approached. Through that, they made the clarified butter; may the three-fold power of the Creator <note>original: 'Tvaṣṭṛ'</note> be placed in you. Svāhā!" || 4 ||

In the mantra that says "Bring this man [so-and-so]," one should 'mention the husband's name' <note>original: 'patināma'</note>—the name of the future groom—in place of the word "so-and-so." Furthermore, with the 'following' two of the three recited mantras, one should specifically 'wash' or 'flood' the girl’s 'lap' <note>original: 'upastha'—referring to the generative region</note>. 'This' entire process, from the initial rubbing with powders to the final washing of the lap, is called 'The Ritual for the Kin' <note>original: 'Jñāti-karma'</note>. Thus ends the explanation of the Ritual for the Kin. || 10, 11 ||

<term>Commentary:</term> If a girl’s body is rubbed with powders of barley, black gram, or fruits, and then a friend of the girl pours excellent water over her head three times while reciting the three mantras beginning with "Desire, I know..." and ending in "Svāhā," her body is thoroughly washed. Specifically, after reciting the latter two (the 3rd and 4th mantras), her lap <note>generative organ/region</note> should be thoroughly cleansed. This is what is termed the 'Ritual for the Kin.' || 10, 11 ||

<vocab>Ritual for the Kin (Jñāti-karma), Maiden-Examination (Kanyāparīkṣā), Earth-Clod (Piṇḍa), Soil (Mṛttikā), Desire-Knowledge (Kāmaveda), Sacred Verse (Mantra), Lap/Generative Region (Upastha), Three-Mantra Set (Mantratraya)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes two preparatory marriage rituals: a divination test using clods of earth from different locations to determine a bride's suitability, and a ritual purification bath (Jñāti-karma) where the bride is washed with grain powders and water while specific mantras are recited.</summary>
<keywords>marriage rituals, bride selection, divination, purification bath, Jñāti-karma, Gobhila Gṛhya Sūtra, mantras, Mantra Brāhmaṇa</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page (58) provides the ritual instructions for the "Examination of the Bride," a traditional procedure to determine a prospective wife's character and future through symbolic soil samples. This precedes the bath and purification ritual described on page 59.</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Grihya Sutra—</header>

If, upon observing her physical characteristics, a girl possessing all the auspicious marks is not found, then one should test the girl by having her touch a clod of earth in the following manner: || 3 ||

### From the sacrificial altar, from a furrow in a plowed field, from a pool of deep water, from a cow-pen, from a crossroads, from a gambling place, from a cremation ground, and from barren soil—from all these locations, one should gather earth and make eight lumps of equal size and with secret marks. || 4–6 ||

'From the altar' <note>original: *vedyāḥ*</note> means from the site of a Vedic sacrifice. 'From a furrow' <note>original: *sītāyāḥ*</note> means from a place turned by a plow. 'From a pool' <note>original: *hradāt*</note> refers to a place of deep, unfathomable water. 'From a cow-pen' <note>original: *goṣṭhāt*</note> is where cows are kept. 'From a crossroads' <note>original: *catuṣpathāt*</note> is where four roads meet. 'From a gambling place' <note>original: *ādevanāt*</note>—*devana* refers to a place for playing dice. 'From a cremation ground' <note>original: *ādahanāt*</note> refers to the cemetery. 'From barren soil' <note>original: *īriṇāt*</note> refers to salty or desolate land. One should take earth from these places and make them 'equal' <note>original: *samān*</note> in size and appearance, and furthermore 'with marks' <note>original: *kṛtalakṣaṇān*</note> so that the person conducting the test knows which lump came from where. Thus, eight lumps are prepared. 'From all' <note>original: *sarvebhyaḥ*</note> these types of soil, one should take a little bit of each and combine them to make one single lump; this shall be the 'ninth' <note>original: *navamam*</note> of the clods. || 4–6 ||

**Translation of Commentary:** From the sacrificial altar, from a plowed field, from a deep pool of water, from a cow-pen, from a crossroads, from a gambling place, from a cremation ground, and from barren soil—take a little earth from each and make eight distinct lumps. Then, take a little earth from all these together and form one more lump. In this way, nine lumps should be placed. || 4–6 ||

### Holding them in his hand, he should bring them near the girl and say: "Truth was first, truth no one transcends; on truth this earth is founded, on truth all this is established." Then, addressing her by name, he should say, "Pick one of these." If she picks one of the first four, he should marry her. Some say the ninth as well. || 7–9 ||

Having 'placed' <note>original: *ādhāya*</note> the aforementioned lumps in his 'hand' <note>original: *pāṇau*</note>, he should 'bring them near' <note>original: *upanamayeat*</note> the 'girl' <note>original: *kumāryāḥ*</note> who is to be examined for marriage. The mantra to be recited is "Truth was first..." <note>original: *ṛtam eva...*</note> up to the word "established." Then, 'taking her name' <note>original: *tasyā nāma gṛhītvā*</note>, effectively addressing her, he should say, "Of these nine lumps, 'pick' <note>original: *gṛhāṇa*</note> any 'one'." If it is so said, and she 'picks' <note>original: *gṛhṇantīm*</note> any of the 'first four' <note>original: *pūrveṣāṃ catūrṇāṃ*</note>—those made from the altar, furrow, pool, or cow-pen—then he should 'marry' <note>original: *upayacchet*</note> her. 'Some' <note>original: *eke*</note> teachers say that he may also marry her if she picks the 'combined' <note>original: *sambhāryam*</note> ninth lump. It follows that if she picks the lumps from the crossroads, gambling place, cremation ground, or barren soil, she is considered to have inauspicious signs and should not be married. ->This concludes the examination of the maiden.<- || 7–9 ||

**Translation of Commentary:** Taking the said nine lumps in his hand, he should bring them to the girl intended for marriage and, after reciting the "Truth" mantra, say:

<vocab>Gobhila Grihya Sutra (a manual of domestic rituals), Kanyapariksha (the ritual examination of a prospective bride), Mrittika-pinda (lump of earth/soil), Vedi (sacrificial altar), Smashana (cremation ground), Upayacchet (the act of taking in marriage)</vocab>

<summary>This page details the ritual of Kanyapariksha, where a prospective bride chooses from nine clods of earth gathered from different locations to reveal her character and future suitability. Soil from holy or productive sites like altars and fields is considered auspicious, while soil from cemeteries or gambling dens is not.</summary>
<keywords>bride selection, divination, Vedic rituals, Gobhila Grihya Sutra, auspicious signs, soil symbolism, marriage customs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the marriage preparations from page 58. Having tested the bride's character with clods of earth, the ritual now moves to the purificatory bath and the ceremonial dressing of the bride before she approaches the sacred fire.</meta>

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>[Chapter 2, Section 1, Sutras 12-22] The Ritual of the Kushika-Gandika</header>

**Commentary:** Following that, the groom should have the bride he is taking in marriage bathed up to her head. This constitutes the bride’s ritual bath on the wedding day. || 17 ||

## 18. The husband should clothe her in a new garment while reciting the verse "Those who spun..." and the verse "Put on the garment."

After this bath, the 'husband' <note>referring to the future husband</note> should clothe her in a 'new garment' <term>ahatena vasanena</term><note>An "ahata" garment is one that is brand new, unwashed, and has not been cut from a larger bolt of cloth.</note>. This is done while reciting the verse: "The goddesses who spun, who wove, and who stretched the threads from end to end—may those goddesses wrap you in old age. Live a long life and put on this garment." (Mantra Brahmana 1.1.5). 

He also recites: "Put on this garment; provide her with a life of a hundred years; grant her a long life. May you live for a hundred autumns, full of splendor, and while living, may you distribute riches." (Mantra Brahmana 1.1.6). This concludes the ritual of dressing the bride. || 18 ||

**Commentary:** After the bath, the groom recites the mantras beginning with "Those who spun" and "Put on the garment" and dresses the bride in a whole, uncut cloth. This is known as the "Dressing of the Bride." || 18 ||

## 19–22. While leading her forward—she being covered and wearing the sacred thread—he should mutter, "Soma gave her to the Gandharva..." Behind the fire, he should have her move a rolled-up mat or something similar with her foot, making her recite, "May the path leading to my husband be prepared..." If she does not recite it, he should mutter, "May her path be prepared..." and lead her to the edge of the sacrificial grass near the end of the mat.

Then, the future husband leads the bride forward while she is 'covered' (with the new cloth) and 'wearing the sacred thread' <term>yajnopavitini</term><note>This indicates that in this tradition, the bride was invested with the sacred thread, a practice that highlights the ancient Vedic education of women.</note>. As he brings her near, he should 'mutter' (recite) the following mantra: "Soma gave her to the Gandharva; the Gandharva gave her to Agni; and Agni has given her to me, along with wealth and sons." (Mantra Brahmana 1.1.7).

Furthermore, 'behind the fire,' there is a 'rolled-up mat' <note>original: *katam*</note> or a similar floor covering. As the bride 'moves' it forward with her 'foot,' the groom should 'make her recite': "May the path leading to my husband be prepared; may it be peaceful, auspicious, and free from danger. May I go to my husband’s world." (Mantra Brahmana 1.1.8).

'If she does not recite' the mantra herself, the groom should 'mutter' the following variation 'himself': "May her path leading to her husband be prepared; may it be peaceful, auspicious, and free from danger. May she go to her husband’s world." (Mantra Brahmana 1.1.8 variant). In this manner, while she moves the mat, he should 'lead her' to the 'edge' of the 'sacrificial grass' <note>original: *barhisah*</note> spread on the ground. || 19–22 ||

**Commentary:** Afterward, having covered the girl with cloth and ensuring she wears the sacred thread...

<meta>This page contains Sutras 12 through 17 of the second chapter, preceding the events on page 61. It describes the spatial arrangement of the sacred fire and the specific preparations of ritual implements and the bride herself before the core marriage ceremony begins.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>In the Commentary on the Gobhila Grihya Sutra—</header>

## 12. For the ceremony of taking the hand, the fire is established in the front part of the hall which has been smeared [with cow dung for purification].

In the 'taking of the hand' <note>original: *pāṇigrahaṇe*—the marriage ritual</note> to be performed, in the 'middle of the hall' <note>original: *śālāyāḥ*</note>, in the 'front' or eastern direction <note>original: *purastāt*</note>, the 'fire' <note>original: *agniḥ*</note> should be 'established' or placed <note>original: *upasamāhitaḥ*</note>. || 12 ||

**Interpretation:** During the marriage ceremony, the fire should be established in the eastern part of the fire-hall or the home. || 12 ||

## 13–16. Then, one of the bride’s attendants, having filled a water-jar from a permanent source, covered and silent, walks around the front of the fire and stands to the south facing north. Another stands with a goad. To the west of the fire, they place four handfuls of parched grain mixed with Shami leaves in a winnowing basket, and a grinding stone with its muller.

'Then' <note>after the fire is set</note>, among the 'bride’s attendants' <note>original: *janyānām*—her kinsmen or friends</note>, 'one' person fills a 'vessel' with 'water' from a 'permanent' source <note>original: *dhruvāṇām*—a body of water that does not dry up even in intense heat</note>. Being 'covered' with a garment and 'silent' <note>original: *vāgyataḥ*—restraining speech to maintain sanctity</note>, he keeps the 'fire' in 'front' of him while 'circumambulating' it. He then 'stands' to the 'south' of the fire, facing 'north'. 'Another' man stands in the same manner holding a 'goad' <note>original: *prājanena*—a stick used for driving cattle</note>. Behind or 'to the west of the fire', the kinfolk 'place' a winnowing basket containing 'four handfuls' of 'parched grain' <term>lājā</term> mixed with 'leaves of the Shami tree' <note>original: *śamī-palāśa*—the Prosopis cineraria, considered sacred</note>. They also 'place' the 'muller' <note>original: *dṛśat-putram*—literally "the son of the grinding stone," referring to the smaller stone held in the hand</note> which rests upon the larger grinding slab. || 13–16 ||

**Interpretation:** After this, a kinsman of the bride should fill a jar from a never-failing pool of water, cover it with a cloth, and—remaining focused—walk clockwise around the fire to sit on the south side facing north. Another person should stand nearby holding a staff. To the rear of the fire, they should place four handfuls of parched grain mixed with Shami leaves, along with a grinding stone (muller). || 13–16 ||

## 17. Then she whose hand he is about to take is bathed, including her head.

'Then' <note>following these preparations</note>, the maiden whose 'hand' the groom 'is about to take' must be 'bathed' <note>original: *āplutā*</note> 'including her head' <note>original: *sa-śiraskā*</note>. This refers to the formal ritual bath of the bride according to tradition. || 17 ||

<vocab>Gobhila Grihya Sutra, Panigrahana (taking the hand), Agni (fire), Shala (hall/room), Kalasha (water-pot), Laja (parched grain), Shami (sacred tree), Drishat-putra (muller/grinding stone), Kanya-snana (bride's bath)</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the marriage rites from the Gobhila Grihya Sutra, shifting from the preparation of the fire and implements described on page 60 to the actual seating of the couple, the initial offerings, and the start of the core symbolic rituals like stepping on the stone.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>
<header>[Chapter 2, Section 1, Sutras 23–26; Section 2, Sutras 1–4] The Marriage Rite</header>

**Commentary:** The bride should sit to the right side of the husband, who is prepared for the "taking of the hand" <note>original: *pāṇigrahaṇa*—the central marriage ritual</note>, at the eastern edge of the mat that was moved by his foot. || 23 || Through her right hand, the maiden should touch the right shoulder of the groom. The groom, proceeding with prayers for a blessed marriage, should offer six oblations into the fire while reciting the six mantras beginning with "May Agni come first." <note>original: *agniretu prathamaḥ*</note> || 24 || Afterward, he should perform three separate offerings <note>original: *homa*</note> while reciting the three Great Mystical Utterances: *Bhūḥ* (Earth), *Bhuvaḥ* (Atmosphere), and *Svaḥ* (Heaven). || 25 || Then, reciting these three together as "*Bhūḥ Bhuvaḥ Svaḥ*", he should perform a fourth offering. || 26 ||

The vernacular translation of the first section of the second chapter of the Gobhila Grihya Sutra is now complete. || 2.1 ||

->❧❧❧❧❧❧❧❧❧<-

## 1. After making the offerings, they both stand up.

"Having offered" <note>original: *hutvā*</note> refers to the completion of the offerings ending with the Great Mystical Utterances. "They stand up together" <note>original: *upottiṣṭhataḥ*</note> indicates the couple rising while their hands are placed on each other's shoulders from behind. || 1 ||

**Commentary:** After the offerings to the Great Mystical Utterances, both should perform the "rising together" <note>original: *upotthāna*</note>. That is, at the time of rising, the groom's right hand should be over the bride's back onto her right shoulder, and the bride's left hand should be over the groom's back onto his left shoulder. || 1 ||

## 2. The husband, having walked around behind her back, stands to the south facing north, having taken the bride’s joined palms.

The "husband" <note>original: *patiḥ*</note>, "walking around" <note>original: *parikramya*</note> by way of her "back" <note>original: *anupṛṣṭhaṃ*</note>, goes to the "right side" <note>south</note> of the wife. Having "taken the bride’s joined palms" <note>original: *vadhvañjaliṃ gṛhītvā*</note>, he "stands" while "facing north" <note>original: *udaṅmukhaḥ*</note>. || 2 ||

**Commentary:** The husband moves behind the bride's back to her right side, takes her joined hands in his, and stands facing the north. || 2 ||

## 3. Previously, the mother or a brother, taking parched grain, should make the bride step on the stone with the ball of her right foot.

The "mother or brother" <note>original: *mātā bhrātā vā*</note>, "taking" <note>original: *ādāya*</note> the "parched grain" <note>original: *lājān*—puffed rice</note> near them, should "make the bride step" <note>original: *vadhūm ākrāmayet*</note> with the "ball of her right foot" <note>original: *dakṣiṇena prapadena*</note> onto the "stone" <note>original: *aśmānam*—a grinding stone or slab</note>. || 3 ||

**Commentary:** The mother or brother, holding parched grain, helps the bride perform the "stepping on the stone" <note>original: *aśmākramaṇa* or *śilārohaṇa*</note> with the front part of her right foot. That is, she is made to step onto the stone slab (along with the muller). || 3 ||

## 4. The one taking her hand chants: "Mount this stone..."

At that very moment of stepping, he chants: "Mount this stone; be as firm as a stone. [Tread] the enemies..."

<meta>catchword: Tread the enemies... (original: dviṣ-)</meta>

<summary>This page details the seating of the couple, the groom's fire offerings using the Great Mystical Utterances, and the symbolic "stepping on the stone" ritual where the bride's family helps her mount a grinding stone to represent her future stability.</summary>
<keywords>Pāṇigrahaṇa, Mahāvyāhṛti, Homa, Upotthāna, Aśmākramaṇa, Lājā, Gobhila Grihya Sutra, Marriage Rituals, Stepping on the Stone, Vedic Wedding</keywords>
